A browser plays at most eight channels from one stream, so a 7.1.4 or wider recording (#74) is carried in two or more lossless streams that play together. The owner chose this over stereo pairs or a downmix only. Decision 098's "up to eight channels" no longer holds.

- **Parts of eight, in the file's own order.** The first part, `flac`, carries channels 1 to 8 (the 7.1 bed of a 7.1.4 mix). The next, `flac-2`, carries channels 9 to 16 (the heights), and so on. ffmpeg picks each part's channels out by index, so a layout ffmpeg cannot name is carried whole as well. Each part is an ordinary rendition with its own content key, and its segments fall at the same times as the first part's.
- **Not an alternative.** A following part is left out of the master playlist, so no player (Safari's own included) can choose it in place of the first. The web player loads it from its own playlist when the lossless stream is heard. A second part costs one more key request, well inside the 30 a minute.
- **One recording in the browser.** Each part plays on a hidden element of its own, following the main player's play, pause, seeks, speed and volume. It is put back on time whenever it drifts by more than 20 ms. All the parts feed the same Web Audio graph, where each channel is the recording's own, whichever stream carries it. Solo, mute, the BS.775 downmix and binaural listening therefore work on every channel. Played as recorded on a device with fewer outputs, a wide layout is folded down to stereo in the graph, as the browser would fold one stream, rather than dropping the channels it has no room for.
- **Where only one element plays.** A comparison source, and Safari's own HLS on iOS (which chooses among the master's renditions itself), play no parts. For a wide layout they play the AAC, a stereo fold of every channel, rather than the first part's eight channels alone; the player names it.
- **Not sample-locked.** The parts stay within a frame or two of each other, which is enough for review listening. Measurements (levels, phase, loudness) come from the server's own decode of the whole file, not from the browser, so they are exact.
- **Earlier files.** A file prepared before this change has one stream of eight; the player says its channels cannot be heard apart rather than playing a partial layout.


## 140. An ADM master's objects are heard through the EBU ADM Renderer

An ADM master (BS.2076, as Atmos and other object-based mixes are delivered) holds a bed of channels and objects whose place moves with the music. Until now only the bed was heard (#74). The owner chose the EBU ADM Renderer (`ear`, BSD-3-Clause, the reference renderer of ITU-R BS.2127) over writing one.

- **Python in the media worker, by exception.** Hekate is TypeScript end to end; the renderer is Python with NumPy and SciPy, and nothing comparable exists for Node. It runs as a separate process, as ffmpeg does, given the decrypted master in the job's private workspace and writing its render there; it opens no network connection and reads nothing else. It is the only Python in Hekate.
- **Pinned, built apart, scanned.** `tools/ear` lists the renderer and every package it needs, each pinned by hash; the dependencies install from wheels only. An `ear` stage of the image installs them with pip into `/opt/ear`; the runtime gets Alpine's `python3`, the one the stage installs with, and that directory, never pip or a build tool (decision 244). ear is published as source only, and is built with a current setuptools that stays in the build stage. ear reads its data files with `pkg_resources`, which setuptools no longer ships (the last releases that did have a known vulnerability), so a four-line stand-in over the standard library's `importlib.resources` serves that one call instead. Trivy scans the packages with the rest of the image; Dependabot moves the pins. CI installs the same pins to run the renderer's tests. `ADM_RENDERER_PATH` names the directory; without it, only the bed is heard, as before.
- **Onto the layout nearest the bed.** The master is rendered onto the BS.2051 system matching its bed: 2.0, 5.1, 5.1.2, 5.1.4, 7.1 or 7.1.4. Any other bed, or objects with no bed, are rendered onto 7.1.4, which holds every bed this side of 9.1.6. ffmpeg then puts the render's channels in its own order for that layout, so the render plays, folds down and is heard apart like any recording of that layout (decision 139 carries a 7.1.4 render in two parts).
- **What the streams carry.** The render replaces the master as the source of its streams, and the file records the layout rendered (`metadata.adm.rendered`); the player hears that layout and names it ("7.1.4 render"). Measurements, the waveform, peaks and the quality checks still come from the master itself. Downloads are the master, untouched.
- **A render that fails** is logged and the streams are made from the master's bed as before, so a file the renderer cannot read still plays. A retry records again what its streams carry.
- **Objects told by their pack.** Reading the master's `chna` chunk, an object is now known by its pack's type (`AP_0003…`), not its track format's, which names the sample format; earlier, every channel of an ADM master was counted in its bed.

## 141. Reviewer permissions per person in a project, and the default roles' tools per workspace

The rest of #55: what the prototype's People page and workspace Defaults show.

- **Per person in a project.** A project's People page sets, for one member or guest, what they may do in every review of the project that asks them: comments they see, commenting, deciding, inviting, tools, download, earlier versions and a play limit. It holds no dates and no items, which belong to one review. It is kept as a change over the workspace's defaults (only what departs from them), so a later change to a default still reaches them. Only those who manage reviewers set it; each change is in the access log; it moves with the project to another workspace, matched to the gaining workspace's guests, and goes with a member who does not.
- **Order.** The more particular wins: the workspace's defaults, then what a request sets for everyone (a workflow stage may), then the person's People page grant, then the request's own override for them. A reviewer added to an open review later gets the review's grant with their People page grant on top. A member's grant still cannot reach past their project role (decision 030).
- **Tool access per workspace.** Workspace settings, Defaults, chooses the player tools each of the five default roles starts with in a new project. Projects made from a template keep the template's roles; existing projects keep their own.

## 142. Video in AV1 beside H.264, and the ladder is the instance's

#148. H.264 plays everywhere a licence for it was paid, and nowhere else: open-source browser builds, some Linux desktops and the browser the visual suite runs do not decode it.

- **A royalty-free encoding at every rung.** Each rung of the ladder is encoded twice: H.264 with AAC, as before, and AV1 (SVT-AV1, a fast preset) with Opus. An instance may choose VP9 instead (quicker to encode, larger) or neither (`VIDEO_ROYALTY_FREE`). Both are packaged, encrypted and delivered like every rendition (decision 022); the playlist names each by its codecs and level, so a browser takes only what it plays. The encode costs about twice what H.264 alone did.
- **One family per viewer.** A player picks one encoding before the stream starts and drops the other's renditions, so the picture never changes codec mid-stream. It takes AV1 where H.264 cannot be played, or where the browser says it decodes AV1 smoothly and power-efficiently; otherwise H.264, which every device decodes in hardware. The browser is asked at the video's own frame rate, as a decoder smooth at 30 frames a second may not be at 60. Choosing a rendition by hand offers the chosen family's rungs, named "720p · AV1". Downloads still make their proxy from the H.264 encoding, which is quicker to decode.
- **The ladder is the instance's.** `VIDEO_LADDER` lists the rungs as height and bitrate, highest first (1080p at 6 Mbit/s, 720p at 3, 480p at 1.2 by default). A video gets the highest rungs at or below its own height, up to `VIDEO_RUNGS` (two). The AV1 or VP9 rung gets two thirds of the H.264 rate. A video rendition advertises its measured peak (RFC 8216's peak segment bit rate) wherever a busy scene took it over its planned rate, so a player never picks a picture its connection cannot keep up with.
- **A GPU where there is one.** `VIDEO_HARDWARE` names a VAAPI render node (Intel or AMD); H.264 is then encoded on it. A rendition the GPU cannot encode (no device, a busy one, a picture it refuses) is encoded again in software, and the worker logs why. AV1 stays in software: few GPUs encode it.


## 143. Cuts compared in the player, their comments and findings on the comparison page

#147. A deliverable's cut is compared with the cut before it where it is reviewed, in the player, rather than on a page of its own.

- **Which cut.** The latest earlier cut ready to play, its primary rendition; a cut with none before it offers no comparison. The Frame menu offers "Compare cuts: wipe" and "Compare cuts: side by side" to whoever has the compare tool.
- **Frame-locked.** The other cut plays from its own protected stream, issued to the session like any other (decision 022), muted and never focusable: the player's own controls move both. It follows the player within half a frame while paused and three frames while playing, and is seeked back whenever it drifts further. It plays the encoding the player chose (decision 142), or the H.264 an older cut made before the royalty-free one has, at no more than the size it is shown; where it cannot be loaded it says so in its place.
- **Wipe and side by side.** The wipe lays the earlier cut over the player's own, cut away left of a line the bar's Wipe slider moves (not dragged over the picture, where a click still plays and pauses); side by side puts the earlier cut on the left. Each picture carries its name, so the bar does not repeat it. A visible mark (decision 079) is laid over both.
- **Comments and findings.** Mapping comments across and listing what the checks found fixed and new is the comparison page's work (decision 083), which a deliverable already has; the bar links to it with the two cuts chosen, for members. Its copy speaks of the sound and the moment rather than the music where the project is a video one.

## 144. Captions come from sidecar files, read once into cues

#146. A video's captions are shown over its picture from a sidecar file: SubRip (`.srt`), WebVTT (`.vtt`) or TTML (`.ttml`, `.dfxp`).

- **Added to the video, not uploaded beside it.** Whoever may upload adds a caption file from the video's Details, naming it for the Frame menu ("English", "SDH"); up to twelve per video, each at most 2 MB. The file is stored as it came, encrypted like every other blob (decision 022), and goes with its video. It is never sent back: delivering a sidecar belongs with the deliverable's own download.
- **Read once.** The file is read when it is added: its format by its contents first and its name second, then its cues (start, end and text, markup dropped, line breaks kept). A file that cannot be read is refused with the reason ("This timing cannot be read: …"). TTML's clock times, frames (at its frame rate and multiplier), ticks and offsets are read, with a division's begin added to its paragraphs; styles, regions and positions are not, nor SCC, whose CEA-608 codes are a decoder of their own.
- **Shown, as text.** Whoever may view the video receives its cues with it, as a guest receives comments: words a reviewer reads, not media. The Frame menu offers "Captions" (or "Captions: English" and the rest where there are several), one at a time; the line sits low in the picture itself, sized with it, and is announced to a screen reader as it changes.

## 145. A video's filmstrip is one picture, made with its streams

#16. Under a video's picture, a strip of its frames shows where things happen and is where a reviewer clicks to go there, as in Frame.io and Dropbox Replay.

- **One picture.** The media worker makes it while it packages the streams, from the same copy: twelve frames, each from the middle of its twelfth of the video (so not the black of a fade in), scaled to 90 pixels high and laid side by side in one WebP. Its frames keep the picture's shape, so a vertical cut gives narrow frames. Should it fail, the video still plays, with a plain loop lane in its place; videos packaged before it have none.
- **Delivered like the streams.** It is an encrypted blob on the file (`files.filmstrip_blob_id`), moved with the file between workspaces and counted as in use by storage reconciliation. The playback response names its URL under the playback token, bound to the session, and the viewer's mark is burned into each of its frames where the project marks them, so no frame shown alone goes unmarked. It is logged as page thumbnails are: within the playback session, not on its own.
- **In the player.** It fills the video's loop lane: a click moves the playhead and a drag loops a passage, where loops are allowed; each cell shows its frame cropped to fit, with the playhead across it. The browser fetches it once and shows it from memory. The file's Details list it among the renditions ("Filmstrip, 12 frames").

## 146. A video's picture is measured once, beside its sound

#42. A video's picture is checked by the media worker, as a recording's samples are (decision 013): measured once, in the same analysis job, and read into findings that do not depend on the project.

- **One decode.** ffmpeg reads every frame once: field order on the picture as it was made, then the bounds of everything that is not black over the whole video, then black, freezes and levels on a copy no wider than 640 pixels. Its per-frame measurements stream to the worker line by line and are never kept. A silent video is analysed for its picture alone, and a checks stage now waits for it; one probed before this has its picture measured at the next start, as do videos with sound.
- **Black and frozen.** A second or more of black is a note at either end, where films start and finish, and worth a look inside; a picture black throughout fails, however short. Two seconds or more of a picture that does not move is worth a look, unless it is black (already said) or covers nine tenths of the video, which is a still image (a song set to its cover) rather than a fault.
- **Missing frames.** Where the frame rate is known, a gap between frames of one and a half frames or more says how many are missing there. A variable frame rate (a screen recording, a phone) holds a frame as long as it likes, so where more than one frame in twenty is timed off the rate's grid the check says so as a note and counts nothing.
- **Interlacing.** ffmpeg's detector counts frames that look interlaced. A picture truly interlaced keeps one field order throughout, while a progressive one it misreads splits between the two, so only a field order four times as common as the other counts: across four fifths of the frames it is a note ("Interlaced, top field first"), across a tenth or more it is worth a look, as a picture interlaced in places is usually an edit's mistake. Whether interlacing is acceptable is the delivery profile's to say.
- **Broadcast-safe levels.** EBU R103 allows luma from −1% to 103%. Values inside that are moved into the nominal range before ffmpeg counts the pixels outside 16 to 235 (scaled to the bit depth), so the undershoot compression leaves at every black edge is not counted; more than 1% of a frame outside, for a fifth of a second or more, is worth a look. Full-range and RGB pictures, with no headroom to be outside of, are not judged; a picture is full range where its stream says so, else where its pixel format does (`yuvj`). Chroma and RGB gamut are left to the delivery checks.
- **Letterboxing.** Bars the picture never reaches into, over the whole video, more than 1% of the frame on both sides, are a note naming the picture's own shape ("Letterboxed: the picture is 1920×804 inside 1920×1080 (2.39:1)").
- **Flashing.** A first look for screen-wide flashing, not a certified test: the mean luma of each frame is taken as screen luminance on ITU-R BT.1702's 200 cd/m² display, a change of 20 cd/m² or more with its darker side under 160 is a transition, and more than six transitions in a second (Ofcom's three flashes) is worth a look. Flashing in part of the picture, and red flashes, need a dedicated analyser such as Harding.
- **In the product.** The checks sit under Video in a project's QA rules, always on, their severity the project's to change. Their problems are marked along the video's timeline, under its filmstrip, and listed in the QA view and report, whose format line now leads with the picture.

## 147. A video's format and timecode are checked against its delivery

#42. A delivery profile can say what it asks of a video's picture, beside what it asks of the sound, and the picture's timecode is checked for breaks.

- **What a profile asks.** A codec (ProRes, DNxHD or DNxHR, H.264, HEVC, MPEG-2, JPEG 2000, AV1), a size, a frame rate, a scan (progressive or interlaced), colour primaries (Rec. 709 or Rec. 2020) and a range (limited or full); each may be left as any, and a profile that leaves them all asks nothing of a picture. Broadcast (EBU R128) asks for 1920 × 1080 at 25 fps, Rec. 709, limited range; Streaming for a progressive, limited-range picture; the audio-only profiles ask nothing. A workspace's own profile chooses them in its editor, stored with its other numbers, so nothing needed a migration.
- **Picture format.** A delivery check, read against the stored probe whenever it is shown, like the audio's format check: one finding, a pass saying what the picture is ("ProRes HQ, 1920 × 1080, 25 fps, progressive, Rec. 709, limited range"), or a failure naming each difference. A size asked for asks for square pixels too. Frame rates agree within five thousandths of a frame a second, so 23.976 is 24000/1001 while 23.976 and 24, 29.97 and 30, and 59.94 and 60 stay apart. What the file does not say (an older file's probe kept no scan, range or primaries) is not held against it. Aspect follows from the size; audio layout is the layout check's (decision 096).
- **Timecode.** Measured once, in the picture checks' decode: `showinfo` logs each frame's carried timecode (an MPEG-2 group's, or an SMPTE 12-1 timecode from H.264 or HEVC picture timing), and each should be the first plus the frames since, through midnight and dropped frames. One that is not is a break, failed at the frame where it happens ("Timecode jumps from 10:00:01:24 to 11:00:00:00"). ffmpeg's MPEG-2 decoder gives a group's timecode to the frame before it, so a group's timecode a frame early is taken as on time. The file's own records are compared too: start timecodes that differ between the picture, a timecode track and the container, a carried timecode that starts elsewhere than the file says, and drop-frame timecode at a rate that drops none are each worth a look. A file with no timecode is a note; one with only a written start passes, as its count runs on with the picture.
- **Measured again.** Videos whose findings have no timecode check are analysed again at the next start, adding only the checks they lack.

## 148. A video's QA reads like its delivery sheet

#42. A video's QA view follows the prototype's (`qa-video.tsx`). The picture shrinks to 300 pixels beside its filmstrip and transport (the same element, so switching views never stops it, and comparing cuts is left to the review view). Above the checks, four tiles give loudness, true peak, the picture and the length, each with what the profile asks or where the timecode starts. The measured checks are listed as for a recording. The delivery checks sit apart in a delivery checklist, each once at its worst, so nothing is said twice. On the right are the streams as a delivery sheet gives them, the frames worth a look (taken from the filmstrip, a click away from the moment), and the embedded metadata. The prototype's checklist also names what cannot yet be measured (head and tail, slates, captions, a checksum); those wait for their checks.

## 149. The take log writes ordinary takes, numbered as the session says

#51, first part. The take log does not keep a list of its own: each take it logs is a row in `takes`, of its track and in its session, so the take manager, coverage and import (#21, #45) see it at once.

- **Lifecycle.** A take is idle, armed, recording, recorded or aborted. It may go from idle or armed to recording, from recording to recorded or aborted, back from armed to idle, and an aborted take may be counted after all; a recorded take stays recorded, never rolled again or aborted after the event. Takes made from files, and those already kept, are recorded. A move is checked against the state it was read in, so two people stopping one take at once cannot overwrite each other: the second is told the take changed.
- **Wall clock.** Rolling and stopping are timed by the server's clock unless the log gives the times itself (as one written offline will). The recorder's timecode in and out are kept as written.
- **Numbering, per session.** *Continuous* runs on from the project's last take, whichever track it was of, so every take of a recording has its own number, as a classical session counts them; *per track* runs on from the track's last take; *recorder* takes the recorder's number as given, refused when that number is already logged for the track. Numbers stay unique per track: continuous numbers are above every number in the project, and every way a take gets a number (logged live, made from a file, imported) waits on one advisory lock per project, so two takes numbered at once on different tracks never share one.
- **Session.** A session says how it numbers and the slot it is booked for, which the take log counts down to. A take logged in a session for a track it did not name adds the track to it.
- **More of a take.** False starts, the page a take ends on (beside the page it starts on and its bars; never without the first, nor before it), and both timecodes.
- **Export.** The log as CSV is built in the browser from the session the page already holds, like the task list's; notes and names are never read as formulas.

Several people at once with their own views, local-first writes, the session clock and the tone and metronome follow in the next parts.

## 150. The take log is a session's page, kept by keys, read from the clock

#51, second part. Each session has a take log at `/sessions/:id/log`, opened from the session's page: the prototype's take log is the log of one session too.

- **Keys.** Space starts the next take, or stops the one rolling (an armed take rolls); A arms the next; X aborts the take rolling or armed; F counts a false start, 1 to 5 rate, S stars and N writes a note, each on the take rolling or, between takes, the last one logged. The keys are registered with the page, so "?" lists them with the rest.
- **The next number.** The session says what the next take of each of its tracks would be numbered, so the button reads "Start take 5" before anything is logged; the server still numbers the take when it starts. A session the recorder numbers asks for the recorder's take number beside the track, and starts nothing without one.
- **One live take.** A session has at most one take armed or rolling. Two people starting at once, the server refuses the second under the numbering lock and says which take is rolling; the log shows why.
- **Clocks.** The take's time, the slot's countdown and the wall clock are worked out from the clock whenever they are shown, never counted up, so a laptop that sleeps through a take shows its true length when it wakes. Under ten minutes left, the countdown turns to the signal colour.
- **A view per person.** Engineer, producer and logger see the same log: the engineer each take's timecode in and out and its length, the producer its pages and bars, the logger a table of everything. The log is read again every two seconds, so a take one person starts the others see; the producer's score and notes, the engineer's patch, and pushing changes to everyone at once follow in the next part.
- **Responsive.** A rating, a star or a false start shows at once and is put right by the server's answer. The note field takes up someone else's change to the note unless it is being typed in, so it never writes an old note back over theirs. While the server cannot be reached the log says so plainly and logs nothing; writing offline and syncing later is the part after.

## 151. Beside the take log, what the room keeps

#51, third part. The take log has a column beside it, as the prototype's has.

- **For everyone, the session's notes.** One text for the day ("piano a touch bright after lunch"), kept on the session.
- **For the producer, the score, a to-do list and a quick reference.** The score shows the page the take the keys act on starts on, of the score its track follows, titled by the bar it starts at; it comes through the score's own page links, so its renders are logged as any other. The quick reference is a second text on the session. The to-do list is a table of its own, an item a row, so two people ticking different items never overwrite each other.
- **Kept in step.** The notes and quick reference save when their field is left, and take up someone else's change whenever they are not being typed in, as the take's note does (decision 150).
- **Who may.** Everyone who sees the session's takes reads them; those who may change takes write them.

The engineer's patch list waits for patch lists (#54); tone, metronome and the recorder's numbers and timecode for the session tools.

## 152. The take log's tuning tone and metronome

#51, fourth part. Beside the take log, in every view, a tuning tone and a metronome, as the prototype has.

- **On this device only.** Each is played by the page in the browser (Web Audio) for whoever has it open; nothing is stored on the session or sent to the room, as a tuning fork and a click are personal tools.
- **The tone.** A sine at 415, 432, 440 or 442 Hz, the pitches rooms tune to; pressed, it sounds until pressed again or another is chosen, fading in and out so it never clicks.
- **The metronome.** A short click on every beat, from 30 to 300 a minute. The beats are timed on the audio clock and scheduled a moment ahead, so a busy page does not make the click drift. The last tempo is remembered on the device.
- **The output.** Where the browser lets a page play to a chosen output (Chrome and Edge do), a list chooses where the tone and click play, such as headphones while the room monitors stay on the desk. Such a browser names its outputs only to a page it trusts with a microphone, so the list appears once it has; where the browser can ask the person for an output itself, "Choose an output" does. The log never asks for the microphone just to list speakers. Elsewhere the tone and click play through the default output.
- **Steady under load.** Beats the scheduler missed (a busy page, a tab in the background) are skipped rather than played late, so the click never bursts and stays on the beat.

The recorder's numbers and timecode wait for the session tools; local-first writes and the large-print overview follow.

## 153. The take log in large print

#51, fifth part. The take log has a large-print view for the far side of the room: the musicians on the floor, a producer across the desk. The prototype draws it too.

- **What it shows.** The take very large: the one rolling or armed, else the next. Under it, its state and, while it rolls, its running time. Above, the track and the session, and the wall clock. Below, the last take logged (rating, star, note) and the session clock.
- **The session clock by colour.** Plenty of the slot left reads plainly; under ten minutes, in the signal colour; past the end, still in the signal colour, it says by how much the slot is over, rather than standing at 0:00. The take log's own countdown does the same.
- **Keys still work.** The view covers the page without taking its keys, so Space starts and stops takes, and the rating keys rate, while it shows. L opens and closes it, as does the button in the take log's header.
- **Full screen.** Where the browser allows, it fills the screen; leaving full screen (Escape) closes it.

## 154. The take log, ready to be written offline

#51, sixth part: the server half of writing the take log offline and syncing it later. The browser half (keeping writes while the connection is down and sending them when it returns) follows.

- **A take made once.** The take log may give a take its own id (a UUIDv7, as every id is). A start sent again, because the log could not tell whether the first arrived, returns the take it made rather than a second one. An id another session or workspace already holds is refused.
- **At its own time.** A start, or a change, may say when it happened by the log's clock, so a take logged offline rolled when the room rolled it, not when the connection came back. A time still to come is taken as now.
- **Conflicts per field, with history.** A change sets only the fields it names, so two people changing different fields both stand. On the same field the later write stands. Every field a change sets is kept in the take's history (`take_changes`): what it replaced, what it became, who wrote it and when, by their clock and by the server's. A change that carries what its writer last saw of a field, and finds something else there, is marked as having overwritten someone else's change, so the history shows the conflict and the overwritten value can be put back.
- **Who may.** Those who may change takes write them; those who may see takes read their history.

## 155. The take log, written offline

#51, seventh part: the browser half of decision 154. The take log is written here first and sent when it can be.

- **Every write through the outbox.** Starting a take, stopping it, a rating, a star, a false start, a note: each is kept in this browser (IndexedDB, so it survives a reload or a laptop closed mid-session) and shown at once, then sent in the order it was made. The take log names the takes it starts (UUIDv7) and says when each write happened, so a take logged offline is made once, at its own time, whatever the connection did.
- **While offline.** The log carries on: the room keeps logging, and the log says "Offline: written here, synced when back" with how many writes wait. When the connection returns (the browser says so, or a retry every few seconds finds it), they are sent. Signed out, they wait for signing in again.
- **Numbers.** A take started offline shows the number it would get; the server numbers it when it arrives, as it numbers every take.
- **Refused.** A write the server refuses for what it is (another take already rolling, a stop someone else made first) is let go, and the log says why. A change that replaces someone else's change to the same field stands, and the take's history keeps both (decision 154).
- **Storage that fails.** Where the browser will not keep the outbox (storage turned off, full or broken), it is kept in memory instead: still shown and sent, but the log says to keep the page open until it is.
- **What it does not do yet.** The session's notes and to-do list are not written offline; the take log must have been open when the connection went, as the session itself is not kept in the browser.

## 156. Imported files find the takes the log made

#51, eighth part: "logged takes are the take manager's takes; import (#45) matches files to them".

- **By the number it was recorded as.** The import sends each take's number as the files give it (iXML, or the file name's pattern). Where that is a whole number, and the session it goes to holds a take of the same track logged under that number that has no file yet, the file becomes that take's: its rating, notes, times and history stay, and no second take of that number is made.
- **Otherwise as before.** A number that names no such take, one whose take has its file already, or one that is not a whole number ("12A") makes a new take, numbered next on its track, as decision 115 has it.
- **Told.** The import answers how many files it matched to logged takes, beside how many it made and skipped.

## 157. Score marks belong to the track

#52, first part: the score marks and the edit plot check, on the server. The score marking page follows.

- **On the track, not the file.** A score mark (docs/domain.md) is kept on its track and placed by page, by a point on the page (0 to 1 across and down, a region's corner and size for an issue) and by bar where known. Swapping the score for a new edition keeps every mark where it was.
- **The kinds.** An issue (in a category, with the takes it occurred in, the take that fixed it, and open or resolved), an edit point, a take start or end, a punch-in, a wrong-note cross, a circled dynamic, a sticky note, and freehand ink. Each needs what it says: an edit point the take it goes into, a dynamic or note its words, ink its stroke.
- **Edit points and the plot.** An edit point hands over from one take into another (written "3T12 into T7": bar 3 of take 12 into take 7); without a take to hand over from it is the lead-in ("T3"); an overdub lays a take over the chain without cutting to it. The edit plot check reads the track's edit points in reading order and says where the chain breaks: a hand-over from a take the plot is not in, a second lead-in, no lead-in, or a take never recorded for the track.
- **Categories.** Each project has its categories of issue, each with a colour and a key from 1 to 9; it starts with Intonation, Ensemble, Noise and Balance. Those who use the production tools add, rename, recolour, re-key and remove them; no two share a name or a key, and an issue whose category is removed keeps everything else.
- **Kept whole.** A change to a mark keeps what its kind needs: an edit point or a take start never loses its take, a dynamic or a note its words. The plot counts only takes that were recorded: one aborted, or still rolling, is none to edit from.
- **Who may.** Those who see the production tools see the marks; those who use them make, change and rub them out.

## 158. Score marking, in Production

#52, second part: the score marking page, as the prototype's Production › Score marking.

- **Production's sections.** Production now has its sections under its title, as the prototype does: Sessions and Score marking so far, the rest as they are built.
- **A track at a time.** The page marks one track's score; a project with several scored tracks chooses the track above the score. A track without a score is not offered, and a project with none says how to give a track its score.
- **The pencil on single keys.** I, E, [, ], P, W, D, N and K choose the kind; 1 to 9 choose an issue's category. A click on the score places the mark there; ink is drawn by dragging. A kind that needs to know something first (an edit point's takes, a take start's take, a dynamic's or a note's words) asks in a short dialog; an issue and a wrong note are placed at once and filled in from the inspector.
- **Reading the score.** Single, two-up and continuous layouts; paper, invert, sepia, amber, cyan and grey displays for a dark room; the arrow keys turn the page, and full stop steps through the edit points in reading order.
- **Beside it.** The inspector for the chosen mark (its bars, words, category, the takes it occurred in, the take that fixed it, resolved; an edit point's takes), the edit plot check with the chain it follows, and the marks on the pages shown.
- **Exports.** The marks as CSV now; the annotated score PDF, going to a bar by the score's printed numbers, and swapping the score follow.

## 159. Score marks answer "other options for this bar?"

#52, third part: "marks feed take coverage (#46)".

- **Which take fixed it.** An issue marked on the score covers its bars, or the bar it was marked at. Asked for the takes of a bar (the thread's Other takes, or the track's takes filtered by bar), the take an issue there was fixed by comes first and says what it fixed ("Fixed Left hand rushes"). It is offered even when its own bars were never set, since the producer's mark says it holds the bar.
- **Where it went wrong.** The takes an issue there occurred in say so, in the signal colour ("Left hand rushes here"), so nobody picks the one the producer marked down.
- **Otherwise as before.** Starred takes, then by rating, then in order. A role that does not see the production tools sees the takes as before, without what the marks say.

## 160. The annotated score is a download

#52, fourth part: "exports: annotated score PDF".

- **Through the download path.** The annotated score is the track's score as a document download, proxy or original, with the track's score marks drawn over its pages. It needs the download permission as any other, is watermarked for whoever asks, and is in the access log with the track it was drawn for (`scoreMarksOf`). Asking for it also needs the production tools (`view_production`), checked as the one-time link is made and again as it is used, and the file must be the track's score.
- **Marks as vectors.** The marks are drawn in the PDF itself, over each page's picture, in vectors and the standard Helvetica: they print sharp at any size, and a PDF reader can find their words. The page beneath is unchanged, mark and all. Each kind looks as the score marking page shows it; on paper, the screen's blue and green print darker, and its paper colour prints as graphite.
- **Every score at once follows.** One track's score at a time for now, from the score marking page's Export menu, as the prototype has it.

## 161. Going to a bar by the numbers the score prints

#52, fifth part: "go to bar via the score's text layer".

- **Read once, as the score is drawn.** When a PDF's pages are drawn, its text is read for the whole numbers in it and where they sit (`pdftotext -bbox`). The bar numbers among them are the longest run that rises through the score in reading order, the earliest such where there are several, after leaving out large type (titles, headings) and the top and foot margins (page numbers). Fingerings, tuplets and rehearsal figures rise and fall, so they drop out; and since a run of fingerings can rise too, the number at the start of each system (the first on its line, near the page's left edge) is preferred, with every number read only for a score that numbers its bars along the systems instead. Fewer than three is not trusted. The file keeps them (`files.printed_bars`), and the score's view returns them with its pages: numbers and places only, nothing else of the text. A score drawn before this has none until it is drawn again.
- **Go to bar.** Beside Go to page, as the prototype has it. The score turns to the page of the last number at or before the bar, and marks that system for a moment with a hairline in the signal colour. It says where it went, so a screen reader hears it too ("Bar 21, page 2"), and between two numbers which system the bar is in ("Bar 23 is in the system from bar 21"). A scanned score, with no text, goes by the bars its marks name, and says when it has nothing to go by.
- **Not a bar map.** This finds a bar on the score; it does not tie the score to a recording (decision 034 stands).

## 162. Swapping a score keeps every mark on its bar

#52, sixth part: "swap a score file mid-project and keep every mark and link".

- **Marks were never the file's.** Score marks and takes belong to the track (decision 158), so swapping its score (the track's Use … menu) never lost one. What a swap changed was where they sat: a new edition puts the bars elsewhere.
- **They follow their bars.** When a track's score is swapped for another, a mark that names a bar moves to that bar in the new score, by the bar numbers both print (decision 161), and keeps its place above or below its system's number; the whole mark moves by one shift, kept on the page, so a region keeps its height and ink its shape. A take's score pages move the same way, each end by its own bar (the first page by its first bar, the last by its last), never leaving the last before the first; the take's history records each as a change by whoever swapped the score. This happens in the transaction that swaps the score, under the track's lock, and needs only what swapping it needs (organise).
- **A score still being drawn.** A score chosen straight after upload has no bars read yet. The track then remembers which edition its marks sit on, and the marks follow once the new score is drawn; a swap meanwhile starts from that same edition, and a score that cannot be drawn leaves them where they are.
- **What cannot follow, stays.** A mark that names no bar, or a swap to or from a score without printed bars (a scan), leaves things where they were. The track page says what moved and what stayed ("Moved to their bars in the new score: 12 marks and 2 takes. 3 marks stayed where they were, with no bar to find them by.").
- **Links.** Comments on the old score stay on it, as comments belong to their file; bar comments on recordings name bars, not a score, and need nothing.

## 163. Every score at once, as one download

#52, last part: "exports: annotated score PDF (per track or every score)".

- **One PDF, through the download path.** Every track's score in a project with its marks over it, in the tracks' order, as one PDF: proxy (the pages as reviewed) or original (drawn again at print resolution). From the score marking page's Export menu ("Every score, annotated"), once a project has more than one track with a score.
- **Checked for each score.** Asking needs the production tools in the project and, for every score, the download permission for that kind and a score ready to give it; the first that fails refuses the whole download and says which. The link works once, in the asking session, for five minutes, and is checked again as it is used. Each score is written to the access log (`scoreMarksOf`, `everyScore`) before anything leaves, and each page is marked for whoever asked.
- **A ticket names a file or a project.** A download link now names either one file or a project's scores, never both (a check constraint); moving a project to another workspace drops its links as it drops its files' links.

## 164. The take log's notes and to-do list, written offline too

#51: what decision 155 left out. Beside the take log, the session's notes, the producer's quick reference and the room's to-do list are written through the same outbox as the takes: kept in this browser, shown at once, and sent in order when the server can be reached.

- **Notes.** The last written stands, as before; a note typed offline is sent when the connection returns.
- **To-dos made once.** A new item is named here (a UUIDv7), so one sent again, because the log could not tell whether the first arrived, is the item it made rather than a second. An id another session or workspace holds is refused.
- **Already gone.** Removing an item someone else removed meanwhile is done, not refused. Ticking one someone removed is refused, and the log says so.

## 165. The patch list

#54, first part. The patch list is in Production beside sessions and score marking, as the prototype has it: the project's stage boxes, each with an input table and an output table (channel, source, device, cable or channel, notes).

- **Stage boxes.** Named, in the order they were added; no two in a project share a name. Removing one removes its channels.
- **Channels.** Each input or output has a channel number on its box, unique for its direction; one added without a number takes the next after the highest. What it carries (source, device, cable, notes) may be left blank until it is known.
- **Who may.** Those who see the production tools read the patch list; those who use them change it.
- **In the take log.** The engineer's view shows each box's inputs, channel and source, under the log.
- **Follows.** Exports as PDF and XLSX; linking a channel to a microphone on the stage plan, once stage plans exist (#53).

## 166. The patch list as a PDF and a workbook

#54, second part: "engineer-style patch list exports as PDF and XLSX".

- **From the Export menu** on the patch list, as the prototype has it, for those who see the production tools; made on the server each time, sent `no-store`, and named after the project ("Joplin Rags patch list.pdf").
- **The PDF** is A4 portrait in Helvetica, as an engineer prints it: the project and "Patch list" at the top of every page, each stage box's inputs and then its outputs as a table (channel, source, device, cable, notes), words cut with an ellipsis where a column ends, a long box carried over pages with its header repeated, and the page numbers at the foot.
- **The workbook** has a sheet for each stage box (named as the box is, within what a workbook allows): a row for each channel, inputs then outputs, with the header row in bold and frozen and channel numbers as numbers. It is written here, as a zip of a few XML parts, rather than by a new dependency.

## 167. Stage plans

#53, first part. Stage plans are in Production between score marking and the patch list, as the prototype has them: a venue's stage drawn to scale in metres on a snapping grid, and what stands on it.

- **Plans.** A project may hold several, one a venue or layout, each named, with its width, depth and grid. No two in a project share a name. A venue's own record (#48) can be linked once venues exist.
- **Items.** Performers, ensemble blocks, microphones, pianos, timpani, instruments, risers, stage boxes, cable runs, walls, labels and plain shapes. The set is open: a kind the toolbar does not offer is drawn as a box. Positions run from the stage's back-left corner; round kinds sit on their centre, a cable runs from its point by its width and depth.
- **Steps and undo.** Every edit is one step (what it adds, changes and removes), sent and stored all at once, so moving or aligning a selection is one undo. Undo and redo are the editor's own: it sends the inverse step. Items are named by the client (UUIDv7), so an undone removal puts the same item back, and a step sent twice adds once.
- **Editing.** Drag to move (the pressed item lands on the grid, the rest keep their places to it), drag on the stage for a marquee, Shift to add to a selection, arrow keys to nudge, align to edges or centres, centre on the stage, duplicate, remove. A list of what is on the stage, as Figma lists layers, reaches every item by keyboard however close they stand.
- **Touch.** A finger on an item drags it; a finger on the stage around it pans the plan, as a map pans. The marquee is for a mouse or a pen; on a phone, several are picked from the list.
- **Sizes.** Everything but a cable run has a width and a depth above nothing, held by the database too; a step that would break that is refused whole, and the editor goes back to what the server holds.
- **Patch.** A microphone, stage box or cable run may be linked to one of the project's patch channels. Its label is then the channel's source, so renaming it in the patch list renames it on the plan; unpatching the channel leaves the item unlinked.
- **Label groups.** Items given a group show or hide their labels together.
- **Who may.** Those who see the production tools see the plans; those who use them change them.
- **Follows.** The vector PDF export (A3 or A4 landscape, rulers and scale bar, decision 168), the floor-plan underlay (decision 169), and ensemble blocks sized from personnel (#49).

## 168. The stage plan as a PDF

#53, second part: "export vector PDF, A3 or A4 landscape, with rulers and a scale bar".

- **From Export PDF** on the stage plan, as the prototype has it, for those who see the production tools; made on the server each time, sent `no-store`, and named after the project and the plan ("Joplin Rags Endless Hall stage plan.pdf").
- **To a standard scale.** The stage is drawn at the largest of 1:20, 1:25, 1:50, 1:75, 1:100, 1:200, 1:250 and 1:500 that fits the paper, so it can be measured with a scale rule; the page says which ("1:50 on A3"). A stage too big for 1:500 is drawn to fit and says it is not to a standard scale.
- **On the page.** The project and plan at the top; the grid, the stage's edge and metre rulers along the back and the side; each item drawn as the plan draws it, in vectors, greys and Helvetica, turned as it is turned; microphones with their patch channel under their label; the audience at the foot; a scale bar.
- **As shown.** Label groups hidden on the plan are left off the print too.

## 169. A floor plan under the stage

#53, third part: "optional floor-plan underlay (offset, scale, opacity)".

- **A page of the project's own files.** The underlay is a page of one of the project's documents or pictures, chosen under *Floor plan* in the plan's menu: no upload of its own and no new way for a stored file to leave the server. The editor shows the page as every page is shown (decision 032): drawn by the server, behind a short-lived link bound to the session, its render logged.
- **Placed in metres.** Where its top-left corner falls (left and top edges, from the stage's back-left corner), how many metres it spans across (its height follows the page), and its opacity. The plan widens to take in what of the floor plan reaches past the stage.
- **Drawn light.** A floor plan is drawn dark on white paper; on the dark stage its colours are inverted and screened, so the lines read light and the paper drops away.
- **Shown or not.** The underlay switch hides it for this person, as the prototype has it; removing it is in the same dialog. A file removed from the project takes the underlay with it.
- **Not printed.** The PDF export draws the stage and what stands on it; the floor plan stays on screen until printing pages of stored files has its own path through the download permission.

## 170. Personnel

#49, first part. Personnel is in Production after the patch list, as the prototype has it: everyone on the production, grouped as artists, ensembles, staff and others.

- **An entry.** A name, a kind, a role ("Conductor", "Strings"), and whether the session agreement is signed and whether they need headphones.
- **Ensembles.** An ensemble is set out in sections, in order: a head-count from the start, how it divides ("2 + 2"), and its players as they are named, each with their own agreement and headphones. A section never names more players than it counts, and only an ensemble has sections. Seating and forces are a note on the ensemble. Once players are being named, the list counts them ("agreement 1/12"; those not yet named have not signed).
- **Who they are in Hekate.** An entry may be linked to a member of the project or a guest its reviews and groups ask, so the conductor on the stage plan is the one who reviews. Only those are offered or accepted, by name alone. A project moved to another workspace keeps its personnel and loses the links, as its members and guests stay behind.
- **Written whole.** An entry is added or changed with its sections and players at once, in one transaction.
- **Who may.** Those who see the production tools see personnel; those who use them change it.
- **Follows.** Ensemble blocks and performers on the stage plan linked to personnel and sized from the head-count (#53), and the breakdown in schedule exports (#50).

## 171. Who stands on the stage

#53, last part, with #49: "performers and blocks linked to personnel, ensemble blocks sized from the real head-count".

- **Who.** A performer or an ensemble block on a stage plan may stand for someone on the project's personnel: a performer for an artist, staff or other, a block for an ensemble. It is then labelled with their name, so renaming them in personnel renames them on the plan and in its PDF. Only this project's personnel is accepted; taking someone off the personnel leaves the item, unlinked.
- **Sized from the head-count.** Linking a block to an ensemble sizes it in the same step from its players (a metre across and 1.2 m deep each, in rows a little wider than deep: `ensembleBlock` in packages/shared). *Size from the head-count* does it again after the ensemble grows or shrinks; the block may be resized by hand as before.

## 172. Repertoire and venues

#48, first part. The repertoire and venues are in Production, first and last of its sections, as the prototype has them; the sections scroll sideways on a phone rather than wrapping.

- **Works.** A title, composer, forces, the abbreviation a schedule shows, notes, and the work's tracks in order, each numbered as the score prints it ("1", "I", "2a"; typed in place). A track is in one work at most; a single-track work is just one track. A work holds tracks; it never replaces them, so removing a work leaves its tracks, and removing a track takes it out of its work.
- **Order.** Schedule order, or alphabetical by title past a leading article, numbers in order ("No. 2" before "No. 10"). Schedule order is the order the schedule first reaches each work, through the work, one of its tracks or a slot of all repertoire; works with no slot follow in the order they were added (#50, `scheduleOrder` in packages/shared).
- **Venues.** A name (one per project), a multi-line address, and notes for the get-in.
- **Who may.** Those who see the production tools see them; those who use them change them.
- **Follows.** A work's score with each track's printed page range and the page offset (#48, second part); a stage plan for a venue, and the schedule's days at venues (#50).

## 173. A work's score and its tracks' pages

#48, second part: "link a work to a score file; each track has its printed page range; a per-file page offset maps printed pages to PDF pages".

- **One score for the work.** A work may name one of the project's PDFs as its score. Its tracks then follow it: each track's score becomes the work's, by the same swap as a track's own (#52), so what was marked on a track's old edition follows its bars. A work without a score leaves its tracks' scores as they are.
- **Printed pages.** Each of the work's tracks may have the printed pages it runs over ("pp. 8–14", or one page). A printed page is the number on the page, not the PDF's; the work's page offset is added to find the PDF's page (2 when a cover and a contents page come before printed page 1).
- **Opening at its pages.** Score marking opens a track at its first page in the work's score (`pdfPages` in packages/shared), so a track inside one long PDF starts where it starts.
- **Swap.** *Swap the score, keeping marks* on a work chooses another edition; the page ranges stay as typed, for the new edition's offset to place.

## 174. The schedule

#50, first part. The schedule is in Production after the repertoire, as the prototype has it: days, their rows in time, ticked off as the day goes, and the coverage of every track.

- **Days.** A label, a date, a venue (removing the venue leaves the day without one) and when its first row starts. Days run in the order they were added.
- **Rows.** A repertoire slot, a break, a get-in, a venue change or anything else, each with its minutes, personnel and notes. A row starts where the row before ends unless it sets its own start (a lunch at 13:00 whatever runs late before it); `timesOf` in packages/shared works the times out, past midnight too. Rows move up and down, and *Insert a break after* puts one straight after a row.
- **Slots.** A slot holds any mix of works and tracks of the project, or the whole repertoire; only a slot holds repertoire. Removing a work or a track takes it out of its slots.
- **Ticking off.** Those who use the production tools tick rows off on any day, after the fact too; a ticked row fades. On a day dated today the schedule refreshes every few seconds for everyone, and each unfinished slot can run a timer to its end.
- **Coverage.** Every track with its slots across the days and their minutes; a track with none shows *No slot*.
- **Who may.** Those who see the production tools see the schedule; those who use them change it and tick it off.
- **Follows.** The PDF and XLSX exports (front page, per-day tables, the ensemble breakdown) and the schedule driving the workflow (#50, later parts); schedule order for the repertoire.

## 175. The schedule's exports

#50, second part: "exports: a typeset PDF (front page with public session information, per-day tables, an ensemble breakdown page) and an XLSX workbook".

- **PDF.** A4, made on the server like the patch list (#54). A front page with what may go beyond the team: the project, the dates it spans, the venues used with their addresses, the repertoire with composer and forces, and each day with its venue and hours; no notes and no personnel. Then each day as a table (time, what, personnel, minutes, notes), carried over pages with *(cont.)*, slots in bold. Then the ensemble breakdown: each ensemble with its head-count and its sections' players, and the artists and staff by role.
- **Workbook.** Three sheets: every row with its day, date, venue, times, kind, what it holds, minutes, personnel, notes and whether it is done; the coverage of every track; and the ensembles' sections.
- **Words.** What a row says (`rowWords` in packages/shared) is the same on the page, in the PDF and in the workbook.
- **Who may.** Those who see the production tools; the files are not kept between them and the server (`no-store`).
- **Follows.** The schedule driving the workflow, and the repertoire's schedule order (#50, last part).

## 176. The schedule drives the rest

#50, last part: "the schedule can drive the workflow (for example, a track's first review starts the day after its last slot)".

- **Recorded.** A workflow may start from a new trigger, *A track's last slot is done*. Ticking off a slot fires it for each track the slot holds that has no slot left to do (`tracksFinished` in packages/shared): the track's own, its work's, or all repertoire. A run under way that waits for it carries on. A wait after it gives "the day after its last slot". Ticking a row that was already done fires nothing again. Ticks on one project's schedule take turns (a transaction lock), each working out inside its turn which tracks it finished, so two people ticking a track's last two slots at once record it once.
- **Schedule order.** The repertoire's schedule order follows the schedule (decision 172).
- **Calendar.** Each dated day of the schedule is on the project's calendar and its feed, as "Day 1 schedule", for those who see the production tools, linking to the schedule. The feed's description says it holds schedule days, as anyone with its link sees them.

## 177. Migrations that lose nothing unasked

#154, first part: "`MIGRATE_ON_START` takes an advisory lock so two replicas cannot migrate at once; a migration that would drop or rewrite data refuses to run without `MIGRATE_ALLOW_DESTRUCTIVE`; a pre-flight at start-up compares the schema version with the code".

- **One at a time.** Migrating takes a session advisory lock in PostgreSQL on its one connection and reads what is pending under it, so replicas starting together migrate once: the first applies, the rest wait and find nothing left.
- **Losing data.** A pending migration that drops a table, a column or a type, changes a column's type, truncates or deletes rows is refused, naming each statement, until the operator starts once with `MIGRATE_ALLOW_DESTRUCTIVE=true`. Nothing is applied when one is refused, the harmless ones before it included. A backfill (`UPDATE`) adds data and is not counted. A new, empty database is never refused, though early migrations dropped columns: there is nothing in it to lose.
- **Known.** The migrations that lose data are listed in `packages/db/src/migrate.test.ts`. Adding one fails that test until it is listed, which is the reminder to write its upgrade notes.
- **Pre-flight.** Without `MIGRATE_ON_START`, start-up compares the database with the code: behind, it names the pending migrations and how to apply them; ahead (migrated by a newer release), it refuses whatever the setting, as running would misread the data. Each stops the app before anything is served.
- **Upgrade notes.** A change an operator must act on carries a `BREAKING CHANGE:` footer, which Release Please writes under the release's "⚠ BREAKING CHANGES"; a release pull request may add an "Upgrade notes" section by hand. The docs site's build gathers both from `CHANGELOG.md` into one Upgrade notes page, newest first, and says so when none were needed.
- **Follows.** Maintenance mode, with instance settings (#160).

## 178. Instance settings and maintenance mode

#160, first part, with #154's maintenance mode: "settings live in an `instance_settings` table (global) with a zod schema, and every change is an audit event".

- **One row.** `instance_settings` is global: a setting is the instance's, not a workspace's. Its one row holds the settings as JSON, checked by `instanceSettingsInput` in packages/shared and read over the defaults, so a row written before a setting existed still reads. Every process reads it again within 15 seconds, so a change needs no restart.
- **Every change an event.** Saving records one instance event for each thing that changed (a second factor required or not, maintenance on, off, or a new notice), shown on the Overview's Recently.
- **A second factor for every account.** The switch joins `REQUIRE_SECOND_FACTOR`: either asks it of every account. When the environment sets it, the switch shows on and cannot be turned off.
- **Maintenance mode.** While it is on, every page, signed in or not, shows a notice under which the operator may add a line. Uploads (starting one, its chunks, finishing it, a comment's attachment) are refused with 503 and the files they would send stay with the sender. The job workers stop taking jobs and finish those under way, and the queue keeps the rest until it is over. Nothing is lost.
- **What the environment sets.** Shown beside them, read-only: operators' security keys, session length, password rules, the address and instance mail.
- **Follows.** Defaults for new workspaces (storage, members, guest links, forensic marking) with quotas (#175) and marking (#32); workspace subdomains (#176); mail channels and a test (#161); telemetry (#162); modules with billing (#178) and support (#181).

## 179. System information

#158, as decided there: "for 1.0 the page shows versions and available releases only; the image stays immutable". Hot patches would be a later, opt-in feature of their own.

- **What runs.** The release (the server package's version, which Release Please sets) and the build: the commit the image was built from, passed in as `HEKATE_BUILD` by the release workflow, or "a development build". Each release the instance starts is noted once, for the History.
- **Releases.** With `HEKATE_UPDATE_CHECK=true`, a job reads the project's public releases feed once a day (and on *Check now*); nothing about the instance is sent. Off by default, the instance asks nothing of anyone. Drafts and tags that are not versions are left out; a failed check is kept beside the releases the last good one found, so the page says why it is behind.
- **Channel.** Stable offers releases, Beta their release candidates too (an instance setting, #160). The prototype's Edge channel and instance id are gone: no feed offers every build, and nothing needs to identify the instance.
- **Available.** The releases newer than the one running on the channel, each with its notes and how to upgrade (the docs' Upgrades page). A newer release is also on the Overview's "Needs an operator".
- **Environment.** Node.js, PostgreSQL, ffmpeg and Poppler versions, the storage backends, the licence.

## 180. Liveness and readiness

#156, first part: "`/api/health/live` (the process answers) and `/api/health/ready` (database, storage backends, worker, mail reachable), for Compose, Kubernetes and the Dockerfile's HEALTHCHECK".

- **Live.** The process answers: nothing more is checked, so a slow database never gets a healthy process restarted.
- **Ready.** Ready to serve when the database answers and every storage backend can be written to; not ready otherwise, and while the installer runs. The worker (a job finished in the last ten minutes) and mail (the SMTP server answers) are reported beside them but never make a replica unready: taking every replica out of a load balancer for want of mail would only make things worse (`readiness` in packages/shared).
- **Public, so states only.** The answers name no host, bucket or address, only `ok`, `failed`, `stale` or `off` for each part.
- **Kept ten seconds.** A storage check writes a probe, so a readiness answer is kept that long rather than written for every probe.
- **`/api/health`** stays as it was for the image's `HEALTHCHECK`.
- **Follows.** The System health page: gauges, the last 24 hours, services and storage (#156, later parts).

## 181. System health

#156, second part: "The System health page: Now (CPU, memory, disk, database connections as gauges), the last 24 hours (requests per minute, p95 latency, error rate, queue depth, worker lanes busy, uploads) as sparklines … services … The metrics catalogue … Done when the page reads from `/metrics` data kept in PostgreSQL for a day".

- **A minute a row.** Each process writes one row a minute to `health_samples` (global; a replica is a random id, never its host), and deletes rows a day old as it does. The counts are the differences between two readings of the registry's own counters, and the p95 is worked out from the histogram's buckets as `histogram_quantile` does, so each chart shows what the metric it names would.
- **Every replica together.** Requests, errors, lanes and uploads add up across replicas; the p95 is the slowest replica's, and queue depth (read from the shared queue) is the same for all.
- **Points.** The hour is a point a minute, the day a point a quarter of an hour, rates per minute measured (`healthSeries` in packages/shared). A point no process measured is a gap, not a zero. The prototype's "Last 7 days" went: a day is kept.
- **Now** is the answering process's machine: processors over its last minute, memory, the disk holding local storage (or the working directory) and the database's connections.
- **Services** are the readiness checks (decision 180) with what explains them. The prototype's Proxy went (Hekate cannot see Caddy from inside), and so did the worker's Start button: the worker runs in the app's process, so a stopped one is a restarted container. The billing gateway's row follows billing.
- **Worker lanes** are new metrics: `hekate_worker_lanes` (jobs a process may run at once) and `hekate_worker_lanes_busy`.
- **Metrics** lists the registry itself, with each metric's help, so the catalogue cannot drift from what is exported.
- **Follows.** Storage backends with usage, the master key and reconciliation (#156, last part).

## 182. System health: storage

#156, last part: "storage backends with usage, the master key with its rotation date, reconciliation with \"run a slice now\"".

- **Backends.** Each configured backend with where it keeps objects (path, or bucket and region), whether it takes new objects, the bytes stored there, and whether it answers now. A local backend also shows its disk filling; S3 has no size to fill, so it shows none.
- **The master key** lives in the environment, so the page cannot rotate it: it says how many workspace keys it wraps, warns while earlier keys are still configured, and links to the rotation steps in the docs. The prototype's rotation date went: nothing records when an operator changed the environment.
- **Reconciliation** runs over everything each night, not a slice at a time, so the prototype's "Run a slice now" became "Run now": it queues the same job (one at a time, three a quarter of an hour), recorded in the instance's events. The row says when the last instance-wide run finished and what is still open.

## 183. A workspace's Operations page

#165: "For an owner on a single-workspace install: what the instance pages show the operator, reduced to what an owner may see (version, worker on or off, queue depth, encryption state, the master key's rotation date, backups and upgrade notes with links to the docs)."

- **Owners only**, in the workspace's settings under Operations, read every 15 seconds (`/api/organisation/operations`).
- **This workspace's jobs**, not the instance's: the queue counts jobs whose data names the workspace, so an owner on a shared instance learns nothing of the others.
- **Encryption** is the workspace key's version and age, and how much is still being re-wrapped after a rotation. Rotating stays an operator's action (decision 174), so there is no Rotate button. The master key's rotation date is not recorded (decision 182), so it is not shown.
- **Metrics** say whether Prometheus may read `/metrics`, and when this process was last read.
- **Backups and upgrades** are the operator's: the page links to the docs, and names the newer release when release checks are on. The prototype's "Connected" went: the page loading says as much. So did its backup time and size, which Hekate does not know: backups are taken outside it.

## 184. Instance settings: defaults, a mail test, modules

#160, second part.

- **Defaults for new workspaces** start with how long guest links last, the one default a workspace already keeps (its owner may change it). New workspaces an operator makes are seeded from it; setup's first workspace takes the plain default, as no setting exists before it. Storage quota and members follow usage limits (#175), forensic marking follows #32. Older clients that send no defaults keep what is stored.
- **Instance mail has a Test**: a message to the operator's own address, which says whether the mail server took it and shows its refusal when not. A few a quarter of an hour.
- **Modules** are shown read-only, as the environment set them: billing, the support desk, the documentation and the status page.
- **Follows.** Telemetry with #162, registration with #184, subdomains with #176.

## 185. Logs as tables: audit and mail first

#157, first part.

- **Audit** reads what is already written, not a new table: the operators' instance events, and each workspace's access log less streams and page renders (which stay in the workspace's own log, as on the instance's workspace page). An operator's event about a workspace (its id in the details) counts as that workspace's when filtered.
- **Mail** gains a writer: every message handed to instance mail is written to `mail_events` (global: a message may concern no workspace) as sent, refused with the mail server's words (kept to 500 characters), or written to the log where there is no SMTP. Writing it never holds up the message nor hides its failure.
- **Reading.** Newest first a page at a time, by id: ids are UUIDv7, so a range is a scan of the primary key. Search, a range of an hour to thirty days, and a workspace (audit).
- **Export** streams the whole filtered log as CSV, its cells neutralised as the access log's; it is an instance event (`logs.export`) written before any of it leaves.
- **Follows.** Authentication, with writers for sign-ins, refusals, lockouts, second factors and guest links; then retention per kind as a job, and the live tail. API, webhooks and the billing gateway arrive with their features. The prototype's Email tab loses its Template and Provider columns: a message knows its subject, not its template.

## 186. The authentication log

#157, second part.

- **One writer for every sign-in.** A session made by a sign-in path is the sign-in: password, passkey, a second factor, the workspace's directory or its single sign-on (`signInMethodOf`), recorded from the session Better Auth just made, with its workspace where the session names one. A password that waits for its second factor is not yet a sign-in; the second factor's is. Refusals are recorded with Better Auth's reason, a lockout as locked, and a try made to wait as refused for too many attempts.
- **Guests too:** a link opened (or one that opens nothing), a code right or wrong, each with the guest's workspace.
- **Global** (`auth_events`, in `globalTables` like error reports): a member's sign-in belongs to their account, not a workspace. Where a workspace is known it is kept, and goes with the workspace.
- **The address** is read as the access log reads it: X-Forwarded-For's last entry behind Caddy (TRUST_PROXY), none otherwise, as a client could claim any.
- **Written before the answer goes back**, so deleting the account later finds every row that names it; a write that fails is told to the server's log, and never refuses the person.
- **Forgetting.** Deleting an account rewrites its address to "A former member" here and in the mail log, as the access log and the instance's events already do.

## 187. Logs: retention and the live tail

#157, last part.

- **Retention per kind** is an instance setting: 30 or 90 days, a year, two years, or for good. A job trims each log once a day (`logs-retention`) and writes what it trimmed as an instance event (`logs.purged`), as every change of the setting is one (`logs.retention`).
- **Defaults:** authentication a year, mail 90 days, and the audit log for good. Nothing already logged goes because the instance was upgraded; an operator who wants the access log trimmed says so.
- **A few thousand rows to a transaction**, oldest first by their time-ordered ids, for every log: a long backlog never holds one transaction. The audit log is the operators' instance events and every workspace's access log; the access log goes only through its purge setting (decision 020), one workspace at a time.
- **What went is recorded even when a run fails part-way:** `logs.purged` counts what each committed batch removed, marked incomplete if a later one failed, and the next day's run carries on.
- **The live tail** reads the open log again every five seconds while Live is on.

## 188. Several replicas: what each process holds, and how they agree

#188. An audit of every piece of state the server keeps in memory, for running several app processes against one PostgreSQL.

- **Already shared through the database:** sessions, rate limits and sign-in lockouts (decision 051), jobs and their schedules (pg-boss runs each scheduled job once, whichever process starts it), upload records, download tickets, workspace keys and their rotation (each request asks for the current version), migrations (decision 177) and the health samples (decision 181). Playback tokens are signed with a key derived from `AUTH_SECRET`, the same on every replica.
- **Settings and flags are announced.** An operator's change is announced on one PostgreSQL channel (`NOTIFY hekate_instance`) in the transaction that makes it, so PostgreSQL delivers it on commit and never for a change rolled back. Every process listens and reads the settings or flags again at once, and holds or frees its job workers as maintenance mode says. On each connection a listener reads everything again, as it may have missed something; the fifteen-second rereads (decisions 068 and 178) stay as the fallback. One channel, no message broker.
- **Scratch directories carry their host.** A job's directory is named for its host and process, as process ids repeat across containers. A host removes its own at once when their process is gone; another host's, or one named before hosts were, only once nothing in it has changed for an hour past the longest any job may run, as by then its job is over whoever ran it. That also clears what a replaced container left under its old name. Each worker sweeps at start and hourly.
- **Configured through the environment.** The installer's settings file belongs to one replica, so several are configured through the environment, or a shared file. The setup code's tries are counted per process: with twelve letters and digits to guess, ten tries per replica every ten minutes changes nothing, and the code is used only until the first owner exists.
- **Local storage needs a shared filesystem** (an upload's parts go to whichever replica takes each request); S3 is the choice for more than one machine.
- **The master key changes by restarts, in three rounds** (every replica learns the new key as a previous one, then makes it current, then forgets the old), as each process re-wraps the workspace keys at start with its own.
- **System health is every replica's.** The page's current processors, memory and lanes are the answering replica's own, read as they are, with every other running replica's last minute from the health samples: lanes and memory add up, and the processors' share is weighted by cores (two replicas on one machine leave every share as it was). The disk stays the answering replica's, as it is a local path. Each minute's sample also records when that replica's metrics were last read, so the Operations page shows the latest of any replica's.

## 189. Roles and lanes

#189. How the work divides between processes, so media work scales without adding API replicas.

- **`ROLE`: all, api or worker.** `all` is the default, as one process serves most studios. A worker answers only `/api/health*` and `/metrics`, for probes and Prometheus; every other request is refused there, so it is never a second way in. `WORKER=false` keeps meaning `api`: no setting is renamed, nothing for an operator to change.
- **Five lanes, fixed:** transcode, analyse, pages, mail and system. Each kind of job belongs to one, written beside its definition (`jobDefinitions`); lanes follow the work's cost (transcoding apart from the lighter analysis) and the jobs that must run somewhere (system and mail), not one per job.
- **`WORKER_LANES`** names the lanes a worker takes and, optionally, how many of each kind of job in the lane at once (`transcode:4,analyse`); unset, every lane at the defaults, as before. A lane given no number keeps each job's own concurrency. The count is per kind, as `WORKER_CONCURRENCY`'s always was, not shared by a lane: pg-boss claims a job before its handler runs, so a shared count would have a worker hold claimed jobs while it waited, which idle workers could have run, until their claims expired hours later.
- **Queue depth by lane** (`hekate_lane_jobs_waiting`) beside the per-queue gauges, the one figure an autoscaler needs per worker pool.
- **Graceful stop** stays pg-boss's: running jobs finish for up to thirty seconds; one that does not is retried once it expires.

## 190. PostgreSQL: high availability, poolers and failover

#192, a spike. Hekate was run through PgBouncer 1.22 in transaction mode, its whole server test suite against it.

- **One writable primary; no read replicas.** Requests write, lock and read their own writes. A managed service's writer endpoint, CloudNativePG's read-write service or Patroni's primary address are all the same to Hekate.
- **Transaction pooling needs the pooler's prepared-statement support.** postgres.js prepares its queries by name. Through PgBouncer with `max_prepared_statements = 0` (1.22's default), 118 of 500 tests failed with wrong answers, not errors: a statement prepared by one client ran for another. With `max_prepared_statements = 200`, or with postgres.js told not to prepare, every test but `LISTEN`'s passed. The docs say to set it; not preparing at all is #411.
