- **`LISTEN` needs a session.** Behind a transaction pooler, replicas' word to each other (decision 188) never arrives, and the fifteen-second rereads carry settings and flags instead. A direct connection for listening and migrating is #412.
- **Migrations' session lock** wants a direct connection too, and is taken at every start that migrates, pending migrations or not. The docs say to migrate once past the pooler and to start every replica behind it with `MIGRATE_ON_START=false`, which the image otherwise turns on.
- **Everything else is transaction-scoped** (`set local`, transaction advisory locks, pg-boss's own locks, which also polls rather than listens), so safe behind a transaction pooler.
- **Failover** costs the requests and jobs under way: requests fail for the moment of the switch, jobs are retried, listeners reconnect and reread. Asynchronous replication can lose committed transactions on failover; storage reconciliation then removes what the database no longer names.

## 191. No statement prepared by name

#411. postgres.js no longer prepares statements by name (`prepare: false`): behind a transaction pooler without prepared-statement support, which is PgBouncer's default, a name prepared on one server connection ran another client's statement and answered with the wrong rows (decision 190). Unnamed statements are parsed for each query, a small cost, and cannot be confused. One fewer thing for an operator to set, and get wrong silently.

CI runs the API suite through PgBouncer in transaction mode at its defaults (the `pooled` job), so it stays so; the replicas' signals, which need a session (#412), are tested only directly.

## 192. A direct connection for what needs a session

#412. `DATABASE_DIRECT_URL`, optional, reaches the primary past a transaction pooler for the two things that need a session of their own (decision 190): migrations, whose advisory lock lasts the run (at start and in the browser installer alike), and the `LISTEN` by which replicas hear of a change (decision 188). Each replica holds one direct connection to listen on, and opens another only to migrate as it starts; every request and job goes through `DATABASE_URL`. Unset, `DATABASE_URL` serves for both, as before. The `pooled` CI job runs the whole server suite through PgBouncer with it set, the replicas' signals included.

## 193. Zero-downtime deploys

#193. Replicas upgrade one at a time, so for a while the old release and the new run side by side, against one database.

- **Expand, then contract.** A release's migrations work with the release before it still running: add before using, stop using before removing. A rename, a drop, a tightened constraint or a changed type takes two releases. AGENTS.md says so, and reviews block a migration the previous release cannot run against.
- **Told to stop (SIGTERM),** a process says it is not ready (`/api/health/ready` answers 503, `stopping`) and keeps serving for `SHUTDOWN_DRAIN_SECONDS` (5) while the load balancer takes it out; then stops taking connections, closes idle ones, and gives the requests under way half of what is left of `SHUTDOWN_TIMEOUT_SECONDS` (30) before closing the rest; then stops its job workers with the remainder, and closes the database. The drain never takes the two seconds the later steps are owed, so the whole stays within the timeout; both settings are read as every other is, from the environment or the settings file. The steps and their timing are one tested function (`lifecycle.ts`).
- **Jobs are released, not lost.** pg-boss's graceful stop lets running jobs finish within the time; past it, it fails them, which aborts their handlers and queues their retry, so another worker runs them again. Tested by stopping a replica mid-job and finishing the job on another.
- **Uploads resume.** A chunk cut off mid-way is never recorded, so the browser sends it again, to any replica, and the upload completes whole. Tested across two app instances.
- **Compose** gives the app 40 seconds to stop (`stop_grace_period`), longer than the timeout; the Helm chart's grace period and rolling update follow when it comes (#190).

## 194. Docker Swarm

#191. `deploy/stack.yml`, for `docker stack deploy`, beside the Compose file for one machine.

- **Two app services from one image:** `api` (`ROLE=api`, two replicas by default, anywhere) and `worker` (`ROLE=worker`, on machines labelled `hekate.worker=true`); every app task keeps its scratch space in memory, as API replicas make watermarked downloads and marked pages in it too. The API replicas migrate as they start, one at a time (decision 177); workers never do.
- **Every key a Docker secret**, read through the `_FILE` settings: the master key, `AUTH_SECRET`, the database URL and password, the S3 secret key, and the mail URL if mail is sent. Nothing secret is written in the stack or its environment.
- **Configured, not installed.** The browser installer writes to one replica's settings file, so the stack gives every setting, and requires `HEKATE_SETUP_CODE`, as replicas each making their own code would refuse each other's.
- **S3 only.** Local storage would need a filesystem every machine mounts; the stack does not pretend to offer one.
- **Rolling updates without a gap.** `api` starts each new replica, healthy, before stopping an old one, and rolls back by itself when a new one fails; workers stop first, their jobs finishing or going back to the queue. Every app task has 40 seconds to stop (decision 193).
- **One PostgreSQL** on a labelled machine, for a start; the docs point to a managed service or Patroni for more (decision 190). Caddy on a manager, through Swarm's virtual address for `api`, retrying for up to five seconds when a replica stops as a request arrives.
- **CI checks the stack reads** (`docker stack config`) on every pull request; it is not deployed there, as it needs S3 and a swarm.


## 195. Load testing

#196. k6 scenarios in `load/hekate.js`, with the targets in the docs (Operate, Load testing) as their thresholds.

- **k6, outside the workspace.** Its scripts run in k6's own runtime, not Node, so they are plain JavaScript with no package; CI runs k6's image, pinned by digest, and nothing is added to the lockfile or the app image.
- **Self-contained.** The test sets up a fresh instance with its setup code (or signs in to one), makes its own project, uploads a minute of audio made by ffmpeg, waits for it to stream and asks for a review, so it needs no seed and runs against any test instance.
- **What a studio does at once:** playback (a stream started, its playlists, key and every segment), comments posted and listed, the review page, uploads in parts, and transcodes timed from upload to streaming. Arrival rates, not looping users, so a slow server shows as latency rather than as less load; `HEKATE_SCALE` multiplies them all.
- **Within Hekate's limits, not around them.** Ten sign-ins a minute from one address and thirty stream keys a minute for each session stay as they are; the test signs in a few sessions once and spreads playback over them. No setting relaxes a limit for testing.
- **Targets as a floor.** They are what the image meets on a GitHub-hosted runner with k6, PostgreSQL and Caddy on the same four cores, with room to spare: a run that misses one is a regression to look into, not a statement of what production hardware does.
- **When it runs:** by hand (Actions, Load test), on every release pull request before it merges (Release Please changes `.release-please-manifest.json` in each), and on any change to the test. The app image is served as the Compose file serves it, and k6's summary is kept as an artefact.

## 196. No CDN for media; more replicas instead

#194, a spike. Segments are ciphertext, but every playlist, segment and key request is checked against the viewer's session and current membership, and starting a stream, every key and every segment are logged (decision 022, `docs/security.md`), so no shared cache can serve them today. Three ways were weighed.

- **Cacheable segment URLs, the key alone session-bound.** Segments would be signed for a few minutes and marked public, so a CDN could serve them, with playlists and keys still bound to the session. It costs three things the design relies on. *The access log* would lose `stream.segment` for every request the edge answers, and with it which parts of a file reached whom; getting it back means importing the CDN's own logs, late, and trusting them. *Revocation* would no longer be immediate: taking a role away ends playback at once today, because every segment request checks membership; with cached URLs, segments keep flowing until they expire, and hls.js fetches a rendition's key only once. *A copied URL* would work for anyone until it expires: harmless without the key, but one captured key plus cached ciphertext is then a whole file with no session behind it. Forensic A/B marking (#32) does not rule it out (each segment would be cached as two variants, and each viewer's sequence lives in the playlist, which stays session-bound), but the sequence would then be visible to anyone holding the playlist's URLs, and the log of which variant reached whom would again be the CDN's.
- **Edge caching of peaks and spectrograms.** They are small, made once, and each read is one stored object; a cache would save little, and they are derived from unreleased material (a spectrogram shows a mix's content), so they stay `no-store` and session-bound like everything else from the file.
- **No CDN, more replicas.** API replicas are stateless (decisions 188 and 189), so serving more viewers is adding replicas behind the load balancer. The load test (decision 195) has one replica on a shared runner serving two streams a second, about twenty segments a second, at a p95 of well under the 500 ms target and far from its limit; a listener needs one six-second segment every six seconds, so those twenty a second are already some 120 people listening at once, more than a studio's review has.

**Decided:** no CDN or shared cache in front of media, pages, peaks or spectrograms; every response stays `no-store` and session-bound, what is logged today (streams started, keys, segments and full-size pages) stays logged, and scale comes from replicas. Revisit if a hosted offering (or an install with reviewers across continents) shows segment latency that replicas in one region cannot fix. The path then is the first option with the playlist and key endpoints kept session-bound, segment URLs living no longer than a segment's worth of playback, the CDN's logs imported into the access log as `stream.segment`, and forensic marking (#32) in place first, so that a leaked copy still names its recipient.

## 197. Kubernetes: a Helm chart

#190. `deploy/helm/hekate`, beside the Compose file and the Swarm stack, released with the app at one version.

- **Two Deployments from one image,** `api` and `worker` (decision 189), each with its own replicas, resources, scratch size, scheduling and autoscaler. `api` rolls with a surge and nothing unavailable, so a new replica is ready before an old one stops; `worker` stops its old pod first, its jobs finishing or going back to the queue. Every pod has 40 seconds to stop (decision 193), its readiness probe saying `stopping` first.
- **Probes from the health endpoints** (#156): liveness on `/api/health`, readiness on `/api/health/ready`, and a startup probe long enough for a first start that migrates.
- **Migrations:** a `pre-upgrade` Job runs the new command `cli.ts migrate` (the same lock and the same refusal of destructive migrations as a starting server, #154), so upgrades migrate before anything rolls. As a hook runs before the release's ConfigMap and Secret change, it reads copies of them made for it, so an upgrade that allows a destructive migration (or changes the database) migrates with its own values. On install, the first `api` replica migrates as it starts, as nothing else can reach the database before the release's own Secret exists. Workers never migrate.
- **Configured, not installed.** Settings are a ConfigMap and the keys a Secret, the chart's or one named in `secrets.existingSecret`, so External Secrets and the like work unchanged. The setup code is required, as in the Swarm stack (decision 194), and kept in the Secret: until the first owner exists, it is what takes the instance. Client addresses come from `X-Forwarded-For` only behind the Ingress, unless `trustProxy` says otherwise. A release runs against a later one's database when the later one's migrations only expand it (decision 198), so `helm rollback` works after most upgrades; after one that contracts, going back means restoring the backup taken before it, as with Compose.
- **Storage:** S3 by default; a `ReadWriteMany` volume, kept on uninstall, for clusters that have one. **Database:** an external URL (with a direct URL past a pooler, decision 192), or a CloudNativePG `Cluster`, whose own Secret supplies the URL.
- **Hardened pods:** a non-root user, a read-only filesystem, no capabilities, the runtime's seccomp profile, no service account token, and scratch space in memory only.
- **Autoscaling** on CPU for both, and for workers optionally on `hekate_lane_jobs_waiting` through an external metrics adapter; Hekate does not ship one. A disruption budget keeps one `api` replica up through node drains; workers have none by default, as their jobs are retried.
- **Ingress** with TLS, and an optional wildcard host for workspace subdomains once they exist (#176). Its default annotation lifts ingress-nginx's body limit above an upload's 8 MiB parts.
- **CI** lints the chart, renders it with its defaults and with every option on, checks the manifests with kubeconform and Trivy's misconfiguration rules, and installs and upgrades it on a kind cluster with the image built from the commit.

## 198. An older release runs against an expanded database

#421. A release used to refuse any database with a migration it did not know, which made the expand-then-contract rule (decision 193) half true: replicas of the old release that were already running served on during a rolling upgrade, but one that restarted meanwhile (a crash, an eviction, an autoscaler adding one), and every rollback, was refused.

- **What older code must know.** Each migration either expands the schema, which the release before it can run against, or contracts it: it loses data (decision 177's destructive statements), makes a column required, adds a required column without a default, adds a uniqueness, check or foreign key constraint or a unique index to a table older code knows, or renames. `contractingStatements` finds them, a pure function with tests, erring towards contraction; a migration can say so itself with `-- hekate: contracts`.
- **Recorded in the database.** Before migrating, under the same lock, a release writes the newest contracting migration of its journal to `drizzle.hekate_readable_from`, beside drizzle's own table: first, so that a release starting while it migrates, or after a migration failed half way, never reads a looser cutoff than the schema needs. A release that finds migrations it does not know reads it: if it knows that migration, it runs (and says so, without migrating); otherwise it refuses, as before. A database migrated before this record existed has none, and is refused as before.
- **What it gives.** Old replicas restart during a roll; `helm rollback`, `docker service rollback` and setting `HEKATE_VERSION` back work after an upgrade that only expanded, with no restore. After one that contracts, the older release still refuses, and the backup taken before the upgrade is the way back.
- **How far back.** Not only the release before: any release that knows the newest contraction. The checks are conservative, so some migrations that are in fact safe (a check on a column the same migration adds) count as contracting; that costs a rollback a restore, never a misread.

## 199. Upload requests

#139. A producer asks someone outside a project, with no account, for files: a link that sends them into one of the project's folders.

- **Made from the files page** by those who may organise the project's files, as the prototype offers it beside new folders: a name, what is wanted, the folder, a file limit (up to 100), the day it closes (within 90) and an optional password, and optionally an address to mail the link to with the workspace's upload request template. Members see their requests, what arrived and from whom, and may close one or give it a new link.
- **The link is the credential, and reads nothing** (docs/security.md, Upload requests). Only its hash is kept, so it is shown once; a new link replaces it. The visitor's page shows the request's own words and counts; its routes start, send and finish the visitor's own uploads into the request's folder, and nothing else.
- **The same uploads as members'.** The chunked, encrypted upload steps are one engine (`upload-engine.ts`) behind both the members' routes and the visitor's, which differ only in who owns an upload and how the file is recorded: by the member, or as from the request, with the sender's name, in `upload_request_files`.
- **The limit holds.** A file takes a place under a lock on the request as it starts; one still being sent counts, and one abandoned frees its place when its upload lapses. A closed or lapsed request refuses new files and the finishing of ones under way.
- **Told and logged.** The requester is told as each file arrives (`upload_arrived`, in the app and by mail by default). The access log records the request made and closed, wrong passwords, and each upload under a `visitor` actor named as the sender gave.
- **Not moved with a project.** Moving a project to another workspace (decision 135) drops its upload requests, like calendar links, as each was given out under the losing workspace; the files sent through them move with the project, and the access log keeps who sent them.
- **Left out for now:** "as the next version of" a track, which the issue lists but the prototype does not show; a version is made only from a measured file, so it would follow the upload rather than be part of it (it followed: decision 223).
- **Expanding only:** the request, its uploads and its files are new tables (`upload_requests`, `upload_request_uploads`, `upload_request_files`), so the release before runs against the database (decision 198).

## 200. General projects: simple version chains on loose files

A general project is folders and files (#149). Documents and pictures are drawn as pages (decision 032) with region comments (decision 033); anything else has an "other file" page: what it is, its details, and comments about the whole file. Reviews ask about files exactly as about tracks, approvals included.

- **A file replaces the one before.** A loose file (in no track's version and no take) can take a next version: dropped or chosen in New version on its page, or picked from the project's loose files of its kind. The new file takes the old one's folder, and `files.previous_file_id` names the one it replaces. Each file has one later version at most, and only a file in no chain becomes one, so a chain never forks or loops; linking and deleting take the project's lock, so two at once cannot break that. A track's files keep their versions in the track.
- **The latest stands for the chain.** Lists and the review picker show each chain by its latest file, its details led by its number ("V3 · PDF · 4 pages"); the file page offers every version a step away, and a review naming a file stays on that file, as one naming a track's version does.
- **Simple means simple.** Comments stay on the file they were written on; nothing is carried, aligned or compared along a chain. Versions are always "V1, V2" here, whatever the profile, since cuts and versions belong to tracks.
- **Taking one out closes the gap.** Deleting a file in the middle of a chain links the one after it to the one before; deleting the first leaves the rest as a chain of their own. A file under review cannot be deleted, as before.
- **Expand now, constrain later.** This release adds the nullable column and a plain index only, so the release before runs against the database (decision 198). Its foreign key and uniqueness constrain a table older code knows, which contracts, so they follow in a later release (#427), once no release before this one need run against the database. (They came before any release was cut, so none ever ran without them: `0140_file_chain_constraints`.)

## 201. A spectrogram's window, FFT size and overlap, drawn when first asked for

#34. Every file's audio is analysed once with a Hann window, an FFT about 23 Hz a bin and windows overlapping by three quarters. A viewer may choose otherwise, as engineers do in RX: the window (Hann, Hamming, Blackman–Harris or Kaiser), the FFT size (Auto, 1,024, 4,096 or 16,384 points) and the overlap (50, 75 or 87.5 per cent), under Channel, levels, range and analysis beneath the player. The choice is part of the player's view, so it follows the account and is kept in its presets (decision 085); a view kept before the choice existed reads as the default.

- **Drawn on the server, once.** The browser never has the audio to analyse, so anything but the default is drawn by the media worker (`spectrogram-variant`) from its own decode of the file, the first time anyone asks for those settings on that file, and kept as its own spectrograms (`spectrogram_variants`, by the settings' key, `kaiser/16384/75`). Until then the list answers pending and the spectrogram says it is being drawn. One job at a time for each file and set of settings; a second ask while it runs waits for the same one.
- **No more audio than before.** The rules for what a spectrogram may hold (docs/security.md) do not bend with the settings: each column still spans at least 100 ms and now pools at least four windows, whatever the FFT size and overlap; levels are 8 bits with no phase. A large FFT is coarser in time for it.
- **The time and frequency balance is the FFT size.** With columns fixed at a tenth of a second or more, a smaller FFT is sharper in time within a column's span, and a larger one sharper in pitch; there is no second control for the same trade.
- **Failed is a state.** Settings that cannot be drawn for a file after every retry are recorded (`spectrogram_variant_failures`); asked for again they answer failed, and the spectrogram says so with Try again, which alone queues them once more. A failure is forgotten after 30 days.
- **Kept while used.** Each ask marks the set as used; a set unused for 30 days goes (`spectrogram-variants-expire`, daily), and the next ask draws it again. They are the file's like its own spectrograms: removed with it, counted in its project's storage, moved with its project, and kept from reconciliation.
- **Expanding only:** a new table, so the release before runs against the database (decision 198).

## 202. Mail providers: SMTP where they offer it, Microsoft Graph and Mailcoach where they must

#71. Instance mail leaves one way, chosen in the installer's Mail step or set in the environment (docs/configuration.md); the settings refuse two at once, or one given in part.

- **SMTP first.** Every provider that offers SMTP is sent to by SMTP, with its host, port and fixed user filled in: Resend, SparkPost, Mailgun, SendGrid, Amazon SES (the region's endpoint, as it differs), Postal, Plunk and Google Workspace (an app password), and any other server. One protocol, one library, and no client to keep up with each provider's API.
- **Microsoft 365 through Microsoft Graph.** Exchange Online turns basic sign-in for SMTP off by default at the end of December 2026, so Microsoft 365 is reached with an app registration granted Mail.Send (`MAIL_GRAPH_TENANT`, `MAIL_GRAPH_CLIENT_ID`, `MAIL_GRAPH_CLIENT_SECRET`), sending as the `MAIL_FROM` mailbox. The message goes as MIME, so its plain-text and HTML parts arrive as SMTP sends them; the token is kept until a minute before it lapses.
- **Mailcoach through its API.** Mailcoach offers no SMTP of its own, so its transactional API is used (`MAIL_MAILCOACH_URL`, `MAIL_MAILCOACH_TOKEN`), keeping no copy of the message there. The health check asks who the token belongs to.
- **The same everywhere else.** Each way sends through one mailer, so the mail log (decision 157), the metrics, the health check and the instance's Test treat them alike; a refusal is shown in the provider's own words, never with a secret. The secrets may come from files (`_FILE`), and the Helm chart keeps them in its Secret.
- **Bounces follow** with the event system's inbound webhooks (#161), which every provider's bounce reports arrive by.

## 203. Events: an outbox in the causing transaction, one delivery per channel, claimed before it is sent

#161. What happens in a workspace reaches its channels (Slack, Discord, Microsoft Teams and webhooks) through one path.

- **An outbox.** An event is a row in `events`, written by the code that makes it happen and, where that is a transaction (a review asked for, a decision, feedback, a new version, a stage starting, a guest signing in), in that transaction: it is told exactly when the change commits, and never when it does not. A download and a mention are written as they are logged. Nothing is written while no channel of the workspace carries the kind, so a workspace without channels pays one indexed read.
- **Kinds with schemas.** Each kind (`review.requested`, `decision.recorded`, `feedback.submitted`, `version.arrived`, `stage.started`, `comment.mentioned`, `guest.signed_in`, `file.downloaded`, `processing.failed`) has a zod schema in `packages/shared`, checked as it is written. An event names things as they were when it happened, and never carries media or a comment's words.
- **Dispatch, then delivery.** A job a moment after each event (and every minute, as the safety net) makes one delivery for each channel that carries the kind; a unique index makes a second dispatch a no-op. Each delivery is its own job. Before sending, a worker claims the delivery for a minute in one statement, so two workers never send it twice, and a delivery delivered is never sent again. A webhook receives the event's id, the same on every retry and replay, to drop repeats.
- **Retries the delivery's own.** A failure is tried again after 30 seconds, doubling, at most six times, or later where the receiver asks (`Retry-After`); a 404 or 410 is given up at once. Five failures in a row pause the channel for five minutes, and what waits on it waits with it; Resume starts it now. The retries are rows, not queue retries, so a paused channel or a lost job loses nothing: the sweep queues what is due.
- **Kept as a log.** Deliveries are the instance's webhook log (decision 157's fourth kind), trimmed with their events after 30 days unless an operator chooses otherwise; Debug tools send one again as a new delivery of the same event.
- **Expanding only:** new tables, and new values for the access log's actions (decision 198).
- **Mail follows.** Per-workspace mail and the providers' bounce reports are the second half of #161.

## 204. A workspace's own mail: the installer's providers, standing in for nothing lost

#161, second part. A workspace can send its mail through a provider of its own (Settings, Notifications and integrations) rather than the instance's.

- **The installer's choices.** The same providers and checks as the installer's mail step (decision 202), so a workspace and an instance are set up alike; only "log only" is the instance's alone.
- **What it carries.** Every message that names a workspace: notifications and digests, review requests and shares, guest links and codes, reminders, upload requests and invitations. Account mail (password resets, members' codes) stays with the instance: an account is not any one workspace's.
- **One mailer, routed.** Every part of Hekate sends through one mailer, which sends a message that names a workspace through that workspace's mail while it works, and everything else through the instance's. The mail log (decision 157) and the metrics see both.
- **Nothing lost.** A message the workspace's mail refuses is sent by the instance's; five refusals in a row pause the workspace's mail for fifteen minutes, so a broken password does not slow every message. Resume and a new save start afresh. The test sends through the workspace's mail alone, so its failure is the answer.
- **Public only.** An SMTP host is resolved, checked and connected to at that address; Mailcoach is checked before each request. `WEBHOOK_TRUSTED_ORIGINS` allows private ones (`smtp://host:port`).
- **Expanding only:** a new table and a new access-log action (decision 198).
- **Bounces follow:** the providers' bounce reports, and what they stop sending to, are the third part of #161.

## 205. Bounce reports: signed webhooks, and addresses no longer sent to

#161, third part. A provider's bounce and complaint reports stop mail to the addresses they name.

- **Four providers to begin with.** Resend (Svix), Mailgun, SendGrid and SparkPost, whose reports Hekate can check: an HMAC, an ECDSA signature or basic authentication, with the time where the provider signs one. Amazon SES (through SNS), Postal, Plunk and Mailcoach follow; Google Workspace, Microsoft 365 and plain SMTP send no such reports.
- **Two scopes.** The instance's reports (its SMTP host names the provider, `MAIL_WEBHOOK_SECRET` the secret) stop mail to the address for everyone, as an address that does not exist does not exist anywhere. A workspace's own provider's (its secret sealed with its mail) stop only that workspace's mail.
- **Hard bounces and complaints only.** A transient bounce is the provider's to retry; acting on it would stop mail to a full mailbox for good.
- **Held back, not failed.** A message to a reported address is not sent, and the mail log says so; nothing retries it. *Send again* takes an address off the list, in the workspace's access log or as an instance event.
- **Expanding only:** new tables and new mail log results (decision 198).

## 206. OpenTelemetry: traces and logs to the operator's collector, and nowhere else

#162. Hekate sends a span for each request, each job and each media tool run, and every log line, to the OpenTelemetry collector the operator names (`OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT`), over OTLP/HTTP.

- **Off until named.** Without an address the API's no-op stands in: a span costs nothing and nothing leaves the server. There is no default collector and no telemetry to the project.
- **The SDK, not auto-instrumentation.** Hekate makes its own spans in three places (the request middleware, the job runner and the media tool runner) rather than loading the auto-instrumentation packages, which patch modules as they load and would put query text, URLs and arguments into spans. The packages are pinned to one consistent release set, as the SDK's parts must match.
- **Nothing personal, nothing about the media.** Requests are named by their route pattern, jobs by their queue, media tools by their name with only a count of arguments. A failure's message and stack go with its span, as they already go to the log.
- **Logs as they are.** Each console line is also sent as a log record in its span's trace; stdout is unchanged, for log shippers that read it.
- **Joined to error references.** An error report keeps the trace id it happened in (decision 159's references), shown in Debug tools and in the copied report.
- **Expanding only:** a nullable column on error reports (decision 198).

## 207. Quotas and usage: limits an operator sets, refused under a lock, counted each hour

#175. A workspace may be held to a storage quota and a member limit, and what it uses is counted.

- **Set by an operator, seeded from the defaults.** Two nullable columns on the workspace; null is no limit, which is where every workspace starts on upgrade and every default starts. Plans (#177) will set them later; the workspace's own people never do.
- **Refused as it happens, never after.** An upload takes its room in the quota as it begins, and an invitation its place under the limit as it is sent, each checked under a lock on the workspace's row in the transaction that records it, so two at once cannot both slip under. Every invitation waiting takes a place, whichever way someone joins; accepting one discounts only its own. Comment attachments are checked before they are stored, and again under the lock once they are. Renditions of what was let in are always made.
- **Nothing removed.** Lowering a limit below what a workspace uses refuses only what would add to it.
- **Warned from 90%.** On the workspace's Storage settings, its Storage tab on the instance side, and the instance's Overview.
- **The month, counted hourly.** One row per workspace and month: storage now and at its peak, people and projects, and the plays and downloads the access log holds, counted on from where the last count stopped, so each hour reads only what is new. Each count stops fifteen minutes short of now: an access event's id is made before its transaction commits, and an event that lands late must not fall behind a count that has passed it. A month just ended is counted to its end first. For billing (#177, #179) to read.
- **Expanding only:** new nullable columns and a new table (decision 198).

## 208. Workspace subdomains: one session for every host, each host held to its workspace

#176. With `WORKSPACE_SUBDOMAINS` on, each workspace also answers at its own name under `PUBLIC_URL`'s host.

- **The host picks the workspace.** A request to `endless-hall.review.example` is held to Endless Hall: a member of it acts there whatever their session's active workspace was, and anyone else is refused (403, `not_member_here`), which the web app follows to the root. A guest's session is likewise its own workspace's only. A name under the root that is no workspace's answers 404.
- **One session, the root's cookie.** The session cookie is set for the root's domain, so one sign-in reaches every workspace someone belongs to, as Slack does with its workspace subdomains. A cookie per host would mean signing in at each, or handing sessions between hosts; the host check above keeps each workspace to its own members either way. The guest cookie stays per host. The browser sends the root's cookie to every name under the root, so the instance needs a host of its own with no other service beneath it; the setting and the install page say so.
- **The root keeps the instance.** The instance's pages and the sign-in are at the root; a signed-in member opening the root is sent to their active workspace's host, and an instance page opened at a workspace's host goes back to the root.
- **Sign-in from the host.** At a workspace's host the sign-in page names the workspace, and single sign-on and the directory need no workspace typed. Single sign-on's callbacks stay at the root, so a provider's settings do not change; a session made through a workspace's provider starts in that workspace.
- **Origins.** Every workspace's host is a trusted origin for Better Auth; passkeys stay bound to the root's name, and their origin list answers yes for any workspace host under it.
- **Certificates.** Caddy's on-demand TLS gets one per name as it is first opened, asking the app first (`/api/tls/ask`), so the stock image serves it without a DNS provider module; Kubernetes uses a wildcard certificate from a DNS-01 solver.
- **Names kept.** `www`, `api`, `mail`, `instance` and a few more can be no workspace's.
- **Links in mail stay at the root,** opening in the person's active workspace; links naming their workspace's host can follow (they did: decision 222).

## 209. Plans and entitlements: limits copied to the workspace, features asked of one function

#177. A plan is what a workspace may be on: a price, limits on storage, members and projects, the features it includes (single sign-on, the directory, forensic marking, a named operator) and the support severities it unlocks.

- **Global plans, one row per workspace.** `plans` belongs to the instance; `workspace_plans` holds the workspace's plan, its status (trial, active, past due, cancelled) and its trial and renewal dates.
- **Limits are copied, not looked up.** Putting a workspace on a plan, or changing the plan, writes its limits to the workspace's own columns, where the checks of decision 207 already read them under their lock. Nothing in the hot path learns about plans, and an operator can still give one workspace its own limits afterwards. Back on the internal plan, its limits are cleared. The project limit is a third such column, checked under the same lock when a project is made or moved in.
- **Features through `entitled`.** One pure function in `packages/shared` answers whether a plan includes a feature; every gated route asks it (through `assertEntitled`, 403 `not_entitled`) and every gated screen reads the answer the route gives. Turning single sign-on or the directory on is refused without it, and so is signing in through them, so a plan that drops one stops it at once; turning one off is always allowed.
- **Without billing, the internal plan.** With `HEKATE_BILLING` off, every workspace is on a plan that includes everything, whatever was assigned while billing was on, and no workspace can be put on a plan. The limits it holds are its own, as decision 207 has them: none unless an operator set some, or a plan copied them before billing was turned off, which an operator then changes per workspace. Plans can still be kept, ready for when it is turned on.
- **Retired, not deleted.** A retired plan is hidden and no workspace is put on it again; those already on it stay until moved, so their price and limits never change under them.
- **Operators only.** Plans and a workspace's plan are changed on the instance side, each as an instance event; a workspace's members read their own plan at `/api/organisation/plan`.
- **Expanding only:** two new tables and a nullable column (decision 198).

## 210. The billing gateway: Stripe behind one interface, webhooks taken once, dunning that deletes nothing

#178. With `HEKATE_BILLING=stripe`, workspaces pay for their plans through Stripe.

- **One interface, facts in Hekate's terms.** A gateway makes customers, checks out, changes a plan, opens its portal and reads its own webhooks into facts (a checkout, a subscription, an invoice, a payout). What a fact means for the workspace is the same for every gateway, so Paddle can follow behind the interface without touching plans, invoices or dunning.
- **Stripe's API by hand.** The few calls Hekate makes are written out against a pinned API version (`2025-09-30.clover`) rather than taken from Stripe's library. The version cannot move under it, nothing is added to the image, and tests stand in for the network with a function. The signature on each webhook is checked as Stripe documents it, any `v1` signature holding, within five minutes.
- **No prices to copy.** Each subscription carries its plan's price; the product it is sold against has an id Hekate chooses (`hekate_<plan id>`), made the first time and renamed with the plan. Nothing about a plan is set up in Stripe first. A plan by agreement (no price) is never sold through the gateway.
- **First Checkout, then in place.** The first payment goes through Stripe Checkout; once a workspace has a subscription, a change of plan is made on it, pro-rated, so a workspace never ends up with two. Only one checkout is open at a time: asked again for the same plan, the open one is given back, and another plan closes it first (refused while one has just been paid). The customer is made once (Stripe's idempotency key), and the portal handles the card.
- **The customer names the workspace.** An event is tied to a workspace by the gateway and customer it names, through `gateway_customers`, a global table kept for that one lookup; everything after is asked of that workspace. A checkout whose reference names another workspace than its customer's changes nothing.
- **Taken once, kept.** Every event is written to `gateway_events` before anything is done with it, keyed by Stripe's id, and taken under a lock on that row, so a second delivery, at once or later, answers as the first did, and one that failed part-way is tried again on its next. The log outlives the workspace, and a payout belongs to none, so the table is global (only operators read it).
- **Order does not matter.** Stripe does not send events in order, and their times are to the second. A subscription's event is read against the subscription as Stripe holds it now, fetched with the workspace's account locked, so the newest state always wins. An invoice's status never moves back (draft, open, uncollectible, then paid or void).
- **Dunning.** Counted from the first failed payment of the workspace's own subscription (not an invoice made by hand in Stripe, nor an old subscription's): the owners are warned at 7 and 14 days, and the workspace is suspended at 21, by an hourly job that claims each step in the database first so it is taken once. Telling the owners is kept apart from the step, and tried again each hour until the mail goes. It runs only while the gateway that would settle it is on. A payment ends it and reopens a workspace dunning suspended; one an operator suspended or set to be deleted stays as it is. Nothing is deleted for want of payment.
- **Test mode only, for now.** `STRIPE_SECRET_KEY` takes `sk_test_` and `rk_test_` keys; live keys come with the hosted offering. The test the issue asks for (a test clock driving a trial to conversion and a failed payment to suspension) runs against Stripe's test mode where `STRIPE_TEST_SECRET_KEY` is set, reading the events back from Stripe since a test cannot receive webhooks.
- **Expanding only:** four new tables (decision 198).

## 211. The Gateway log, events taken again, and invoices by hand

#178, the second part.

- **Every event read against now.** An invoice's event, like a subscription's (decision 210), is read against the invoice as the gateway holds it now. Events can then be taken again from Debug tools without risk: the stored event says which invoice or subscription, and the gateway says where it stands, so an old failure taken again after a payment owes nothing.
- **The Gateway log** is `gateway_events` as a log kind, shown only with billing on. It is kept for good rather than trimmed with the other logs: it is the instance's record of what it was paid, and it grows by a handful of rows a workspace a month.
- **Invoices by hand** are for plans by agreement: an operator makes one (amount, currency, what it is for, its period and due date), numbered in the instance's own series under a lock (`HK-0001`), and marks it paid or void by hand. They are not sent from Hekate and dunning does not chase them: the operator who agreed the plan does. Only an open invoice made by hand can be settled by hand; a gateway's are the gateway's.
- **`recordInstanceEvent`** moves to its own module, so billing and the instance's routes can both use it without importing each other.
- **Expanding only:** a nullable column (decision 198).

## 212. Promo codes: a use held while its checkout is open

#178, the last part.

- **The instance's codes, in Hekate.** A code is a percentage or an amount off (an amount in one currency), for some months or for as long as the subscription lasts, for the first so many, until a date, on some plans or any. They are kept in Hekate rather than as Stripe's promotion codes, so a second gateway can honour the same codes and the checks are one pure function (`promoRefusal`).
- **A coupon per set of terms.** At checkout, or on a subscription changed in place, the code becomes a Stripe coupon whose id carries the code's terms. Stripe's coupons cannot change, so changing a code's terms makes a new coupon for its uses from then on, and subscriptions that have the old one keep it.
- **Held, taken, given back.** A use is counted when the checkout is made, under a lock on the code's row, so the last use cannot go to two workspaces at once. It is given back when that checkout is closed for another (the same plan and code get the same checkout back) or lapses unpaid (`checkout.session.expired`), and taken once the checkout is paid. Applied to a subscription at once, it is taken there and then.
- **Who used it** is kept per use (`promo_redemptions`) with its workspace and plan, read across workspaces by the operators: a global table, like the gateway's log.
- **Ending** a code stops new uses; nothing given is taken back.
- **Expanding only:** two new tables and a nullable column (decision 198).

## 213. The workspace's Billing settings, and paying while suspended

#179. With billing on, the workspace's settings gain a Billing section, as the prototype has it.

- **Admins read, owners pay.** Admins see the plan, the card and billing details, the month's usage and the invoices. Owners alone change the plan, open the gateway's portal and cancel, as the server already enforced (#178).
- **The gateway's details, read live.** The card and the billing details (name, address, VAT number) are Stripe's: they are read from it as the page opens, and changed in its customer portal, never copied into Hekate. If Stripe can't be reached, the page still shows, without them.
- **Cancelling at the end of the period.** Cancelling sets the subscription to end when its period does. The webhook records the day it ends (`workspace_plans.cancels_at`), and until then it can be kept. There is no free plan to fall to, so the prototype's "drops to Solo" became what actually happens: nothing more is charged, and nothing is deleted.
- **Paying while suspended.** A workspace dunning suspended (and only one dunning suspended) still lets its owners read what is owed and open the portal: two paths, still behind the session, second-factor and single sign-on checks. The suspended page tells them it was a payment, not the operators, and offers to pay. A workspace an operator suspended stays shut, billing included.
- **Tabs scroll** sideways where they are wider than the page, which the Logs page's sixth tab made true on a phone.
- **Expanding only:** two nullable columns (decision 198).

## 214. The instance's Billing section: overview, plans and promo codes

#180, the first part. With billing on, the instance side gains a Billing section, as the prototype has it; its Gateways and Reports tabs follow.

- **One overview read.** The tiles and the workspaces are one read across workspaces, for operators only: each workspace's plan, its subscription, when it first owed and its oldest invoice past due. Revenue is the list price of the plans workspaces pay for, a yearly price as a twelfth, by currency; it is what the plans are worth, not what was collected, which the Reports tab will show. The next payout is asked of Stripe as the page opens, and the page shows without it.
- **Waiving voids.** Waiving what a workspace owes voids its open invoices: at Stripe first, so nothing can collect them, then those by hand. Voiding (rather than marking uncollectible) settles them under either of Stripe's ways of deciding a subscription's status, so the subscription returns to active and its webhook agrees with what Hekate did. Dunning ends and a workspace it suspended opens again; one an operator suspended does not.
- **An operator's plan change reaches the gateway.** Putting a workspace that pays through Stripe on another plan moves its subscription too, so Stripe bills what the workspace is on and the next webhook does not move it back. It cannot be put on a plan by agreement, or none, while that subscription runs.
- **No new tables.** The plans and codes keep their routes from #177 and #178; the overview and waiving are reads and writes of what is there.

## 215. Billing's rules are settings; the Gateways tab

#180, the second part.

- **Rules in the instance's settings.** The currency, dunning's three days and a trial's length are kept with the instance's other settings (#160), read as the server runs, so a change needs no restart and is an instance event. A save from a client that leaves them out keeps them as stored. Dunning's days must each come after the one before; stored ones that do not are read as the defaults, never half-applied.
- **Dunning reads its days each run.** A workspace already owing moves to new days at the next run. A step already taken is never taken again, so shortening the days cannot warn an owner twice, and lengthening them cannot reopen a workspace already suspended (paying, or an operator's waiver, does that).
- **The reason will stop naming a day, in two releases.** A workspace dunning suspended is told "21 days overdue"; with the days an operator's to set, the reason should say only that a payment is overdue. The reason is also how a workspace dunning suspended is told apart, so changing the words it is written with is a contracting change (decision 198): this release recognises both and still writes the old, and the next writes the new. The suspended page and the mail already say only that a payment is overdue.
- **The currency is a default, not a conversion.** It is what new plans and amounts off start in. Each plan keeps its own currency, and Stripe charges in it.
- **Trials have a length before they have a sign-up.** Open sign-up with a trial is #184's; the length is set here so the rule lives with the others.
- **The Gateways tab reads, it does not connect.** Stripe's account, its webhook's last event and failures, and Stripe Tax are shown; the keys stay in the environment. Paddle is not built yet, and invoices by hand cannot be turned off (a plan by agreement would have no way to be billed), so the prototype's Connect button and switch became plain rows.

## 216. Billing reports: list price, collected, and recognised by days

#180, the last part.

- **Two kinds of figure, said apart.** MRR, ARR, the average and the by-plan split are what the plans paying workspaces are on are worth, at list price: they move the moment a plan changes, with no history to keep. "Collected" is what invoices actually paid, month by month. Discounts, credits and failed payments show in the second, not the first. A plan by agreement has no list price, so it counts at a twelfth of what its workspaces paid by hand in the last year.
- **One currency.** The figures are in the instance's currency (decision 215). Plans priced in another are left out and named, rather than converted at a rate Hekate would have to choose.
- **Churn without history.** Churn is the plans that ended in the last 90 days against those plus the ones paying now. A workspace's plan records when it was cancelled; nothing else is kept for it.
- **Recognition by days.** The recognition export spreads each paid invoice over its period by days, shares rounded down and the remainder given to the last month, so a month's shares add up to what was paid. Invoices do not record their plan, so the export names the plan the workspace is on now.
- **Exports are recorded first**, as the logs' exports are (#157), and streamed.

## 217. The support module: tickets in the app, deflection first, the plan's severities

#181. With `HEKATE_SUPPORT=app`, a workspace asks the people who run the instance in tickets; with `link` or `mail` the help menu opens their own desk or mailbox (`HEKATE_SUPPORT_ADDRESS`); with `off`, only the documentation. The help menu and the workspace's Support settings learn which from `/api/me`.

- **Three tenant tables.** `tickets` (a reference, subject, type, severity, state, who asked and their name as it was, the error reference, the file or review asked from and whether the operators may open it, when it was first answered and resolved), `ticket_messages` (the thread: the workspace, an operator, or Hekate's own notes) and `ticket_attachments`. A ticket stays with its workspace when a project moves (decision 135): it is the workspace's whatever it was about.
- **A reference made on sending**, `T-7F3A-2C`: three random bytes, unique across the instance so an operator can find it, and unlike an error's `HK-`.
- **Who reads what.** Any member may ask. Each reads their own tickets; owners and admins read every one the workspace sent, and answer for it. Another workspace's ticket, and another member's to a member, is not found, never refused, so its existence is not confirmed.
- **Severity held to the plan.** Low and Normal on every plan; High, Urgent and Emergency as the plan allows (`severityAllowed`, decision 209), refused with 403 `severity_not_entitled`. The page says which plan unlocks the rest, from the plans on offer. Without billing, the internal plan allows every severity.
- **States.** Waiting (on support: a new ticket, or the workspace replied), answered (waiting on the workspace), open (being looked into) and resolved. One pure function moves a ticket on a reply; the workspace marks it resolved, and a reply to a resolved ticket opens it again.
- **Deflection first.** New ticket shows a search of the written answers, three common ones and the status page before the form. The answers are pages of the documentation site; the search runs over their titles and words in the browser, since the site's own search cannot be linked to.
- **Error references attached automatically**, but only the workspace's own: the last failure the person saw there in the past day is offered, ticked, and every reference quoted in the subject or message is attached where it happened in the workspace (or to them, outside any). Another workspace's is never attached, nor its existence confirmed. Hekate's note in the thread names the reference, the route and the time, never the failure's message.
- **Attachments**: up to five a message, 10 MB each, encrypted like any blob. They come down as the answer to a request in the session, never as an address, each in the access log first (`support.attachment_download`). They count towards the workspace's storage but are not refused for its quota, so a workspace at its limit can still ask for more.
- **Mail on each reply** (#120): the desk hears of each ticket and each reply from the workspace; whoever asked hears of each answer. Sent after the change commits, and never in its way.
- **Expanding only:** three tenant tables, one global table (decision 218) and an access log action (decision 198).

## 218. The support desk: every workspace's tickets, read by the operators alone

#182. The instance's Support section, shown to operators with tickets in the app.

- **Across workspaces, for operators only.** The list, its figures and a ticket are read across workspaces behind `operatorsOnly`, the reads marked as such; a workspace's owner who does not operate the instance never sees it. Filters by state (open, waiting on you, all), workspace and severity, and a search over subject, reference, workspace and who asked.
- **Beside the thread**: who asked (their role in the workspace and when they were last seen, from their sessions) and the workspace (its address, plan and open reviews), each opening its page on the instance side; the error reference, looked up in place; the item in question; the severity.
- **A state select and canned replies.** The state is set at once, or goes with the reply being written. Canned replies are the instance's (`canned_replies`, global: the desk is the instance's and a reply names no workspace); choosing one puts its words in the reply box, to change before sending.
- **The desk's own settings** live in the instance settings (decision 178) as their own part, saved from the desk alone so the Settings page never overwrites them: a status page over `HEKATE_STATUS_URL`, and the operator on call, who hears of each new ticket and reply first (with nobody on call, every operator does), an Emergency marked as one in the subject. Severities by plan stay with the plans, under Billing.
- **Recorded.** Each reply, change of state and change to the desk is an instance event; an operator's download of an attachment is in the workspace's access log under their name.
- **No incidents yet.** The prototype's "Post an incident" and "no incidents in 41 days" need an incident log Hekate does not keep; the desk links the status page instead, and the tile says whether one is linked. Decision 227 adds the incident log, and the instance's own page.

## 219. Registration: operators only, anyone invited, or anyone

#184. Who may make a workspace is an instance setting: operators only (the default, and how every instance worked before), anyone an operator invites, or anyone who confirms their email. Joining a workspace is unchanged: its own invitations work whatever registration says.

- **One choice, not two.** The prototype had the three-way choice and a switch, "Allow sign-up without an invitation", which said the same as "Anyone". The switch is gone; the prototype changed with it. "Anyone invited" means an operator's invitation to make a workspace of one's own; an operator who wants to name the workspace and its subdomain still makes it on the Workspaces page, as before.
- **A setting, not a column.** Registration lives with the instance's other settings (#160), read as the server runs; a save from a client that leaves it out keeps it as stored, and anything unknown reads as the defaults, so never as open.
- **The link confirms the email.** Open sign-up asks for the email first and mails a link; the form to name the workspace and make the account is behind it, so an account is never made for an address nobody holds, and no account waits unconfirmed. An operator's invitation is the same link, lasting a week instead of a day. Asking says the same whether or not the address has an account; one that has sees, at the link, that it should sign in, then names the workspace alone.
- **No new table.** The links are one-off verifications, kept in Better Auth's own table for them (as its links to confirm an email are), the token only as a hash. Using one removes it in the transaction that makes the workspace, so it works once; a failure after the account was made removes the account, so the link stays a first try.
- **Closed means closed.** The mode is checked when the link is used, not only when it was sent: turning registration to operators only stops every link still waiting, and an open link asked for before it became invite-only no longer works.
- **The trial.** With billing on, a workspace that signs up starts a trial of the length billing's rules set (decision 215), on the first plan the operators offer (priced, not hidden, not retired, in their order); choosing a plan through Checkout carries it over (decision 210). Without such a plan it is on the internal plan. What happens when a trial ends unconverted is billing's to decide, not registration's.
- **Abuse limits** are counted in PostgreSQL, like the other limits (#172): links asked for by address and by email, workspaces made by address and by email. No CAPTCHA: the email link already costs a script a mailbox per workspace.

## 220. A workspace's export: everything, encrypted with age to a key shown once; its deletion: two owners, then the operators' purge

#186. A workspace's owners, and the instance's operators, can take away everything it holds and delete it.

- **Everything, in one archive.** Every tenant table's rows as JSON (a file per table, or per 5,000 rows), the members' names and addresses, and every file, comment attachment and caption file as it was uploaded, with a manifest of paths, sizes and checksums. Derivatives (renditions, pages, peaks, spectrograms) are left out: they are made again from the originals. So are wrapped keys, sealed secrets, the hashes of links and codes, live sessions and downloads, and uploads under way; a test refuses a new column whose name looks like a secret unless it is left out or said not to be one.
- **age, to a key shown once.** The archive is a pax tar file encrypted with age to an X25519 recipient: the person's own public key, or one made for the export whose secret is in the response that starts it and nowhere else, so the server cannot open what it made and nothing it keeps can. age rather than an encrypted ZIP: one well-specified format, tools on every platform, and a stream that needs no index at the end. It is written here from Node's own primitives (X25519, HKDF, HMAC, ChaCha20-Poly1305) rather than taken from a library, as tar is, so nothing is added to the image; tests open it with the `age` command and `tar` where they are installed.
- **Never plaintext at rest.** Records and files stream into the tar, the tar into age, age into storage as ordinary encrypted blobs. Each part of the archive is at most 64 GiB, within what an S3 backend takes as one object, recorded on the export as it is stored so reconciliation leaves it alone; the download sends the parts one after another as one file.
- **The protected path.** A one-time link for the session that asks, for five minutes, as with a file's downloads (decision 056), each download in the access log before its first byte; the request too. Not watermarked: it is the workspace's own material going back to it, which is why only its owners (and operators, marked as operators in its log and in the instance's events) may ask for it.
- **A week, one at a time.** An archive is removed seven days after it is made by the hourly purge, which also fails an export whose job was lost. It counts against the storage quota while it is kept.
- **Deleting takes two owners,** exactly as a project's deletion does (#240, the same rules in `deletionProblem`): one asks, another confirms by typing the workspace's name, a sole owner a day after asking. Confirmed, the workspace is suspended with a reason of its own, which lets its owners, and only them, still reach its deletion (to keep it, which opens it again) and its exports; everyone else meets the suspended page, which says when it goes. It then waits the thirty days an operator's deletion waits and is purged by the same job. A subscription it pays for is cancelled at the end of the period paid for.
- **The access log goes with it.** The issue asks for the log to be kept or purged by the audit purge setting (decision 020): the purge removes it, inside the transaction that turns that setting on, as it did for an operator's deletion. Keeping it past its workspace would need its rows to outlive the workspace's foreign key and a place to read them; the owners' export holds the whole log, and the instance's events keep that the workspace was purged. The purge now also forgets the workspace's keys held in memory.
- **Expanding only:** a new table, two nullable columns and new access log actions (decision 198). The requester is a plain account id, not a foreign key, which would be a constraint added to a table the release before uses.

## 221. API tokens: one workspace each, scopes asked of one function, service tokens with an account of their own

#198, the first part. Members and workspaces reach the API with tokens; OAuth clients and connected apps follow in their own issue.

- **Per workspace.** A token belongs to the workspace it was made in, and reaches nothing else; a member makes one for another workspace from there. Account settings already keep notifications per workspace, and a token that spanned workspaces would need a second way to choose one per call.
- **Scopes from the prototype**, eight of them. Which a call needs is `scopeFor(method, path)` in `packages/shared`, a pure function the public API (#197) and the MCP endpoint (#199) will ask too. It names what may be reached and refuses everything else, so a route added later is closed to tokens until someone gives it a scope. Writing a kind includes reading it.
- **Checked before any route.** One middleware takes `Authorization: Bearer hk_…`, finds the token by its hash, holds it to its workspace (and to the host's workspace, #176), checks the scope and the token's own rate limit, and only then lets the request on, where the session middlewares take who it is from the token. A token is judged on itself alone: a session cookie in the same request is ignored, so a script cannot borrow a browser's reach.
- **A member's token is the member.** It acts with their role and project roles as they are at each call, so it can do no more than they can. The second factor and single sign-on a workspace asks for are asked of the session that makes the token; leaving the workspace, or deleting the account, revokes the member's tokens.
- **A service token has an account of its own.** Acting as the workspace means what it makes needs an author that is no person. Each service token gets a user row (`svc_<token id>`, at an address under `service-token.invalid`) that belongs to no workspace, has no way to sign in and is named after the token; it acts with an admin's reach, within its scopes. Owners and admins alone make one.
- **Hashed, shown once, kept when revoked.** Only a hash is stored: scrypt at a low cost (about a millisecond) with one salt for every token, so a token is found by its hash. A token is 256 random bits, so a high cost would protect nothing and slow every call; a password hash is used all the same, so no tool mistakes it for a weak one. A revoked token's row stays, so the API log and the access log can still name it.
- **The API log** is a new log kind (`api_requests`): each call with its path but never its query or body, its answer and its time, written after the answer, refusals included, and trimmed as the instance's settings say (30 days by default). Operators read every workspace's; admins read their own in the workspace's API settings.
- **Not yet:** OAuth clients (confidential, or public with PKCE), the authorisation screen, connected apps and the OAuth conformance check, all in the prototype, follow in a separate issue.
- **Expanding only:** two new tables and three audit actions (decision 198).

## 222. Links sent out at the workspace's own host

#437. With workspace subdomains on (decision 208), a link sent out about a workspace opens at that workspace's address (`https://<slug>.<root>`), so a notification about another workspace someone belongs to opens there, not in whichever one they were last in.

- **One rule, `linkBase`.** The workspace's address with subdomains on, `PUBLIC_URL` otherwise; the senders carry `PUBLIC_URL` and the subdomains together (`LinkRoots`) in place of the bare address, and read the workspace's slug as they send, so a renamed subdomain is followed at once.
- **What it covers:** notifications at once and in digests, invitations (a workspace's and the instance's to a new workspace's owner), review requests and shares, guests' links and reminders, upload requests (the link shown once as well as the one mailed), billing's mail and the gateway's return addresses, the email templates' samples, and channels' and webhooks' links.
- **Guests and visitors too.** A guest's cookie is per host (decision 208) and each guest link is a fresh one, so a guest at the workspace's host simply has their session there; a guest of two workspaces keeps both. Upload links need no cookie. Links already sent at the root keep working there.
- **An invitation is for someone not yet a member,** whom a workspace's host would refuse. Someone signed in who opens one there is taken to the same invitation at the root, which works as before; someone signed out stays, and signs in or makes their account on a page that names the workspace.
- **The account's own mail stays at the root:** a new password and a changed address belong to no workspace.

## 223. Upload requests to a track: each file its next version, once measured

#425, following decision 199. An upload request can send its files to a track instead of a folder: *Into* offers the project's tracks under "As the next version of", as the prototype now shows.

- **After the upload, not part of it.** A version is made only from a measured file, so the file arrives first, beside the track (in the track's folder as it is when the file starts), and the requester is told as now. The measuring job then makes it the track's next version, before the workflows look at what was measured; a file that is never measured (a document) becomes one as it arrives. A failure leaves the file where it arrived.
- **One file, one version.** Each file becomes a version of its own, its one variant named from the file, numbered as each is ready. Files sent together cannot be told from files sent days apart, and guessing which belong together (or which is primary) would be wrong more often than a member's own choice; a member can still add a file to a version as a variant. The file limit is how many versions the request takes.
- **As a member's version, without the member.** The version has no author (`created_by` null) and notes saying who sent it through which request; the previous version's threads are carried as the project says; alignment, the track's followers (and the requester) and the track's workflows hear of it as of any version.
- **The project's checks hold.** True peak and the edit decision list are checked as for a member's version, and nobody can let a visitor's file through: one they refuse stays beside the track, and the requester is told why. A file already in a version is left alone, so the job running again makes nothing twice.
- **Its own workspace and project only.** The track is checked against the request's project and workspace when the request is made, and again when the version is made.
- **Expanding only:** a nullable `upload_requests.track_id`, with no foreign key yet, as adding one to a table the release before knows contracts the schema (decision 198). Tracks are deleted only with their project, which takes its requests with it; the key can follow with the next contracting release.

## 224. The public API: `/api/v1` over the same routes, an OpenAPI document read from them, and versions that only grow

#197. Scripts reach Hekate with API tokens (decision 221); this makes what they reach a public API: versioned, described and documented.

- **One set of routes, two addresses.** `/api/v1/…` reaches the same handlers as `/api/…`: the router reads a version's path without its version (Hono's `getPath`), so nothing is written twice and every check, tenancy included, is the one the web app passes. The version answers only the calls `scopeFor` gives a scope (the calls a token may make, decision 221) and `404` for the rest, whoever asks, so the web app's own calls (accounts, settings, tokens, playback) are never part of it. A version the server does not answer is `404` too, before any token is judged. The unversioned `/api` stays the web app's, free to change in any release; a token still works there (the MCP endpoint, #199, may call through it), but nothing there is promised.
- **Versions only grow.** Within a version, a change may add a call, an optional field or parameter, a field in an answer, an error code, or take more values; clients are told to read leniently. Removing or renaming a call or a field, requiring one, taking fewer values, changing a call's scope, success status or media type: those wait for the next version. When v2 comes, v1 is answered beside it for at least two releases, its answers carrying `Deprecation` and `Sunset` (RFC 9745, RFC 8594), and its document marking what goes.
- **The promise is checked, not hoped for.** The document is committed (`docs/openapi.json`, written by `pnpm --filter @hekate/server api:reference`), and a test fails when it falls behind the routes. The script compares the new document with the committed one (`breakingChanges`: removed calls and parameters, narrowed request schemas, changed scopes, statuses and media types) and refuses to write a break unless run with `--breaking`, which is a new version's decision. So a pull request that changes the API shows it as a diff of that file, and one that breaks it cannot do so quietly.
- **Generated from the routes, not beside them.** No OpenAPI library: `hono-openapi` and `@hono/zod-openapi` would mean rewriting each route or adding a second description and their own dependencies, and zod 4 already writes JSON Schema. Each route's validator is ours (`apps/server/src/openapi/validator.ts`, `@hono/zod-validator` with the same types), which remembers its target and schema; the generator walks Hono's route table, keeps the routes `scopeFor` admits, and reads each one's path parameters, query, headers and body from those schemas, and its success status from its handler. Only the words are written by hand (`operations.ts`: a summary for every call, a description and examples for the common ones), and a test fails on a call without words or words without a call.
- **Examples that are true.** Every body has an example, hand-written or made from its schema, and a test holds each to the route's own zod schema. Answers have examples for the calls scripts make most; a test makes those calls through `/api/v1` with a token and holds each answer to its example's shape and documented status. Answers have no schemas yet: their types live in TypeScript, not zod, and a schema written beside them would drift.
- **One error shape, validation included.** A request that fails its zod schema was answered in zod's own shape (`{ success: false, error }`); it now answers `400` with `{ error: { code: "invalid_request", message, issues: [{ path, message }] } }` like every other refusal, so the web app, which shows an error's message, now shows the first issue in words. The shape (`apiErrorBody`) and the codes a script may act on (`apiErrorCodes`) are in `packages/shared`, and a failure keeps its reference (#159).
- **Where it is.** Each instance serves its document to anyone at `/api/openapi.json` (and `/api/v1/openapi.json`), its own address filled in; it describes calls and holds no data, so it is deliberately public, and it is answered before the token check so a script may fetch it with its token in hand. The documentation site publishes the same document and an API reference page per group (Projects, Folders, Files and so on) written from it at build time, beside a guide (Use, The API). The API log keeps the path as asked, so it shows which version a call used.
- **Not yet:** answer schemas, pagination common to every list, and OAuth (decision 221's separate issue).

## 225. OAuth 2.1: our own authorisation server on API tokens, clients per workspace, PKCE for all

#447, the rest of #198 (decision 221's "not yet"). Workspaces register OAuth clients; members connect them on a consent screen and see them in Account, Connected apps.

- **Not Better Auth's provider.** Better Auth's OAuth provider is a separate package (`@better-auth/oauth-provider`, which needs a newer Better Auth than ours and adds `jose` and its JWT plugin with signing keys in the database). Its clients, consents and tokens live in tables of its own keyed by a free-text `referenceId`, with no `organisation_id` to hold to a workspace (they would have to join `globalTables`, against decision 005); its scopes are free strings rather than `scopeFor`'s; and its access tokens would be checked by it, not by the middleware that holds every API token to its workspace, its rate limit and the API log (decision 221). Fitting those would mean wrapping most of it. The authorisation code flow with PKCE, refresh and revocation is small and well specified, so Hekate's is its own: `apps/server/src/oauth.ts` and `routes/oauth.ts`, with OIDC (ID tokens, user info) left out, as nothing asks for it.
- **An access token is an API token.** Issued as `api_tokens` of a third kind, `oauth` (`hk_oat_`, an hour), with the connection it was issued under (`grant_id`), so `tokenAuth` takes it unchanged: the same scopes, the member's role at each call, limits and log. Refresh tokens (`hk_ort_`, 30 days, used once) and codes (`hk_oac_`, a minute, used once) have tables of their own, as neither may ever reach the API.
- **A client is its workspace's,** registered by owners and admins, and a connection (`oauth_grants`, one per client and member) reaches that workspace alone, as a token does (decision 221). The consent screen asks the member of that workspace whatever their active one is, holding them to what it asks of a sign-in; connected apps are listed per workspace, like tokens. Instance-wide ("official") clients are not built: the prototype's chip went with them.
- **PKCE for every client, S256 only,** as OAuth 2.1 asks; confidential clients also authenticate with their secret (Basic or the body), public clients with their id alone. Redirect addresses match exactly, but for the port at a loopback IP address (RFC 8252); only https, loopback http and an application's own scheme can be registered. A request with an unknown client or an unregistered address is shown, never redirected; every answer carries `iss` (RFC 9207).
- **Spent first, replay revokes.** A code is marked used before its verifier and address are checked, so neither can be guessed twice. A code or refresh token presented again revokes every token of its connection (the consent stays: the application asks for tokens, not for consent). A refresh may narrow the scopes of its access token, never widen them; a refusal spends nothing.
- **Consent remembered.** A member who already granted at least what is asked is sent straight back, unless `prompt=consent`. Granting more widens the connection; each new or widened connection is in the access log.
- **Ending ends at once.** Disconnecting, leaving the workspace, deleting the account or the client revokes the connection's tokens in the same transaction; a client losing scopes takes them from every connection and live token. Rotating the secret stops the old one at once and leaves issued tokens alone; deleting the client is the way to cut everything.
- **The scopes are chosen, not typed.** The prototype's client editor took a typed list of scopes; it now has the token dialog's checkboxes, so no scope is misspelt. Its client menu gained the dialogs for who has connected and a rotated secret, and its connected apps "What it may do"; the consent screen was added to it.
- **Housekeeping.** The daily purge removes codes a day after they ran out, refresh tokens once expired (a used one is kept till then, to tell a replay) and access tokens 30 days after they ended, the API log keeping their names.
- **Expanding only:** four new tables, a nullable `api_tokens.grant_id` (no foreign key, as adding one to a table the release before knows contracts the schema, decision 198), an index, and six access log actions. The release before reads `oauth` tokens as unknown and refuses them, so calls with them fail on an older replica until every replica runs this release.

## 226. Workspace branding: a logo drawn again and public, an accent held to the signal's rule

#185. A workspace may set a logo and an accent colour, shown on its sign-in page at its own address (#176), on every guest page and in every email sent for it. Hekate's own lockup stays in the member's shell.

- **A column beside the settings, not a setting.** Both are kept in one nullable `organisations.branding` (JSON, read through `storedBrandingOf`), set through their own routes (`/api/organisation/branding`) by owners and admins, never through a settings change: a logo is an upload and an accent is checked first. They would sit naturally among the settings, but the release before this one rewrites the settings whole on every change, which during a rolling upgrade would drop them, and with them the logo's only reference. Each change is a new access-log action, `organisation.branding`. The reconciliation counts a logo named there as used.
