- **An accent holds what the signal holds.** It stands in for `--hk-signal`, which is text as well as fill, so it must hold 4.5:1 against every surface text is set on in the dark theme (canvas, ground, pane, surface, raised, overlay; the ground is also a primary button's label). One colour cannot hold 4.5:1 on both a near-black and a near-white ground, so the accent is given for the dark theme, the brand's default, and refused there when it fails, saying by how much. The light theme takes a cut of it computed rather than chosen: the same chromaticity with its linear light scaled down, the lightest that holds 4.5:1 on every light surface, as the brand's own signal darkens from `#F0762E` to `#B53C0A`. An accent already legible there is kept.
- **Signal's themes only.** The accent replaces the signal in Signal dark and light (`data-brand` on the root, with both cuts as properties). Someone who chose Catppuccin, Dracula or another palette in Appearance chose its colours, and keeps them.
- **The logo is drawn again, and only that is kept.** PNG, JPEG or WebP, by their first bytes, up to 2 MB (the API's body limit). ffmpeg opens it with that one demuxer, from a file only, after ffprobe has refused anything past 8192 pixels a side; it is drawn again as a fresh RGBA PNG of at most 512 pixels a side with no metadata. Nothing of the upload survives but its pixels: no SVG (script), no chunks, nothing appended. The PNG is stored like any other object, encrypted with its own key, and counts towards the storage quota. A new logo replaces the old, whose bytes go.
- **The one public object.** Media never has a URL outside a session, but a logo is meant to be seen before anyone signs in, and in mail. It is served to anyone at `/api/branding/<workspace>/logo/<id>`, which answers only for the blob that workspace's branding names as its logo (nothing else stored can be reached by it), only as the PNG drawn above, with `default-src 'none'; sandbox`, `nosniff` and `Cross-Origin-Resource-Policy: cross-origin`, the one response allowed across origins, for mail clients. Its address changes with each logo, so it is cached for good.
- **Mail carries it at one address for everyone.** Every message for a workspace leads with its logo (named for the workspace, for clients that hold images back) and acts in its accent; the foot still says Hekate sent it. The image is the same for every recipient, so loading it says nothing about who opened what; Hekate still has no tracking. The mailer looks the branding up, so every route and job that sends for a workspace carries it without knowing; a lookup that fails sends the message unbranded. A guest's code page is told the workspace's name and branding with the code.
- **Not a plan feature.** Neither the issue nor the prototype gates it, so it is not one of the features `entitled` answers for; a plan can gate it later behind that one function.
- **Expanding only:** a new nullable column and a new access-log action (decision 198).

## 227. The status page: public, its services measured each minute, its incidents posted from the desk

#183, following decision 218, which linked a status page because Hekate kept no incident log. It keeps one now: operators post incidents from the instance's Support section, each with updates through to its resolution, and the instance serves its own page at `/status`.

- **Public, not signed in.** The page and its two reads (`/api/status`, `/api/status/now`) answer anyone, signed in or not. A status page matters most to whoever cannot sign in (single sign-on down, a guest whose link will not open, a member locked out), and signing in is itself one of the services it reports. What it shows is what anyone may know: the services by name, their state, their 90 days and the incidents' own words; never a workspace, a person (the desk names who wrote each update, the page never does), a version or a figure from System health. The readiness probe already says as much to anyone (decision 156's `/api/health/ready`). Each read goes through a cache of fifteen seconds in each process, forgotten where an incident changes, so a page left open by hundreds while something is down costs the database little; another replica follows within the fifteen seconds.
- **The services are System health's** (#156), in words anyone can read: App and API, Database, Storage, Processing (the worker) and Mail; mail only where it goes out, storage only where there is a backend. Each is as bad as the worse of what is measured now (a failing check is an outage, a worker that finished nothing in ten minutes is slow) and what its open incidents say.
- **Measured each minute, counted once.** Every replica checks the services as it samples its minute (decision 188) and adds that minute to the day's row (`service_days`: minutes checked, minutes down), with the last minute counted, so another replica's check of the same minute is not counted again. Days older than 100 are removed as rows are added. One small row a service a day, not a row a minute: the page draws days.
- **Uptime counts what is posted too.** A day's minutes down are the more of those measured failing and those an incident naming the service says were down: all of a major outage, three tenths of a partial one, none of slowness (as Statuspage weighs them). An outage that stops the instance measuring, the database down or every replica gone, leaves nothing measured; posted, after the fact if need be (an incident may begin and end in the past, up to the page's 90 days, never in the future), it counts. Unmeasured time outside any incident counts as up, as on any status page; days before the first measure are drawn as not measured and left out. The uptime is floored to two places and never printed as 100% with a minute down. All of it is one pure function in `packages/shared`.
- **When the database cannot be read**, the page still answers from the checks just made, without history, so it says something is down when that matters most.
- **Incidents.** A title, what it touches (one service or more), its impact (degraded performance, partial outage, major outage) and its state (investigating, identified, monitoring, resolved), with updates newest first. Resolving one sets its end; an update to a resolved incident opens it again. One posted by mistake can be removed, with its updates. Operators alone post (`operatorsOnly`), whatever the support module says; each post, update, resolution and removal is an instance event. Two operators updating at once take turns on the incident's row.
- **Where people look for help.** The help menu offers Status in every mode, with the state, and marks its button while an incident is open. The Support page lists each open incident (its impact, state and last update) above the form and under Help yourself, so it is read before a ticket is written. The desk's tile says Up, or how many are open, and how long since the last.
- **Its own page, or another.** Links go to the desk's address, else `HEKATE_STATUS_URL`, else the instance's own `/status`, which always shows what is posted here. Linking elsewhere stays the way to a page that survives the instance going down, which no page served by the instance can. The desk's settings now save with tickets off too, as the status page is every instance's; the Support section is therefore every operator's, holding the status page alone where tickets are answered elsewhere.
- **The prototype changes with it.** It gains the status page; the desk's "Post an incident" and "no incidents in 41 days" return, in a Status page section of their own beside the Desk, with the address as its last row; the help menu gains Status.
- **Not yet:** scheduled maintenance announced ahead, subscribing to a page by mail or feed, and components an operator defines (the proxy, the billing gateway).
- **Expanding only:** three new global tables (decision 198).

## 228. Bounce reports from Amazon SES, Postal, Plunk and Mailcoach

#432, following decision 205. The other providers the installer offers that report bounces are read the same way: only once what proves a report is the provider's holds, and only for addresses that bounced for good or complained.

- **Amazon SES through Amazon SNS.** Each SNS message is checked as SNS signs it: RSA over its fields in SNS's order, SHA-1 for signature version 1 and SHA-256 for 2, with the certificate at `SigningCertURL`, fetched only from `sns.<region>.amazonaws.com` (or `.com.cn`) over HTTPS and kept once fetched. The secret is the topic's ARN: anyone can make a topic, and SNS signs theirs too, so a message must name the topic given, checked before anything is fetched. A subscription is confirmed by visiting its `SubscribeURL`, on the same hosts, once its signature holds. SES's `Bounce` with `bounceType` Permanent and its `Complaint` are reports, except a complaint whose feedback type is `not-spam`; a configuration set's events (`eventType`) are read alike. Raw message delivery sends no signature, so it is refused.
- **Postal.** Its RSA signature over the body, `X-Postal-Signature-256` (SHA-256, Postal 3.2 and later), or `X-Postal-Signature` (SHA-1) where only that is sent, checked with the public key its DKIM record publishes (`p=`, or PEM). Postal does not class a bounce as hard or soft: a bounce message it links to what was sent (`MessageBounced`) is a report, and so is a message held because the address is on Postal's suppression list, which Postal puts an address on after repeated hard fails. Other holds (a send limit, a credential set to hold) say nothing of the address.
- **Plunk** signs nothing. A workflow on `email.bounce`, and one on `email.complaint`, sends its default body through a Webhook step with `Authorization: Bearer <secret>`, as Plunk's docs advise; the token is compared in constant time. `bounceType` Permanent is a bounce, and a `complainedAt` a complaint.
- **Mailcoach** signs the body with HMAC-SHA256 in its `Signature` header. `BounceRegisteredEvent` is a hard bounce; it sends no complaints by webhook, and its webhooks belong to email lists, so it reports bounces for its lists' subscribers.
- **A time where one is signed.** SNS and Postal sign the report's own time, kept on every retry, so a report may be an hour old (SNS stops retrying an endpoint within an hour; Postal within forty minutes); older is refused. Plunk and Mailcoach sign no time, as SparkPost does not.
- **Which provider.** Amazon SES is known by its regional SMTP endpoints, Plunk by its host, and Mailcoach by its API. Postal runs on the operator's own host, so `MAIL_BOUNCE_PROVIDER` names the instance's provider where its host cannot say (a self-hosted Plunk too). A workspace names its provider when it saves its mail.
- **No new dependency.** Node's crypto reads the certificate and checks each signature. Nothing in the database changes: the providers are names in the existing tables.

## 229. Credits, a code applied by an operator, and an invoice sent again

#445. Each workspace's row on the Billing overview gains the prototype's other three actions, each an instance event, offered only where they apply.

- **A code, as an owner would apply it.** An operator applies one of the instance's codes to a workspace's subscription through the same steps as an owner changing plan (decision 212): the code held under its lock, refused when it does not apply to the plan the workspace is on, the subscription given its coupon on that plan, and the use taken at once. The dialog offers the codes that apply now, rather than a field to type one, since the operators keep them. Without a subscription there is nothing to apply it to: the owners use it at checkout.
- **A credit is the gateway's balance, or a line on the next invoice by hand.** Through Stripe, a credit is a customer balance transaction (negative, in the currency the subscription is billed in, made once under an idempotency key that is the credit's own id), which Stripe takes off the next invoice it finalises. Hekate keeps a row of it (`billing_credits`) for the record, but Stripe's invoices already show it, so nothing more is done with it here. For a workspace invoiced by hand, the credit is held until the operator's next invoice by hand in its currency, which takes the held credits off, oldest first, as a line of its own (`invoices.credit`), with `amountDue` what is left. A credit larger than the invoice is split, the rest held for the next; an invoice covered in full is paid as it is made. The arithmetic is one pure function (`creditsAgainst`).
- **Sending again is Hekate's mail, not Stripe's.** Stripe's own sending (`POST /v1/invoices/:id/send`) mails the customer's one address, only for invoices collected by sending them rather than by charging the card (Hekate's subscriptions charge it), and sends nothing in test mode, the only mode Hekate takes (decision 210). So Hekate mails every owner the invoice's page and PDF, its links read afresh from Stripe as it is sent. Invoices by hand are sent outside Hekate (decision 211) and are not offered.
- **Only what applies.** A code needs a subscription on a priced plan with the gateway on; a credit needs the gateway for a workspace paying through it, or a plan for one invoiced by hand; sending needs an invoice from the gateway with a page. A workspace on a plan by agreement is offered a change of plan, a credit and, while it owes, waiving.
- **Expanding only:** a new table and a nullable column (decision 198).

## 230. Drawing a region by touch: a Draw toggle, and two taps for a region

#150, following decision 033, where a finger scrolls a document's page.

- **A toggle, not a long press.** The page's toolbar gains **Draw**, for whoever may comment. While it is on, the page takes a finger as a mouse or a pen (`touch-action: none` on the page alone), so a drag draws the region; off, a finger scrolls and zooms as before. A long press was the other way the issue offered; it was not taken, since a long press on a touch screen already means select or the context menu, it is not discoverable, and it takes a held finger to start every region. The toggle stays on until it is turned off, as the video's drawing tools and score marking's tools do, so a second attempt needs no second tap on it. A mouse or a pen draws by dragging whether or not Draw is on.
- **Two taps make a region.** With Draw on, a tap sets a corner (marked on the page) and a tap elsewhere its opposite corner, so drawing a region never needs a drag (WCAG 2.2, 2.5.7 Dragging Movements). The toggle is therefore shown on every device, not only on touch screens: a mouse user who cannot drag needs it as much. A tap too near the first corner moves it instead; Escape or another page lets it go. The arithmetic is one pure function (`regionFromPress`).
- **Documents and pictures.** The video's drawing tools already take a finger once opened (decision 137); score marking has its own tools. Neither changes.

## 231. The MCP endpoint: our own small JSON-RPC server, its tools made of public API calls

#199. Assistants that speak the Model Context Protocol reach Hekate at `/api/mcp`, with an API token or an OAuth access token (decisions 221 and 225), to read what waits on their member, read a review and its comments, comment, record a decision and read a QA report.

- **No SDK.** The official TypeScript SDK (`@modelcontextprotocol/sdk`) brings Express, CORS and rate-limit middleware, a JSON Schema validator and OAuth helpers, none of which Hekate would use beside Hono and its own authorisation server, and its Streamable HTTP transport is written around Node's request objects, with sessions held in memory unless run stateless. What a server needs here is small and well specified: four JSON-RPC methods (`initialize`, `ping`, `tools/list`, `tools/call`) over one POST. So it is written in `apps/server/src/mcp.ts` (the transport) and `mcp-tools.ts` (the tools), with the messages, tools and their inputs as zod schemas in `packages/shared` (`mcp.ts`), and each tool's input schema in `tools/list` written from its zod schema by the same function as the OpenAPI document's (decision 224). Nothing is added to the image.
- **Streamable HTTP, stateless.** One address; POST takes one message and answers a request with JSON, or with one event for a client that accepts only `text/event-stream`; a notification or a response is taken with `202`. GET and DELETE answer `405`: the server pushes nothing and keeps no session (`Mcp-Session-Id` is never issued), so any replica answers any message. Protocol versions `2025-11-25` and `2025-06-18`; `initialize` answers the one asked for if spoken, otherwise the newest, and a request whose `MCP-Protocol-Version` header names another is `400`. Batches, gone from the protocol since `2025-06-18`, are refused. A request whose `Origin` is not the instance's own is `403`, against DNS rebinding, as the transport asks.
- **A token, never a session.** The endpoint takes `Authorization: Bearer hk_…` and nothing else: a signed-in browser's cookie is not enough, so no page can make a member's browser call it. It is registered before the token middleware (which refuses a path no scope names) and checks the token itself; without one, or with one that does not work, it answers `401` with `WWW-Authenticate` naming its protected resource metadata (RFC 9728) at `/.well-known/oauth-protected-resource/api/mcp`, which names Hekate's own authorisation server and the scopes, so an OAuth client finds its way. The resource is the endpoint's address (and, at the root document, the instance's API as a whole): Hekate's tokens are for its API, of which the endpoint is part, and no other issuer's token is taken. The `resource` parameter (RFC 8707) is accepted and ignored by the authorisation server; dynamic client registration is not offered, as clients are registered by their workspace (decision 225).
- **Each tool is public API calls with the same token.** A tool makes its calls through the whole app, in-process, to `/api/v1/…` with the request's own `Authorization` (and the headers that say where it came from): so the token middleware judges every call (its scope by `scopeFor`, its workspace, a suspended workspace, the rate limit, the API log), and the routes make every check they make for a script (the member's role and project roles, review turns). Nothing is queried twice, a tool can never do more than the same token could through the API, and the API log shows each call a tool made by its path. `tools/list` shows only the tools the token's scopes allow, and a tool outside them is refused before any call is made; either way the API would refuse it.
- **No media, by allow-list.** What the API answers is reshaped field by field (`reviewOf`, `threadsOf`, `qaReportOf`), so only fields named there leave; a review's reviewers lose their addresses, a comment's attachments keep only name, type and size. A last pass drops any key that names an address or a secret (`url`, `link`, `playlist`, `token` and the like). There is no tool for downloads, playback or page renders, whatever the token holds; a test reads every tool's answer for addresses.
- **Errors an assistant can act on.** Unknown methods and tools are JSON-RPC errors; a refusal from the API (scope, not found, not your turn) or arguments that do not fit are tool errors (`isError`) carrying the API's words, status and code, so the assistant can say why or correct itself, as the `2025-11-25` revision asks of input errors. Answers carry both text and `structuredContent`.
- **Counted once.** A tool's calls are counted against the token's limit as they pass the middleware; the other messages (`initialize`, `tools/list`, `ping`) are counted here, against the same limit.
- **Not yet:** resources, prompts, sampling and server-sent notifications; tools beyond the issue's (search, feedback reviews' *submit*, triage); dynamic client registration and client ID metadata documents.

## 232. Instance settings: telemetry shown, forensic marking left to #32

#160, last part. What #160 asked for is built across decisions 178 and 184, usage limits (#175), workspace subdomains (#176) and registration (#184), except two rows.

- **Telemetry is read-only, from the environment.** The collector is `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT` (decision 206), read once at start: the SDK is loaded only when it is set, so a field that changed it while the server ran would either do nothing until a restart or load the SDK into a process that promised not to. Settings shows it under Address and mail beside the other values the environment sets, as "Off" when unset. The prototype's input became a read-only value, and so did its Address, as the product already had it.
- **Never a credential.** Only the address's scheme, host, port and path are shown (`collectorShown`): a user, password or query string in the address, where a collector's token might be, stays on the server. The headers (`OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_HEADERS`) are never shown.
- **Forensic marking waits for #32.** The prototype's "Forensic marking on" default for new workspaces needs the invisible marking itself, which is not built; a switch that marked nothing would tell an operator their material was traceable when it is not. It follows with #32, with the plans' `forensicMarking` feature.

## 233. A ticket's answer is a notification

#110, following decision 217 (#181). When the people who run the instance answer a ticket, whoever sent it is told through the notification system, under a kind of its own (`ticket`), rather than by a mail sent on the side.

- **In the inbox, and by mail as they choose.** The answer reaches their inbox and the bell, and by mail at once or in their digest, by default both, like every other kind; turning its mail off leaves the inbox. A ticket is a conversation the person started, so the mail stays on by default, as a reply does.
- **The workspace's own.** The notification belongs to the workspace the ticket came from, so it is seen only there, and only by its requester; the operator who answered is named as the actor, whether or not they belong to that workspace. Someone who has left the workspace is not told, as for any notification.
- **The mail says what the inbox says.** Its subject is the notification's line ("Alex Rivera answered your ticket T-7F3A-2C: Score page 7 will not render"), with the answer quoted, and it links to the ticket at the workspace's own address (decision 222).
- **The desk's own mail is unchanged:** operators are not workspace members, so a new ticket or a workspace's reply still reaches them by mail directly.
- **Expanding only:** a value added to `notification_kind`, which the release before never writes.

## 234. Listening together: a room holds where the leader is, sent over server-sent events and woken through PostgreSQL

#200. One member leads a listening room, and everyone who joins hears what they play, as they play it: play, pause, seeking, speed, and which file, version or variant. The prototype now shows it (Listen together, 3.4 in its inventory, which moves it in from the backlog).

- **A listening room, not a session.** *Session* is a recording session in the domain, so the word is *listening room*, with its *leader* and *listeners* (never followers: following a track is something else). Members only for now: guests reach material through their reviews, and a room spanning a review's items is a later step.
- **Only where the leader is.** A room holds a file, whether it plays, a position at a moment by the server's clock, and a speed; nothing else reaches a listener. Each listener's page plays that file through its own playback request, so their own permissions, watermark and access log apply, and no address, token or key passes from the leader to anyone. A listener who may not play the file sees the file page's own refusal.
- **Server-sent events on the member's session.** One stream per page in a room, from the same origin, with the session's cookie; no WebSocket, as the traffic is one way and the leader's moves are ordinary validated requests. The stream sends the room when it changes, says it is still there every fifteen seconds (which keeps proxies from closing it), and asks for `no-transform` and no buffering so a compressing proxy holds nothing back. API tokens never reach a room.
- **Every replica, through the database.** The room's state lives in PostgreSQL. A move wakes the streams on the replica that took it at once and is announced on the `hekate_listening` channel in the same transaction, which the other replicas hear and pass to their streams (as decision 188 does for settings); each stream then reads the room again, within its workspace. Streams read again every five seconds in any case, for replicas behind a transaction pooler where `LISTEN` cannot arrive, and end themselves when the process is told to stop, so the page reconnects to another.
- **Presence by heartbeat.** Each stream marks its person present as it opens and every fifteen seconds; a listener not heard from for 45 seconds has gone, and a room whose leader has not been heard from for 90 seconds has ended, so a leader who closes the tab does not leave a room open. Starting a room ends any other the member leads.
- **Only the leader moves it.** Moving or ending a room is refused to everyone else; a member of another workspace, or one who cannot see the project, does not find the room at all, and someone taken off the project is let go at the next heartbeat. The leader's file must be one they can see in the room's project.
- **Following, in the browser.** A listener's player is moved to where the leader is now (the position played on from the moment it was true, corrected for the browser's clock), seeking only when it has drifted by more than half a second. A listener who plays, pauses or seeks themselves stops following until they choose to again, and one whose browser waits for a tap is asked for it.
- **Expanding only:** two new tables, `listening_rooms` and `listening_room_people`. They are dropped when a project moves workspace (a room ends) and left out of a workspace's export, as neither means anything outside the instance.

## 235. Captions and transcripts: caption tracks, read and exported; no transcription

#201, building on decision 144. The issue asked for caption tracks uploaded or generated by a transcription job, shown in the player, searchable and exportable. The owner decided against transcription: there is no speech-to-text engine, local or hosted, and none is planned in this issue. Everything else is built.

- **No transcription.** A track comes only from a sidecar file someone adds. Running a speech model would mean shipping one (gigabytes in the image, a GPU or a slow CPU queue) or sending unreleased material to a hosted service, which `docs/security.md` rules out for the cargo Hekate protects. A workspace that transcribes elsewhere adds the result as a track like any other.
- **Videos and recordings.** A recording carries caption tracks too (narration, an interview, a spoken-word album), up to twelve per file. A document does not.
- **Language and kind.** Each track has a language tag (BCP 47, stored canonical: `fr-CA`) or none, and a kind: **subtitles** (the words) or **captions** for the deaf and hard of hearing (the words and the sounds), the distinction HTML's track kinds make. Both are read from the file where it says (WebVTT's `Language:` header, TTML's `xml:lang`, a tag or `sdh` in the name, as in `Titles.fr.sdh.vtt`) and can be set when it is added and changed later, with its name ("French (SDH)"), which follows them until it is typed over. Whoever may upload renames a track; each change is in the access log.
- **The Captions menu.** The player's captions leave the Frame menu for a menu of their own, as Frame.io, YouTube and every streaming player have: Off, or one track at a time, its kind beside its name. A video shows the line low in the picture, as before; a recording shows it under the waveform.
- **The transcript, beside the comments.** Where a file has a track, the panel beside the player switches between Comments and Transcript (as Frame.io's side panel does) rather than adding a pane: the panel keeps its place and size, and nothing is shown twice. The transcript lists the track the player shows (or another chosen there) with each cue's time, marks the one being said and keeps it in view as it plays, scrolling the list alone, never the page; a click goes to that moment; scrolling the list stops the following until **Follow playback** brings it back.
- **Search within the transcript,** as a phrase, case and accents set aside and line breaks read as spaces, with the matches marked and counted. Searching caption text across the workspace (the search page) is left for later: it needs an index of its own over a table that only grows by sidecars, and nobody has asked for it yet.
- **Export in any format.** A track exports as SubRip, WebVTT or TTML whichever it came as, written afresh from its cues by pure functions in `packages/shared` (no new dependency) and named as players look for it (`Opening titles - Cut 3.fr.sdh.vtt`). The sidecar as uploaded is still never sent back (decision 144). Exporting is a download of the workspace's material, so it needs the download permission at either level (or a review that grants one), is in the access log before it is sent, and needs the `downloads` scope for an API token. It is not watermarked: text carries no mark, and the access log says who took it. Only what is said and when is written: positions, styles and voices were never kept.
- **Stored as before.** The sidecar is an encrypted blob; its cues are in the database, scoped by workspace like every tenant row, and reach whoever may view the file, as comments do.
- **Expanding only:** two columns on `caption_tracks` (`language`, nullable; `kind`, with a default) and two access log actions (decision 198).

## 236. Moving between markers from the keyboard: Shift+up and Shift+down

#34 asks for marker shortcuts. The player's markers are the comment markers above the timeline and the quality checks' findings under it (decision 045 lists the other keys). Shift+↑ goes to the previous marker and Shift+↓ to the next, as DaVinci Resolve has them: the plain arrows already move the playhead, so the same keys with Shift move it further, and the bracket keys are taken (the sidebar). Both kinds of marker are one list in time (`lib/markers.ts`): the one the playhead is on (within 50 ms, as a seek lands a little off) is passed over, so each press moves on, and where there is no marker in that direction the player says so. The keys are on the map only while the file has a marker. Home and End go to the start and the end, as the prototype had them; like the arrows, they stay with a focused list, menu or tab row.

## 237. Keeping in step with a DAW: the browser reads MIDI timecode, scripts use the public API, and no companion client yet

#58, a spike following #51. The issue asked whether a client could keep a DAW's live state (take numbers, false starts, timecode) in step with Hekate, and send a new version with its edit decision list in one click; the spike also looked at keeping comments and playback in step with a DAW. The deliverable is this recommendation; nothing is built but two worked examples in Use, The API.

- **What Hekate already offers a companion.** Everything such a client would write is in the public API (decision 224) today, with no server change. `POST /api/v1/sessions/{id}/takes` starts a take with its own id, its own time, the recorder's number and the timecode in, made once however often it is sent (decision 154); `PATCH /api/v1/takes/{id}` stops it with the timecode out and counts false starts, field by field with history. An upload, then `POST /api/v1/tracks/{id}/versions` with `edlFileId` (or `PUT /api/v1/versions/{id}/edl` later), sends a version with its list, and a project that asks for one checks it (decisions 054 and 111); `409 version_measuring` says to wait while the file is measured. OAuth clients accept loopback redirects (decision 225, RFC 8252), so a desktop tool signs in as its member without a pasted token; the MCP endpoint (decision 231) is for assistants, not for this. The take log reads its session every two seconds (decision 150), so a take a tool writes is seen by everyone in the room. Comments already leave as each DAW's own marker list and come back from one (decisions 052, 106 and 107). Webhooks (`version.arrived` and the rest) tell a tool what happened. Listening rooms (decision 234) take a session's cookie, never a token, so a tool cannot lead one; a member's page can. What is missing is only the part that reads the DAW.
- **What each route gives.**

| Route | What it carries | Which DAWs | From a browser | Licence |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| MIDI Time Code | The position (hours to frames) and its rate, while rolling; a full-frame message on a locate | Pro Tools, Logic, Cubase and Nuendo, Reaper; every DAW that chases or drives tape | Yes: Web MIDI in Chrome, Edge and Firefox, not Safari; quarter frames need no SysEx permission, full frames do | A published standard; no code to take |
| MIDI Machine Control | Transport commands (play, stop, record strobe and exit, locate) from a DAW set as MMC master | Pro Tools, Logic (sent "whenever you operate the transport"); others unverified | Yes, with Web MIDI's SysEx permission | A published standard |
| OSC | Whatever the DAW is configured to send: Reaper sends time, play, record, markers and regions | Reaper; not Pro Tools or Logic | No: UDP needs a native process | Libraries under MIT and the like |
| Reaper's web interface | `TRANSPORT`: play state (5 is recording) and the position in seconds; `MARKER`: the markers | Reaper | Barely: plain HTTP on the LAN, so mixed content, CORS and Chrome's Local Network Access prompt (Chrome 141 on) stand between | Reaper's own; our scripts are ours |
| ReaScript | Everything inside Reaper (Lua, EEL, Python), run by Reaper | Reaper | No | Our scripts are ours |
| Pro Tools Scripting SDK (PTSL) | gRPC on the same computer: transport state and arming, timecode rate, memory locations (read; created since 2023.6), the timeline selection, bouncing a mix. Dropbox Replay's Pro Tools integration is built on it | Pro Tools 2022.12 on, every tier | No | Free, but each product needs a certificate Avid issues, may not share and may revoke; the SDK's software is distributed only compiled into the product, under an end-user licence forbidding reverse engineering. That cannot be part of AGPL-3.0 code |
| MIDI Remote API | ES5 scripts inside Cubase and Nuendo 12 on: the transport locator's time, and play, stop and record once bound to a control; a script can send MIDI | Cubase, Nuendo | Through a virtual MIDI port to Web MIDI | Steinberg's API; our scripts are ours |
| EUCON | Lets an application be controlled by Avid's surfaces: the wrong direction | Avid | No | Commercial licence |
| Ableton Link | Tempo, beat, phase and start and stop; by design no position or timecode | Live and Link apps | No: UDP multicast | GPLv2 or later (usable under AGPL-3.0), or proprietary |
| ReWire | Nothing now: Reason Studios ended it in 2020. "ReaWire" names nothing we could find; ReaRoute is Reaper's audio routing on Windows | None | No | None |

- **What no route gives.** Take numbers: timecode and machine control carry none, and a DAW's own counters live in clip and file names, which import already reads (decision 156). False starts: a producer's judgement, which stays a key. A recorder-numbered session therefore still takes its numbers typed, or matched on import, or from an adapter for a DAW that exposes them.
- **No native companion client now.** A desktop application means signing, updating and supporting three operating systems against every DAW's releases, for what a browser can mostly do already; and the one route that reads Pro Tools in depth cannot be shipped in Hekate's code.
- **First: follow the DAW in the browser** (its own issue). The take log and the player read timecode, and machine control where the DAW sends it, through Web MIDI. In the take log, a take's timecode in and out come from the DAW, and the take can start and stop as the DAW records (on the record strobe, or on rolling, as the session chooses); numbering stays Hekate's and the keys keep working. In the player, playback chases the DAW's position, less an offset where the file does not start where the DAW's timeline does, seeking only past half a second as a listening room does; so a leader whose player follows the DAW leads a room, with no new API. The decoder is a pure function in `packages/shared`. Nothing to install and nothing under another licence: macOS has its IAC bus; Windows needs a loopback port (loopMIDI today; Windows MIDI Services' own as it reaches every PC); two computers use network MIDI. Safari, which has no Web MIDI, keeps typing.
- **Second: one-step uploads by script.** The public API already does it, so Use, The API now shows a bounce sent with its edit decision list, and a take logged from a tool of your own. A small command-line tool (a watch folder pairing `Mix v3.wav` with `Mix v3.edl`, signed in through OAuth's loopback redirect, plain Node with no native modules) may follow as its own issue if people ask. Sequoia exports a CMX 3600 list; Pro Tools, as far as we could find, has no CMX export of its own (its session text is not one).
- **Adapters for one DAW, only when asked for, and outside the AGPL core where a licence demands it.** Reaper first (its web interface, OSC and ReaScript are open to anyone); Cubase and Nuendo through a MIDI Remote script feeding the browser; Logic through timecode and machine control alone; Pro Tools through its Scripting SDK only as a separately licensed tool, kept apart and talking to Hekate only through the public API, built by whoever holds Avid's certificate, and only after legal advice. Logic is not added to the comment export: it reads a MIDI file's markers only when the file is opened as a new project, and markers pasted into a project at another tempo move.
- **Not used:** Ableton Link, EUCON and ReWire, for the reasons in the table.
- **Not verified,** to be checked before building: which machine control commands Reaper, Cubase and Nuendo send while recording; whether Pyramix and Sequoia send timecode to a virtual port (Pyramix was left aside, with #233); Logic's control-surface feedback; the headers Reaper's web server sends; and whether the Pro Tools Scripting SDK pushes events or must be polled (polling assumed).

Sources: [Avid, Pro Tools Scripting SDK FAQ](https://kb.avid.com/pkb/articles/en_US/Knowledge/Pro-Tools-Scripting-SDK-FAQ); [its licence](https://my.avid.com/cpp/Content/doc/Avid%20Pro%20Tools%20Scripting%20SDK%20License%20Clickthrough.pdf); [its commands by release (py-ptsl)](https://py-ptsl.readthedocs.io/en/latest/implementation.html); [Avid, Dropbox Replay for Pro Tools](https://www.avid.com/resource-center/dropbox-replay-pro-tools); [Avid, EUCON SDK](https://developer.avid.com/eucon/); [Cockos, Reaper OSC](https://www.reaper.fm/sdk/osc/osc.php); [Reaper's web interface](https://github.com/ReaTeam/Doc/blob/master/web_interface_modding.md); [Steinberg, MIDI Remote API](https://steinbergmedia.github.io/midiremote_api_doc/); [Apple, Logic's MIDI synchronisation settings](https://support.apple.com/en-mo/guide/logicpro/lgcp72142361/10.7/mac/11.0); [Apple, standard MIDI files in Logic](https://support.apple.com/guide/logicpro/standard-midi-files-lgcpdf6a3851/mac); [Ableton Link](https://ableton.github.io/link/) and [its licence](https://github.com/Ableton/link/blob/master/LICENSE.md); [Ableton, ReWire](https://help.ableton.com/hc/en-us/articles/360019095140-ReWire-in-Live); [MDN, Web MIDI](https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/Web_MIDI_API) and [support](https://caniuse.com/midi); [MIDI Time Code](https://midi.community/docs/timing/); [Chrome, Local Network Access](https://developer.chrome.com/blog/local-network-access); [Windows MIDI Services](https://microsoft.github.io/MIDI/); [Sequoia's edit lists](https://cdn.borisfx.com/borisfx/Documentation/sequoia-2025/en/Content/Schnittliste%20(_.edl).htm); [Video Sync, Pro Tools by MTC and MMC](https://non-lethal-applications.com/knowledge-base/VideoSync6/14_DAW%20Sync%20Option%204).

## 238. Text outside Latin scripts in generated PDFs: DejaVu Sans and Droid Sans Fallback, subset as each PDF is written

#384. The PDFs the server writes (the annotated score's marks, the patch list, the stage plan, the schedule, the comment and QA sheets) set text in the standard Helvetica with WinAnsi, so a name in Greek, Cyrillic, Hebrew, Arabic or Japanese printed as question marks, and a Czech ř as a plain r.

- **One text writer.** `apps/server/src/media/pdf-text.ts` is shared by every PDF writer: it measures and draws text, and the document writers embed the fonts the drawings use. Text WinAnsi holds is still set in Helvetica, byte for byte as before, so a Latin PDF embeds nothing and does not change (a test compares the bytes). A piece of text WinAnsi cannot hold is set whole in the Unicode fonts, so a name never changes typeface in the middle; a paragraph of a plain-text PDF that needs them is set in them throughout.
- **Embedded and subset at write time, no dependency.** Our own reader of TrueType (`truetype.ts`, Node's zlib for compression) writes each font as a Type 0 font with a CIDFontType2 descendant, Identity-H encoding, glyph numbers as CIDs, a `/W` array of the glyphs used, measured with the font's own `hmtx` widths, and a ToUnicode map, so the text copies and searches. Only the glyphs a PDF uses keep their outlines; every other glyph is left empty, which deflate reduces to almost nothing. A patch list with a few Cyrillic names grows by about 6 KB, one with Japanese by about 3 KB, rather than the 350 KB of a whole font. Only fonts with TrueType outlines are read; a font with PostScript (CFF) outlines is refused, as subsetting CFF is a larger job than this needs.
- **The fonts, and their licences.** `PDF_FONT` and `PDF_FONT_BOLD` name DejaVu Sans and its bold, already in the image for watermarks, so they cost nothing: Latin, Greek, Cyrillic, Armenian, Georgian, Hebrew and Arabic. Their licence (the Bitstream Vera licence, with DejaVu's changes in the public domain) is a permissive font licence of the same kind as the SIL OFL. `PDF_FONT_FALLBACK` lists fonts tried in turn for characters the first lacks; the image sets Droid Sans Fallback (Apache 2.0, which combines with AGPL-3.0) for Chinese, Japanese and Korean. Noto Sans CJK, the OFL choice, was ruled out: Debian ships it only with CFF outlines, at over 50 MB. The image installs Droid's non-Latin fonts and keeps `DroidSansFallback.ttf` alone (decision 244), not the second, larger `DroidSansFallbackFull.ttf` (4 MB, which lacks Korean): the image grows by 3.4 MB on disk, about 1.7 MB compressed. Characters no configured font has (Thai, Devanagari, emoji) print as the font's empty box, and an ActualText span says what they were, so they still copy out.
- **Bold and oblique.** Bold uses the bold face where there is one; a fallback font without one draws its letters outlined in their own colour as well. Oblique (a dynamic on the score) slants the regular face as Helvetica Oblique is slanted.
- **Right to left.** A line is put in drawing order by the Unicode bidirectional algorithm without explicit embeddings (enough for a name, a cell or a note; there is nothing to nest): its direction is its first strong letter's, right-to-left runs are reversed with each mark kept on its letter and brackets mirrored, and numbers in them read left to right. Arabic letters are joined first, into the presentation forms (initial, medial, final, isolated, and lam with alef as its ligature) read from Unicode's own tables, which DejaVu Sans draws. There is no shaping engine: scripts that need one (the Indic scripts, Arabic's optional ligatures and mark positioning) are left for one, should anyone need it. Text stays left aligned in its cell or line, as the layouts are. The glyphs are written in drawing order with no ActualText, since readers (Poppler, pdf.js, Acrobat) put visually ordered right-to-left text back in reading order themselves, and Poppler would reverse an ActualText a second time; the ToUnicode map gives a joined form as the letter it shows.
- **Without the fonts,** in development and tests unless named, everything is set in Helvetica as before. A configured font that cannot be read is left out with a warning at start; the image's smoke test checks the image's fonts are where its settings say. CI installs both fonts so the tests that read the text back out of the PDFs (Poppler's `pdftotext`) run there.

## 239. Forensic marking: an inaudible mark in a recording's download, our own, off until the owners turn it on, and tracing a leak

#32, first part. Visible marks were on documents, pictures and video downloads (decisions 079 to 081); a recording's download left as it was uploaded. Now, once a workspace's owners turn it on, each download of a recording, original or proxy, carries an inaudible mark made for whoever asked, and an owner can trace a suspected leak back to that download. Streams, video, pictures and PDF pages are not marked invisibly yet: they follow after the first version (below).

- **Our own marker, no new dependency.** The issue's comment proposed building `audiowmark` (GPL-3.0) from source into the image, which waits on the owner. A marker of our own, in TypeScript on either side of the ffmpeg already in the image, needs no package, no build stage and no licence question, and keeps the signal processing in pure functions that are tested by themselves (`apps/server/src/media/forensic.ts`). It is simpler than `audiowmark` (no error-correcting code, no blind decoding) and has not been compared with it; if the owner prefers `audiowmark`, it can replace the embedding behind the same `forensic_marks` and trace, as a mark is only ever looked up, never decoded.
- **How it works.** At an analysis rate of 16 kHz, in frames of 32 ms, the spectrum from 406 Hz to 4.6 kHz is cut into 68 bands of 62.5 Hz; in each symbol of 64 ms every band is made 5 per cent louder or quieter by a sign. A quarter of the bands carry the workspace's synchronisation signs, which find where a clip sits; the rest carry the mark's code, 408 signs, changing every 4.1 s over a period of 32.8 s. The signs follow from HMAC-SHA256 under the workspace's forensic key, derived one way (HKDF) from the version of the workspace's key current at the time, with the mark's random id. Each channel is marked from its own sound with the same signs, so any fold-down (stereo, mono, one channel) carries it, and a silent channel stays silent. The mark gives way where it would take a sample past 0.98 of full scale.
- **Two passes, then sent.** The first decodes the recording at the analysis rate and writes the mark, per channel, to a small WAV file in the private workspace, on a thread of its own; the second decodes the recording at its own rate, adds the mark (resampled by ffmpeg) and encodes it. The original keeps its format where it is PCM in WAV or AIFF, or FLAC (the same rate, depth, channels and length; with the mark set to nothing the samples are the original's, which a test checks); other originals leave as FLAC (24-bit, 16 where the source had no more), decoded once and marked; the proxy is AAC at 256 kb/s from the original, as the stream's is. The file is made whole before it is sent, so its header is right: about 23 ms of work a second of stereo 44.1 kHz, both passes. An original keeps what describes it, as it did unmarked: a WAV original is its own file, chunk for chunk (Broadcast Wave, iXML, ADM and Dolby metadata included, so an Atmos master stays one), with the marked samples in place of its own; FLAC and AIFF keep their tags. A proxy carries none. A recording that cannot be marked is refused, never sent unmarked.
- **Written down first.** The access log has the download before anything is made (decision 056); the mark is then a row of `forensic_marks` naming that entry, the file and project (as they were), the recipient's label and id, the review that granted it, and the key's version. No references a deletion would take with it: the row lasts as long as the workspace, moves with no project and goes with the workspace's export without its key version.
- **Detection is matching, with a stated chance of a mistake.** A trace decodes the first ten minutes of the suspected leak at the analysis rate (mono), finds the 16 likeliest positions of the synchronisation signs, and scores every mark's code there. A score `z` from a code the clip does not carry has a chance of at most `exp(-z²/2)` (Hoeffding's bound: the code's signs are independent of the clip), and the chance shown is that bound multiplied by every comparison made (positions times marks, under every key version). A match is reported only below one in a million, and said in words, rounded down ("less than one in a billion"), never as a percentage of confidence. With 1,000 marks that needs `z` of about 6.9; with 100,000, about 7.5. A trace takes about a third of a second for a 20-second clip against 1,000 marks.
- **Owners only, and logged.** Under Workspace settings, Leak tracing (as the prototype shows): the *Audio* switch, off until the owners turn it on (a workspace setting, `forensicAudio`, changed only through its own route by owners and logged as a settings change), and *Trace a leak*. The file is streamed into a private workspace, up to 300 MB, examined and removed; never stored. Each trace is in the access log as `forensic.trace` with the file's name and size and the mark found, if any. Twenty traces an hour per owner. For a guest's match, *Revoke their access* ends their links and sessions.
- **The plan.** Marking needs the plans' `forensicMarking` feature, which every workspace has without the billing module. Tracing does not: marks already made stay traceable after a change of plan. The instance-wide default for new workspaces (decision 232) is still not offered. Marking is off until a workspace's owners turn it on: nobody has yet listened to marked quiet material, and a marked original is no longer the bytes that were uploaded, so an owner chooses it knowingly (the owner's decision, on #32).

**Measured limits.** The whole pipeline on generated music (notes and chords of decaying harmonic tones, a little noise; six pieces of 30 s, 24-bit stereo at 44.1 kHz), each clip taken 3.3 s in, matched against 1,000 marks; a pass is a match on the right mark, and no trace matched a wrong one. Real music, speech and quiet acoustic material were not measured, and neither was audibility by listening: the mark stays more than 25 dB below the music overall and 26 dB below it in its band, which says nothing certain about what a trained ear hears on solo piano. The owner should listen before relying on it.

| Transformation | 30 s | 20 s | 10 s |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| As sent (WAV) | 6/6 | 6/6 | 6/6 |
| MP3 at 128 kb/s | 6/6 | 6/6 | 3/6 |
| MP3 at 96 kb/s | 6/6 | 6/6 | 1/6 |
| AAC at 128 kb/s | 6/6 | 6/6 | 5/6 |
| AAC at 96 kb/s | 6/6 | 6/6 | 2/6 |
| AAC at 64 kb/s | 5/6 | 2/6 | 0/6 |
| Opus at 96 kb/s | 6/6 | 6/6 | 4/6 |
| Opus at 48 kb/s | 6/6 | 6/6 | 1/6 |
| Vorbis at quality 2 | 6/6 | 5/6 | 0/6 |
| 48 kHz, mono, 16-bit | 6/6 | 6/6 | 6/6 |
| 9 dB quieter, 6 dB more at 1 kHz | 6/6 | 6/6 | 6/6 |
| Telephone band (300 Hz to 3.4 kHz), 8 kHz | 6/6 | 6/6 | 3/6 |
| 0.1 per cent faster | 6/6 | 6/6 | 6/6 |
| 1 per cent faster | 0/6 | 0/6 | 0/6 |

So: twenty seconds is enough after encoding at 96 kb/s or more; ten seconds is not, once encoded; a change of speed of a per cent (a 44.1 kHz file played at 44.54 kHz, or pitch shifting) loses it; recording through the air, time-stretching, mixing with other sound and averaging several recipients' copies (collusion) were not tested. The tests (`apps/server/src/media/forensic.test.ts` and `forensic-audio.test.ts` beside it, and the API's `apps/server/src/forensic.test.ts`) check the round trip, cuts, AAC, MP3 and Opus re-encodes, resampling, the telephone band, fold-downs and the speed limit, where the encoders exist.

**Too loud to mark.** The mark makes way for the loudest moments so it never takes a recording past full scale; a recording loud nearly everywhere (peaks above 0.98 in most frames) would keep too little of it to be traced. Each channel with sound in the mark's band must keep at least half the mark's strength on average (`MIN_EMBEDDED`), or the download is refused (422) rather than sent with a mark that names nobody. Masters limited close to 0 dBFS may therefore not download while marking is on; turning marking off downloads them as uploaded.

**Later, by the owner's decision.** The owner settled #32 with this marker, off by default, and moved the rest to the milestone after the first version, each as its own issue:

- **Streams (A/B segment marking, #451).** Two marked variants of every audio segment (the same marker, two codes), each recipient served a sequence of A and B from their playback session, recorded as a mark per session. It doubles the stored audio segments and their encoding, and a leak of a stream identifies its viewer only from enough consecutive segments (about 30 s by the table above). Recommended next, for audio, behind the project's *Audio streams* switch in the prototype.
- **Video (#452 for the sound, #453 for the picture).** The same mark in a video download's sound is a small step (the burn already encodes the sound again) but marks only the sound; a mark in the picture needs a picture marker of our own, built and measured against re-encoding, as this one was.
- **Pictures and pages (#453).** An invisible mark in a picture's or page's pixels (spread spectrum in the DCT domain), measured against JPEG re-encoding, scaling and cropping; until then the visible mark stays the only one.
- **`audiowmark` (#453).** Whether to bring it in for robustness this marker lacks (its error-correcting code and longer integration), as the issue's comment proposed.

**Expanding only:** a new table, `forensic_marks`, and an access log action, `forensic.trace` (decision 198). The workspace setting is a key in its settings, read with a default.

## 240. Recorders in the take log: read live in the browser, one adapter interface, and one browser drives takes

#51's last part ("numbering synced to the recorder") and #450, after the spike in decision 237. The owner chose to read recorders live from the control room's browser, through a modular adapter interface, with two adapters now (a DAW over MIDI, and Sound Devices) and Zaxcom likely next.

- **One interface, adapters in one place.** An adapter (`RecorderAdapter` in `apps/web/src/lib/recorders`) connects (asking the browser and the person for what it needs), reports its status, gives the recorder's position, and sends the same few events whatever the recorder: `position`; `rolling` and `stopped`; `record` and `record-stop`, each with the take's number or name where the recorder gives them; and `status`, whose failures carry words for the person and are always shown, never swallowed. The events and the timecode arithmetic are in `packages/shared` (`recorders.ts`). Adapters are listed in one registry (`registry.ts`); the take log and the player use only the interface. A Zaxcom adapter would read ZaxNet's scene and take metadata (which Zaxcom distributes to slates through an ERX receiver) through a serial port or a small local bridge, and send `record` with the take's name and number: it fits without a change to the interface or the take log.
- **What the take log does with an event** is one pure function (`takeActionFor`): with takes started *when the recorder records*, a `record` starts the next take (or rolls the one armed) and `record-stop` or `stopped` stops the one recording; *when it rolls*, `rolling` and `stopped` do; *never* changes no take. A take already recording is left alone, and nothing that is not recording is stopped. Starts and stops go through the log's outbox (decision 155), named by the browser, so they are written offline as the keys' are. With a recorder connected, a take started or stopped by hand takes the recorder's timecode too. A recorder's own take number, where it gives one, is the take's number in a session numbered by the recorder; where it gives none, the number typed (or proposed) is used, as for Space.
- **One browser drives.** Several people use the take log at once, each with their own view (#51). Only the browser that connected a recorder drives takes from it, and only while its take log is open: it claims the session (`PUT /api/sessions/:id/recorder`, with the adapter's name and a claim id of the connection's own, made when it connects; `edit_takes`) and renews the claim every ten seconds (`renew`, which holds only while the claim is still its own, checked in the same statement that writes it), and everyone's log shows whose browser drives it. A claim not heard from for thirty seconds lapses. Connecting in another browser takes over: the last claim wins, and the browser that drove before lets go, saying so, as soon as the session it reads every two seconds shows a claim newer than its own (both by the server's clock) or its renewal is refused, so it never takes the recorder back by saying it is still there. `DELETE` lets go of only the connection's own claim, named by its id, so a late goodbye never ends another's. A claim is the connection's, not the person's: two tabs of the same member are two claims, and the later takes over from the earlier as anyone else's would. The others see the takes through the existing live log; nothing about the recorder itself is stored. The claim is four nullable columns on `sessions`, cleared when a project moves to another workspace and left out of a workspace's export.
- **A DAW, over MIDI (#450).** A pure decoder in `packages/shared` (`midi-timecode.ts`, no dependency) reads MIDI Time Code (quarter frames, assembled into a timecode two frames on from its first piece, either way; full frames on a locate) and MIDI Machine Control (play, stop, record strobe and exit, pause, locate), from Web MIDI's messages or a byte stream, passing over clocks and other messages and dropping a system exclusive message cut short. A second pure step (`midiRecorderStep`) turns that into recorder events, noticing quarter frames that stop arriving (a quarter of a second) as well as an MMC stop. Both are tested on a recorded stream (`fixtures/midi/daw-25fps.txt`). That stream is written to the two specifications, not captured from a DAW.
- **Permissions, as they now are.** Decision 237 and #450 said quarter frames need no permission. Since Chrome 124, all Web MIDI access asks the person; SysEx is a second, stronger ask. So the take log asks for MIDI on *Connect*, and for SysEx only when takes start and stop as the DAW records (machine control is SysEx); timecode alone, and takes *when the DAW rolls*, need no SysEx. Safari has no Web MIDI, which the choice says where it appears. The site's `Permissions-Policy` now allows `midi` and `microphone` to this origin (decision 241); nothing is asked for until someone connects.
- **The player follows the DAW.** In the View menu, *Follow the DAW* makes the file page's player chase the tab's recorder (the DAW over MIDI, connected for timecode alone if nothing is connected yet), less where the file starts on the DAW's timeline: its Broadcast Wave time reference or a video's own timecode, else zero, and typed in *File starts at*. It plays as the DAW rolls, pauses where it stops, waits at the start before the file begins, and seeks only past half a second, with the listening room's own step (`followStep`, decision 234), so a member leading a room while following the DAW leads the room from the DAW, with nothing new on the server. It is held while the member follows someone else's room. One connection serves the tab (`RecorderProvider`), so the take log and the player never open the same port twice.
- **Not verified,** and to be checked with real equipment before relying on it: which machine control each DAW sends while recording (decision 237's list; if none arrives, *when the DAW rolls* works from timecode alone); that Pyramix and Sequoia send timecode to a virtual port; Firefox's site-permission flow for MIDI; and the whole adapter against a DAW at all. The decoder is tested only against streams written to the specifications.

Sources: [Chrome, the Web MIDI permission prompt](https://developer.chrome.com/blog/web-midi-permission-prompt); [MDN, Web MIDI](https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/Web_MIDI_API); [MIDI Time Code](https://midi.community/docs/timing/); [Zaxcom, ZaxNet's metadata to slates](https://tvtechnology.com/equipment/zaxcom-announces-zaxnet-rf-network); and those of decision 237.

**Expanding only:** three nullable columns on `sessions` (`recorder_kind`, `recorder_by`, `recorder_seen_at`); an older release ignores them (decision 198).

## 241. Sound Devices recorders: no network interface a browser can reach, so their timecode out, read from an audio input

#51, the second adapter of decision 240. The owner asked for take name, number and timecode from Sound Devices 8-Series and MixPre recorders over their network control, their web control or the Wingman protocol, whichever is documented, and for an honest account of what a browser can reach.

- **What the recorders offer.** As far as Sound Devices publishes, neither has a web control or REST interface. The 8-Series (Scorpio, 888, 833) is controlled remotely by SD-Remote, Sound Devices' own app, over Bluetooth LE (and USB on Android tablets), and by USB control surfaces speaking Mackie Control to the recorder; its Ethernet port (Scorpio and 888) carries Dante and Frame.io Camera to Cloud. The MixPre series is controlled by Wingman, again Sound Devices' own app over Bluetooth LE; its USB makes it an audio interface. Neither app's protocol is published, and we found no documentation of either for other software. The 788T's CL-WiFi, an app over Wi-Fi, is long discontinued. What both series do send is timecode: LTC from a timecode output (BNC or Hirose on the 8-Series, BNC or the 3.5 mm output on the MixPre II), with a Rec Run mode in which it runs only while recording.
- **What a browser could reach, had there been an interface.** Hekate is served over HTTPS; a recorder's own web server would be plain HTTP on the local network. Firefox and Safari block that as mixed content. Chrome exempts a request to a private address or a `.local` name (or one marked `targetAddressSpace: "local"`) once the person allows Local Network Access (its prompt, from Chrome 141 on, as decision 237 found); even then CORS applies, so the device must answer with `Access-Control-Allow-Origin`, which a recorder's built-in server is unlikely to. A browser cannot speak Bluetooth LE to an unpublished protocol (Web Bluetooth exists in Chrome, but reverse-engineering SD-Remote is neither honest nor safe to ship), nor UDP or raw TCP.
- **So the Sound Devices adapter reads the timecode out.** The recorder's timecode output goes into an input of the control room's audio interface; the browser opens that input (`getUserMedia`, with echo cancellation, noise suppression and gain control off, since timecode is a signal) and passes the chosen channel through an AudioWorklet served from this origin (`/worklets/ltc-capture.js`, as the Content Security Policy requires) to a pure LTC decoder in `packages/shared` (`ltc.ts`, no dependency): biphase mark read through a band that follows the input's level, either way up, any chunk size, the rate told from how often frames arrive (23.976, 24, 25, 29.97 and 30) and drop frame from its flag. It is tested on LTC the tests write themselves, quiet, inverted, noisy, at 44.1 and 48 kHz, across midnight and drop-frame skips. A further pure step (`ltcRunStep`) says the timecode rolls after three frames in a row move on, and stops a fifth of a second after none has, so one misread frame never stops a take; with the recorder on Rec Run, *when its timecode rolls* starts and stops takes as it records. The same adapter is offered for any other recorder's timecode out.
- **Take names and numbers are not read.** With no interface a browser can reach, a session numbered by the recorder keeps its numbers typed, or matched to the files on import (decision 156). This is said where the recorder is chosen.
- **The smallest route to take numbers, proposed and not built.** A small optional local bridge: a process on the control room's computer that speaks the recorder's protocol and hands the page recorder events over a WebSocket on the loopback address (a secure context, so not mixed content; Chrome's Local Network Access still asks once), as one more adapter. For Sound Devices it waits on a protocol to speak: we propose asking Sound Devices for SD-Remote's or Wingman's protocol, or an SDK, before anything else. The same bridge would serve Zaxcom and Reaper's web interface (decision 237).
- **Not verified** against real hardware, none of it: that the recorders' timecode out reads cleanly at an audio interface's line input; what a recorder on Rec Run sends between takes (a held frame or nothing: both read as stopped); multichannel interfaces' channels beyond the first two in each browser (the adapter says when the input has fewer channels than the one chosen); Safari's AudioWorklet with this input. The research could not open Sound Devices' own pages from where it was done, so it rests on search results quoting their product pages, release notes and app listings; that no network interface exists is "none found", which a support question to Sound Devices would settle.

Sources: [Sound Devices, 8-Series firmware 3.21 (SD-Remote over Bluetooth LE)](https://www.sounddevices.com/?p=81514); [SD-Remote on the App Store](https://apps.apple.com/af/app/sd-remote/id1487546591); [Sound Devices, 8-Series FAQ](https://www.sounddevices.com/8-series-faq/); [Sound Devices, 888 (Ethernet for Dante and Frame.io; timecode modes)](https://www.sounddevices.com/product/888/); [888 user guide v9.50](https://cdn.sounddevices.com/wp-content/uploads/2023/12/888-v9.50-User-Guide.pdf); [Sound Devices, Wingman](https://support.sounddevices.com/hc/en-us/articles/46950119172251-Wingman); [Wingman for Android](https://www.sounddevices.com/wingman-app-now-available-for-android/); [MixPre II user guide](https://guides.sounddevices.com/mixpre-ii/); [CL-WiFi for the 788T](https://www.prosoundweb.com/sound-devices-introduces-the-cl-wifi-for-the-788t-digital-recorder-at-nab-2011/); [Chrome, Local Network Access](https://developer.chrome.com/blog/local-network-access); [MDN, Local network access](https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/Security/Defenses/Local_network_access).

## 242. Importing a ControlRoom project: replayed as ControlRoom replays it, mapped into Hekate, previewed before anything is written

#57. A ControlRoom `.croom` file is ControlRoom's event store: an event-sourced SQLite database. The import was first written without the format and every name in it was a guess; it has since been checked against ControlRoom's own source (its `src/core/store.cpp`, `events.hpp`, `events_json.cpp`, `events_serialize.cpp`, `events_deserialize.cpp`, `events_apply.cpp`, `session.cpp`, `model.hpp` and its core tests), read only to learn the format. No ControlRoom code is in Hekate, and ControlRoom has no sample project files to copy.

- **The file.** Three tables. `meta (key text primary key, value text)` holds `schema_version`, `head` and the desktop app's view state under `ui.*` (`ui.layout`, `ui.filter`, `ui.scale_mode`, `ui.percent`, `ui.current_pdf`, `ui.pdf.<id>.page`, `ui.sel_piece`, `ui.sel_movement`), which is never an event. `events (seq integer primary key autoincrement, ts integer not null, type text not null, payload text not null, branch integer not null default 0)`: `ts` is ms since 1970, and `payload` the event as JSON with its `type` in it. `blobs (id integer primary key autoincrement, name text, bytes blob)` holds every embedded file (scores, attachments, stage plan underlays), referred to by `id`. `sqlite_sequence` exists only once an event or blob has been written, so it is not required. The reader (`apps/server/src/croom/reader.ts`, Node's built-in `node:sqlite`, read-only) refuses a file without the three tables, their columns, or an integer `schema_version`.
- **Schema versions.** ControlRoom writes `schema_version` 1 and has written nothing else (its store has one constant and the repository one version); older files differ only by events and fields it still reads (below), not by schema. Version 1 is the one supported; another is read all the same and the preview says it has not been checked.
- **Undo and redo.** The project is branch 0 up to `head`, in `seq` order (ControlRoom's `active_events`). Undo moves `head` back to the previous branch-0 event and redo forward, so events past the head are undone and still redoable; a new event after an undo moves them to branch 1 for good. Nothing is ever deleted. The import replays branch 0 to the head only, and counts both kinds of undone event; a file with no `head` (ControlRoom always writes one) is replayed whole and the report says so.
- **Replaying, as ControlRoom does.** Each event's type is the one in its payload, as ControlRoom's `deserialize_event` reads it (a payload with no type, or not JSON, is skipped, as there; a table type that differs is reported). Each type goes to one small module of its own family (`families/`: session, categories, views, PDFs, repertoire, takes, score marks, personnel, venues, schedule, stage, to-dos, attachments), which reads each field by its one name with ControlRoom's default where it is missing, and changes the model as its `apply()` does. Updates write the whole entry (`update_piece`, `update_personnel`, `update_schedule_item`, `update_stage_plan` …), so a field an update leaves out takes its default, as in ControlRoom. An event naming an entry the project does not hold changes nothing there and is said in the report. A field of the wrong kind takes its default and is said; a field no family reads and an event no family knows are counted under "Not brought over".
- **Ids.** One counter for every kind (`SessionState::next_id`), starting at 1 on every replay. An id is drawn by exactly these, once each: `add_pdf`, `add_work`, `add_movement`, `add_take`, `add_category`, `add_annotation`, `add_piece`, `add_venue`, `add_personnel`, `add_schedule_item`, `add_stagebox`, `add_stage_plan`, `add_todo`, `add_attachment`, `add_view`, and each element with `id` 0 in `upsert_stage_element` and `upsert_stage_elements`, but only when the plan it names exists. An element with an id of its own keeps it and moves nothing; nothing else draws one, undone events are never replayed so never draw one, and an event ControlRoom does not know draws none. Where a file has an unknown `add_` or `upsert_` event followed by events that make entries, a newer ControlRoom may have drawn an id for it, so the preview warns that what follows may be linked wrongly. A piece's movements have no ids: takes and schedule slots name them by place, from 1. `EntityId` is unsigned 64-bit; its largest value names the whole repertoire in a slot.
- **Every event, and what Hekate makes of it.**

  | ControlRoom event (payload fields) | In Hekate |
  | --- | --- |
  | `create_session` (`name`), `set_meta` (`field`, `value`: `name`) | The import's session, named after it |
  | `set_meta` `conductor`, `producer`, `engineer` | A person on the personnel (Conductor, Producer, Engineer), unless the personnel names them already |
  | `set_meta` `frame_rate` (24, 25, 29.97, 29.97DF, 30) | How the take timecodes are read and written (";" before the frames for 29.97DF) |
  | `set_meta` `per_score_takes` | The session's numbering: per track when each score numbered its own, else continuous |
  | `set_meta` `organisation`, `public_info`, `author`, `score_order`, `lifecycle_enabled`, `personal_notes` | Not brought over: ControlRoom's export letterhead and front page, its score selector's order, its take log's mode, and one person's private notes (not shared with the project) |
  | `set_notes` (`notes`) | The session's notes |
  | `add_pdf` (`name`, `blob`, `pages`), `replace_pdf` (`pdf`, `name`, `blob`, `pages`), `remove_pdf` (`pdf`, its marks with it), `set_pdf_page_offset` (`pdf`, `offset`) | A score document of the project, stored encrypted like any upload; its offset becomes its work's page offset (the same sign: PDF page less printed page) |
  | `add_piece` (nothing), `update_piece` (`id`, `title`, `composer`, `arranger`, `forces`, `abbr`, `notes`, `pdf`, `movements`: `num`, `title`, `forces`, `abbr`, `pdf`, `p0`, `p1`), `delete_piece` (`id`) | A work; its movements its tracks, numbered as printed, with their printed pages and their own score where they have one; an arranger is written into the composer ("Scott Joplin, arr. …"). A movement's forces and abbreviation are not brought over (Hekate's tracks have none) |
  | `add_work` (`name`, `numbering`), `add_movement` (`work`, `name`, `base`), from older files | A work, and its tracks; the old numbering mode and number base are not brought over |
  | `add_take` (`number`, `work`, `movement`, `pdf`), `delete_take` (`take`) | A take of the track it was recorded under (a movement, a piece, an older file's work or movement, or its score), in the session, its time the take's start |
  | `set_take_lifecycle` (`take`, `value`), `set_take_rating` (`take`, `rating`), `adjust_false_start` (`take`, `delta`), `set_take_notes` (`take`, `notes`), `set_take_timecode` (`take`, `in`, `tc`) | The take's state (aborted, else recorded), rating (0 to 5), false starts (never below none), notes and timecodes in and out |
  | `add_category` (`label`, `color`, `keybind`) | A score mark category, only where a mark brought over uses it, in the nearest of Hekate's colours (ControlRoom packs red in the lowest byte); its letter key is not brought over (Hekate's keys are 1 to 9) |
  | `add_annotation` (`kind`, `loc`: `pdf`, `page`, `geom`, `x`, `y`, `w`, `h`; `category`, `note`, `takes_affected`, `take_resolved`, `tedit`: `takes`, `overdub`, `lead`; `ink`) | A score mark on the track whose printed pages it lies on (below): `issue` an issue (a region where it is one), `tedit` an edit point for each hand-over (3T4T5 is two; a lead-in starts the chain; an overdub lays each take it names over), `take_begin` and `take_end` a take start and end, `punch_in` a punch-in, `spot_x` a wrong note, `circled_dynamic` a dynamic, `note` a sticky note, `ink` ink, its points made absolute |
  | `set_annotation_status` (`id`, `status`), `set_annotation_note` (`id`, `note`), `set_annotation_takes` (`id`, `affected`, `resolved`), `move_annotation` (`id`, `page`, `x`, `y`), `delete_annotation` (`id`) | The mark resolved (when it was), its text, the takes it occurred in and the take that fixed it, and its place; the mark keeps ControlRoom's time of making |
  | `add_personnel` (`kind`), `update_personnel` (`id`, `name`, `role`, `rights`, `cans`, `forces_notes`, `sections`: `name`, `members` (`name`, `div`, `rights`, `cans`), `unnamed`; `observers`), `delete_personnel`, `move_personnel` (`id`, `to`) | Someone on the personnel, in order: rights is the signed session agreement, cans headphones, forces notes the seating; an ensemble's sections with their head-count and named players, how each divides written as Hekate writes it ("2 + 2"); an older file's observers people of the other kind. Which part each player sits in is not brought over |
  | `add_venue` (nothing), `update_venue` (`id`, `name`, `address`), `delete_venue` | A venue |
  | `add_schedule_item` (`kind`, `index`), `update_schedule_item` (`id`, `start`, `dur`, `done`, `title`, `notes`, `personnel`, `refs`, `label`, `date`, `venue_id`; older files' `piece`, `movement`, `composer`, `arranger`, `forces`, `venue`, `pdf`), `delete_schedule_item`, `move_schedule_item` (`id`, `to`) | The schedule: a `day` row starts a schedule day (its label, date, venue; it starts at its first timed row, else 10:00); `slot` a repertoire slot of the works and tracks it names, or the whole repertoire; `break` a break, or a get-in where its title says so; `venue` a venue change named for the venue. A day row's notes and an older slot's own composer, arranger and forces are not brought over; rows before the first day go on a day of their own |
  | `add_stagebox` (nothing), `update_stagebox` (`id`, `name`, `ins`, `outs`: rows of `[source, device, cable, notes]`), `delete_stagebox` | A stage box and its patch list, channel n being row n; empty rows are left out unless a microphone or cable is plugged into them |
  | `add_stage_plan` (nothing), `update_stage_plan` (`id`, `name`, `w`, `d`, `paper`, `venue_id`, `blob`, `blob_name`, `ulx`, `uly`, `uls`, `ulo`, `lmask`), `delete_stage_plan` | A stage plan of that size; its underlay a file of the project drawn under the stage, as wide as its pixels times its scale (read from a PNG, JPEG, GIF or BMP header; the stage's width when ControlRoom had not yet scaled it). The paper size, the venue link (the name says where) and the label mask are not brought over |
  | `upsert_stage_element` (`plan`, `el`), `upsert_stage_elements` (`plan`, `els`), `delete_stage_element` (`plan`, `el`), `delete_stage_elements` (`plan`, `els`); an element is `id`, `k`, `x`, `y`, `w`, `h`, `r`, `lb`, `c`, `per`, `sec`, `mem`, `box`, `ch`, `pts` | A stage item: `person` a performer (linked to the person, or labelled with an ensemble player's name), `block` an ensemble of that section, as deep as its head-count makes it, `mic` a microphone plugged into its patch channel, `cable` a cable run for each stretch of its line, `wall` a wall for each stretch, `timp` timpani, `circle` an ellipse, `stagebox` a stage box, `piano`, `riser`, `rect` and `label` the same, any other kind its own. ControlRoom places an element by its centre, Hekate a box by its corner and a round item by its centre; label groups follow ControlRoom's (people, sections, mics, boxes). An element's own colour is not brought over |
  | `add_todo` (`text`), `toggle_todo` (`id`), `delete_todo` (`id`), `reorder_todos` (`order`) | The session's to-dos, in ControlRoom's order |
  | `add_attachment` (`name`, `blob`), `delete_attachment` (`id`) | A file of the project, of its kind, stored encrypted and drawn or probed like any upload |
  | `add_view`, `set_active_view`, `update_view_layout`, `rename_view`, `delete_view` | Not brought over: the desktop app's window layouts |

- **Where things land.** ControlRoom places a mark on a score's page, never on a piece; the import places it on the track whose printed pages it lies on, through the score's page offset: a movement with a first and last page covers those, one with no last page runs on to the next that starts, and a piece whose score is the whole PDF covers it all; a mark on a page none covers goes on the nearest movement, and the report says so. A score the repertoire does not cover, with marks or takes on it, becomes a work of its own named after the file, as ControlRoom's own export treats such a score. A take recorded under a piece goes on its movement's track (on its first where it names none); under no piece but a score, on that score's track; under neither, on a track made for such takes, named after the session. The whole file is one ControlRoom session, so its takes, to-dos and notes go into one Hekate session, dated where all its takes fall on one day.
