- **The plan.** `plan.ts` (pure) turns the replayed model into what the project will gain, checking every entry with the same zod schema the API checks it with, cutting text to Hekate's lengths where needed and saying so. The preview and the import run the same plan.
- **Preview, then import, nothing kept between them.** `POST /api/projects/:id/croom/preview` and `/import` take the file as the request's body (at most 200 MB, its own limit in `app.ts`), for those who may manage the project (`edit_project`: owners, admins, producers). It is written to a private scratch directory under `WORK_DIR` (as the media workers do: `docs/security.md`, scratch space) because `node:sqlite` reads files, and removed when the request ends whatever happens; so the import sends the file again. The preview writes nothing. The import stores each embedded file through `storage.put`, encrypted with its own key like any upload, then writes every row in one transaction under a lock on the project, planned again against the project as it then stands; if anything fails, nothing is written and the stored files are removed. Each file is a file of the project, drawn (scores, pictures) or probed (audio, video) by the media worker and in the access log as an upload; the import itself is in the access log (`project.croom_import`).
- **What is already there.** A work, venue, person, stage plan, stage box or issue category with the name of one in the project is either merged into it (the default: a merged work gains the tracks it lacks, matched by name; a merged box the channels it lacks; a merged plan keeps its own items; takes whose number its track already has are left out) or added beside it, under "Name (2)" where names must differ, as the prototype's Import offers.
- **Tested against a fiction written as ControlRoom writes.** `croom/fixture.ts` writes a `.croom` file at test time with a small event store that behaves as ControlRoom's (the same tables, one transaction an event, the redo tail set aside, undo and redo moving the head, payloads written as its JSON library writes them: keys in order, floats as floats, the largest id exactly), from the sample cast only. The ids each event draws are counted by hand in the fixture, so the tests prove the replay's counter against the source's rules. No binary fixture is committed.
- **Still unverified.** Only how a real file looks against the preview: ControlRoom's repository has no sample project, so the first real import should be read with its report.

**Expanding only:** one value added to the access log's action type (`project.croom_import`); an older release never writes it (decision 198).

## 243. Renovate opens the dependency updates, in place of Dependabot

The owner's choice: Renovate, already installed as a GitHub App, replaces Dependabot's version updates. Renovate reads one file for every ecosystem, pins and moves digests as well as versions, keeps a hash-pinned requirements file's hashes, and can follow a version the image installs by hand, all of which Dependabot did in part or not at all. `.github/dependabot.yml` is gone; `.github/renovate.json5` takes its place, with a comment on each setting.

- **The same updates as before.** npm (the pnpm workspace), GitHub Actions, the ADM renderer's pip pins in `tools/ear` (decision 140) and the images in the Dockerfile and `deploy/`'s compose files, weekly early on Monday (UTC). The same groups: npm minor and patch updates in one pull request and each major on its own (monorepos together), every action update in one, the renderer's pins in one, each image on its own. The same commits, `chore(deps): …`, which Release Please leaves out of a release, and the same labels (`dependencies` and the ecosystem's). No more than ten open at once, and no automerge: a person merges every update.
- **The supply-chain rules, kept by the configuration.** A version waits a day after its release before Renovate proposes it, as pnpm's `minimumReleaseAge` and Dependabot's cooldown required (images had no wait before, and have none now). Actions stay pinned to a commit with their full version in a comment; every image is pinned by digest, which now covers the compose files, the Swarm stack and CI's service containers too, so Renovate first opens one pull request adding those digests. The renderer's requirement files keep their hashes: Renovate rewrites them with `hashin`, a hash for every file of the new release, as Dependabot did.
- **What is new.** The Helm chart's values are watched (its only image today is Hekate's own, which follows the chart's version), and so is the Swarm stack. The pnpm the image installs moves with `packageManager` in one pull request, so they never differ. The workspace's `overrides` are left alone: they are security pins, edited and removed by hand. `.nvmrc` is not watched, as before: a new Node major is a decision of its own.
- **No dependency dashboard.** Dependabot had none, and the open pull requests are the record. Without it, an update held back (by the day's wait or the limit of ten) is not listed anywhere until it opens.
- **Security fixes.** Renovate's `vulnerabilityAlerts` are on: when GitHub's Dependabot alerts (a repository feature apart from version updates, and still enabled) report a vulnerable version, Renovate opens the fix at once, outside the schedule and the day's wait. Dependabot security updates, a repository setting, duplicate them and should be switched off in the repository's settings (Code security, Dependabot security updates); the alerts stay on, since Renovate reads them.
- **Lock file maintenance** stays off, as Dependabot had none.
- **No pnpmfile.** Renovate's hosted app runs pnpm without a repository's pnpmfile hook, so the hook that removed the optional peers of better-auth and drizzle-orm Hekate does not use would have been skipped, and its lockfiles would not match the one CI installs. The same removals are now overrides (`"better-auth>react": "-"` and so on) in `pnpm-workspace.yaml`, which every pnpm reads: the dependency graph is unchanged, and a lockfile written with or without hooks is the same.

This supersedes "Dependabot moves the pins" in decision 140: Renovate moves them.

## 244. The app image on Alpine, holding only the programs Hekate runs

#170, #166. The owner's instruction: "ideally, images use alpine as a base, containing literally only what they need and nothing else". PostgreSQL and Caddy already ran on their `-alpine` images. The app image ran on Debian (`node:26-trixie-slim`, with a `python:3.13-slim-trixie` stage for the ADM renderer), and Trivy reported several hundred findings in it with no fix to take: Debian's base system (util-linux, login, tar, ncurses, perl-base, systemd's libraries), what Debian's ffmpeg pulls in (X11, mesa, libsndfile, libtiff, libxml2 with one critical), and Python 3.13. CI passed, since it fails only on what can be fixed, but code scanning could not reach zero while the runtime was Debian.

- **One Alpine release throughout.** Every stage starts from `node:26-alpine`, pinned by one digest, so building, the renderer's stage and the runtime are the same Alpine release (3.24). The base stage installs pnpm with npm, as before.
- **Python from Alpine, in both places.** The `ear` stage adds Alpine's `python3` and `py3-pip` and installs the renderer with pip into `/opt/ear`, as decision 140 does; the runtime adds the same `python3` and nothing else of Python. Taking Python from Alpine's packages in both, rather than from the `python` image, keeps the two the same version by construction (3.14 in Alpine 3.24), and the runtime's Python is a package scanners see. pip installs with `--target`, which Alpine's marking of its Python as externally managed does not stop. The renderer's compiled dependencies (lxml, NumPy, SciPy) are pinned to their `musllinux_1_2` wheels for CPython 3.14 on x86-64 and ARM64, taken with their hashes from PyPI's JSON API; they carry their own OpenBLAS, gfortran and libstdc++, so the image needs no library for them beyond musl. CI's Ubuntu hashes stay.
- **The runtime's packages.** `ffmpeg` (with `ffprobe`), `poppler-utils` (`pdfinfo`, `pdftoppm` and `pdftotext`), `font-dejavu` and `font-droid-nonlatin`, `ca-certificates` and `python3`, every package upgraded at build. Of the fonts, only the faces in use stay: DejaVu Sans for watermarks and PDFs (decision 238), DejaVu's Sans, Sans Mono and Serif, upright and bold, as Debian's `fonts-dejavu-core` held, for Poppler to substitute a PDF's missing fonts with, and Droid Sans Fallback alone of Droid's non-Latin fonts. The paths are Alpine's: `/usr/share/fonts/dejavu/` and `/usr/share/fonts/droid-nonlatin/DroidSansFallback.ttf`, which the image's `PDF_FONT`, `PDF_FONT_BOLD` and `PDF_FONT_FALLBACK` name; watermarks already looked for DejaVu there.
- **Only the programs Hekate runs.** The last build step is a Node script (`scripts/image-harden.mjs`), since the shell is among what it removes. It keeps `node`, `ffmpeg`, `ffprobe`, `pdfinfo`, `pdftoppm`, `pdftotext` and `python3` (with the links that name them) and removes every other entry in `/bin`, `/sbin`, `/usr/bin`, `/usr/sbin` and `/usr/local/bin`: busybox and all its applets (so `sh` too), apk, npm, and the tools the packages bring (fontconfig's, Poppler's others, `qt-faststart`, `scanelf`, `ssl_client`). apk's library and `/etc/apk` go with it. It then clears every setuid and setgid bit. apk's records (`/lib/apk/db`) stay, so scanners still see every package, as dpkg's did. Removing a program from a package leaves the package's record, so an advisory against a package whose program is gone is still reported: the scan errs towards too much.
- **What stays the same.** The non-root `node` user, the exec-form `HEALTHCHECK`, `ENTRYPOINT` and `CMD`, the read-only, capability-free way Compose and the chart run it, and the smoke test, which now also runs `pdfinfo` and `pdftotext`, checks that no other program is left and that the package records are there.
- **What changes for operators.** Nothing to set: the image's font settings move with it. A setting that named Debian's font paths by hand must name Alpine's. Without a shell or busybox, `docker exec app node -e '...'` remains the way into a running container. Node publishes its musl builds as an experimental platform, built by the Node Docker team for the `-alpine` images; the server and its dependencies are pure JavaScript, so nothing native is at stake.
- **Not done:** a smaller ffmpeg built from source (issue #170's second option) or a Wolfi runtime (its third). Alpine's own `ffmpeg` still links libraries Hekate never uses (X11, PulseAudio, Vulkan), but Alpine follows their fixes closely; if its findings stay above zero, a narrower ffmpeg is the next step.

## 245. Biome checks the code, JSON and CSS, and leaves Markdown and YAML alone

Biome 2.6 formats and lints Markdown and YAML by default. Hekate keeps it to what it checked before.

- **Markdown is written by hand, or generated.** Formatting it would rewrite every page of `docs/` and of the documentation site at once, and the generated pages (`docs/configuration.md`, the upgrade notes, the API reference) would have to come out of their generators already formatted, or fail the check after every regeneration.
- **The YAML is not all Hekate's.** pnpm writes `pnpm-lock.yaml` its own way, and the Helm chart's templates are Go templates that no YAML parser reads, so they are left out of Biome altogether (Helm's own lint checks them in CI).
- **Revisited** when Biome's Markdown formatter can be run by the generators, or the docs want a formatter of their own.

## 246. The take page: one lane, the take's own mix, until takes hold a file per microphone

The prototype's take page (`/takes/$takeId`) shows a lane per microphone, each with solo and mute, and the summed mix as the last lane. A take holds one file today, its scratch mix (decision 115: "a take is its mix until takes hold a file per microphone"), so the page is built for what exists:

- **One lane, the mix.** The take's own file plays in the player every recording plays in, through the protected delivery path (encrypted segments, a playback link bound to the session, the key from the session-bound endpoint, each start logged). No new route returns media; the page asks for the file's playback as the file page does. A multichannel mix still has the player's Channels menu to solo and mute its channels (#74). The prototype keeps its microphone lanes, as the design for when takes hold a file per microphone; the session's table says `mix` under *Mics* until then.
- **Who sees it.** `GET /api/takes/:id` needs the project's takes level of View (`view_takes`), as the track's takes do, and answers 404 for a take of another workspace or of a project the member cannot see. The page is a member's: a guest hears a take only as shared on a thread they see (#133), in their own player, and has no take page.
- **What it reads.** The take with its track (and the score it follows), the session it was recorded in, and that session's takes of the same track in number order, which the page switches between (number keys 1 to 9). A take from no session switches among the track's other takes from none. "In V3" and the table's *Used in* ("V3 bars 12–40") come from the track's latest edit decision list (#56), read as the takes view reads it.
- **Loop** plays the take from its in point to its out point where they are given, else the whole of it.
- **Share to a comment** shares the take on an open thread of the track's latest version, through the existing sharing (`POST /api/comments/:id/takes`, triage on the thread's file and sight of takes, logged). The prototype's dialog also offered a new comment and a choice of audition (at the comment's bar, or the whole take); neither has anything behind it (a shared take is auditioned at its thread's bar wherever the thread has one), so the prototype's dialog now offers the thread alone and says how it is heard.

**Expanding only:** a new read route; no schema change.

## 247. Comment categories: the workspace keeps the set new projects start with, and each project a copy

Decision 055 made categories the project's own, seeded with the prototype's five. The prototype's workspace settings also have a Comment categories section, "the set every new project starts with. A project may add its own." Nothing in decision 055 rules that out, so both hold: the workspace keeps a set, and a project's own categories are a copy of it made when the project is made.

- **A table of its own, `workspace_categories`,** shaped as `comment_categories` without the project (a name unique in the workspace whatever its case, a handling, a stage for a deferring one, a position). It is the workspace's, so a project moving out leaves it (`transferTables.workspace`), and a workspace's export holds it.
- **Seeded once, then the workspace's choice.** The first read of the set, or the first project made, gives a workspace the five defaults, under a lock on its row so two first reads make one set, and marks it seeded (`organisations.categories_seeded`). From then on an empty set is a choice: new projects start with no categories, and triage works without one. The row is read without the lock first, so making projects does not queue on the workspace once its set exists.
- **A copy, never a link.** A project made from the set keeps its categories whatever the set does after; a project made before categories were seeded (decision 055's lazy case) is seeded from the set as it is when first read. A project made from a template or another project still takes theirs (decision 073), not the workspace's.
- **Who.** Every member reads the set (`GET /api/organisation/categories`); owners and admins add, change and remove one. It is the same input as a project's (`categoryInput`), and one dialog in the web app edits either.
- **No colours.** The prototype's workspace list draws a dot per category and offers *Change colour*; no category has a colour anywhere else in the product (a project's list, triage, the composer), so the web app shows neither, as the project's Comments page already does.

**Expanding only:** a new table and a new column with a default; an older release reads neither and seeds projects from the defaults as before (decision 198).

## 248. How a workspace's members sign in: the ways it allows, and how long a session lasts there

The prototype's Security section has *Methods allowed* (password, passkey, single sign-on, directory) and *Sessions end after*. Both are workspace settings, and both are enforced by the server, not by the page.

- **A session knows its way in.** `auth_sessions.method` is set as a session is made: single sign-on where a provider made it, the directory where its route did, a passkey wherever the factor is one (on its own, or confirming a password, which is as phishing-resistant), and otherwise a password, with or without a code (`methodFor`, `apps/server/src/second-factor.ts`). A session from before the column reads as its factor and provider say (`sessionMethod`, `packages/shared`), so a directory session from before it reads as a password.
- **Refused where it acts, and at the door where it can be.** A session signed in a way its workspace does not allow is refused in that workspace with `sign_in_method_refused` (403), on every route, playback, the OAuth consent and the guest-aware routes alike, except `/api/me` and switching workspace, as with enforced single sign-on: it may still work in another workspace that allows it. The web app sends them to sign in another way, saying why. Signing in a way none of the account's workspaces allows is refused at sign-in itself, with the reason, so nobody signs in only to be turned away; an account in no workspace yet is never refused there.
- **Never locked out.** At least one way stays allowed (the page keeps the last one on; the server refuses an empty set). Whoever changes the set must keep their own way in. An owner also keeps a password and a passkey where the workspace allows only single sign-on or a directory, which lean on another system, as enforced single sign-on keeps an owner's password (#37); where a password or a passkey is allowed, owners are held to the set like everyone. The page offers single sign-on and the directory only once the workspace has them on; guests always use their link.
- **Session length, counted from signing in.** The instance ends a session unused for 14 days (`SESSION_DAYS`, sliding as it is used). A workspace may end its members' sessions sooner, 1 or 7 days after signing in however much they are used, which is what a workspace that sets a length means (shorter than the instance, never longer: the server refuses more, and offers only what is shorter). A session past its workspace's length is deleted as it next acts there, and answered 401 (`session_ended`), so it ends everywhere, as an expired session does; the end is `sessionEndsAt`, the sooner of the two.
- **Where it is kept.** Both are workspace settings (`signInMethods`, `sessionDays`), defaulting to every way and the instance's length. A release before this one writes the settings whole and would drop both if someone saved settings on it after a rollback, leaving every way allowed and the instance's length: less strict, never a lock-out.

**Expanding only:** a nullable column on `auth_sessions`; an older release neither writes nor reads it (decision 198).

## 249. "Keyboard map on ?" turns the single key off, and "Metadata editor in QA" is kept for the editor to come

The prototype's Appearance, Tools shown, has both switches. *Keyboard map on ?*, on by default, decides whether "?" alone opens the keyboard map; off, the Help menu still opens it and no longer names the key. A shortcut of one character that can be turned off is what WCAG 2.2's 2.1.4 (Character Key Shortcuts) asks, for people who type with speech input. *Metadata editor in QA*, off by default, is stored as the preference `showMetadataEditor` and does nothing yet: the editor is not built, and whoever builds it reads `usePreferences().showMetadataEditor` to decide whether the QA view shows it.

## 250. The instance's Queues: pg-boss read as it is, a pause kept in the settings, and a file's failure run again as the file's own Retry

The prototype's Instance, Queues: a table per queue with waiting, active, failed and p95, "Retry every failure" and "Pause all", a menu per queue (Retry failed, Pause, Drain) and Recent jobs with Retry and Details.

- **One row per pg-boss queue**, which is one per kind of job in `jobDefinitions`, in the order they are defined, each with its lane and a few words (`queueHints` in `packages/shared`, which a test holds to every queue the server defines). The counts are read from pg-boss's own table, as the metrics are (`created` and `retry` are waiting; `failed` is what the queue still keeps); p95 is the 95th percentile of the last day's finished jobs, start to finish. A queue its tables do not yet hold reads as zeros; one that cannot be read is a 503, never an idle table. The page reads again every five seconds.
- **Recent jobs** are the last day's latest twenty: work for files, channels and workspaces, and the scheduled sweeps only where they failed, as they run every minute or hour and would fill the list. What a job was for (a file's name, a channel's, a track's) is looked up in the workspace its data names, with `inOrg`, as the instance side alone may (decision 066). Details shows its data (identifiers only), its attempts, when it moved, its last error and the error reference its failure left, which opens in Debug tools.
- **Running a failure again.** A file's preparation (`media-probe`, `media-proxy`, `media-analysis`, `document-render`) runs again as the file's own Retry does (`retryFile`): the file waits again, new jobs are queued by its retry plan, and the old failure leaves the queue. One whose file has since been prepared or removed is only cleared, and the result says how many. Any other job runs again under its own id and data (pg-boss's `retry`). "Retry every failure" takes up to 500 at a time, oldest first.
- **Pausing outlasts a restart.** pg-boss has no pause of its own, so a paused queue is one its workers stop taking (`offWork`), as maintenance mode stops them all (#154). The paused queues are kept in the instance's settings (`queues.paused`), saved from Queues alone (the Settings page's save leaves them as they are), and every worker reads them again on the settings signal (#188) and every 15 seconds, and before it registers a worker at start. Maintenance mode still holds every queue whatever is paused, and the page says so. Jobs wait in a paused queue; none is lost.
- **Draining** removes every job waiting in a queue, none run (pg-boss's `deleteQueuedJobs`); running and failed jobs stay. A file whose preparation waited there is marked failed at that stage, so its Retry can prepare it later rather than leave it waiting for ever. It is asked first.
- **Operators only**, behind `operatorsOnly` like the rest of the instance side; every action is an instance event (`queue.retried`, `queue.job_retried`, `queue.paused`, `queue.resumed`, `queue.drained`, `queues.retried`, `queues.paused`, `queues.resumed`).
- **The prototype's** "A failed job retries three times" became "is tried again with backoff": each kind of job has its own retry limit.

**Expanding only:** a key added to the instance settings' JSON. An older release reads the settings without it, and its own save of Settings would drop it (the queues would be resumed); that is the one cost of running both at once.

## 251. The instance's Operations: backups said truthfully, capacity shown from the environment, and rate limits from one place

The prototype's Instance, Operations promised a nightly backup with its time and "verified", "Back up now", how many are kept, and selects for worker lanes, the upload limit and the rate limits.

- **Backups are taken outside Hekate** (docs/backup.md, decision 183): the scripts in `deploy/` and the operator's own tools copy the database and the storage, and nothing tells Hekate they ran. `backup verify` runs against a restored copy, never the live database, so it records nothing the instance could show either. So the section says what Hekate can say truthfully: that backups are taken outside it and why it cannot say when, how to take them and restore (the docs' Backup and restore and Disaster recovery pages), and the command that verifies a restore. The prototype changed with it.
- **Capacity is set in the environment** and needs a restart, so it is shown, never edited: the worker lanes busy of all there are across replicas now (from each replica's last minute, as System health reads them), this process's role, `WORKER_LANES` and `WORKER_CONCURRENCY`; and the largest file one upload carries, which is fixed by the code (10,000 chunks of 8 MiB, what S3 takes for one object), beside the workspaces' quotas. The prototype's selects went.
- **Rate limits** shown are the very rules enforced: sign-in, guest links and codes, content keys, downloads, uploads started and API calls are defined once in `packages/shared` (`rateLimits`) and used where each is enforced, so the page cannot drift from the code. They are not settings; the prototype's Edit went.
- **Operators only**; the page reads again every 15 seconds.

## 252. An error report marked known

Debug tools' "Mark as known" on an error report: an operator has seen it and understood it, so it needs no more looking into. The report keeps when and by whom (`error_reports.known_at`, `known_by`), the list of today's errors shows it as known with a quieter dot, and the report says who marked it. It can be marked not known again. Each change is an instance event (`debug.error_known`, `debug.error_unknown`). Known reports are still listed and still purged after 30 days like any other: known is a note, not a filter.

**Expanding only:** two nullable columns.

## 253. Inspecting a workflow run

Debug tools' "A workflow run" inspector, beside the file, review and session ones (#159): which node a track is at, what its run waits for, and every transition. It takes a run's id, or a track's id or a link to its page, which opens the track's latest run. It shows the run's workflow and version, its state and the status it gave the track, the nodes it waits at and those waiting for their other branches, every step in order with who ended it and what followed, the clocks (`workflow-wait` jobs) the queue holds for it, and the track's other runs. Like the other inspectors it finds the workspace by the row and reads everything else with `inOrg`, operators only, and each look is an instance event (`debug.inspected`). Debug tools' "Inspect the file" on an error report opens the file inspector with the file its job or request was about.

## 254. The support bundle: the machine described, never its content or its people

Debug tools' "Support bundle" downloads one JSON file for an issue or for whoever helps an operator. It holds the release, build, channel and the releases run; what is switched on in the environment (as yes or no: never an address, collector or secret); the instance's settings, with whether a status page and someone on call are set rather than who; capacity and the rate limits; the last hour's health (each storage backend without its path or bucket); the queues' counts; the instance flags; how many workspaces, accounts, projects and files there are; and the last day's error references with where each happened, its status, the error's class and its stack frames. It leaves out every failure's message and the first line of its stack, which can carry a file's name or an address, every name, address, workspace and file name, and every id that would point at one. Operators only, never cached, and each download is an instance event (`debug.support_bundle`). A test reads the bundle for the names, addresses and paths of the instance it was made from.

## 255. Clearing a workspace's rendition cache: renditions made again from the originals, the old ones playing until then

A workspace's Storage tab, Housekeeping, "Rendition cache, Clear". Renditions are not a cache filled on demand: each recording's are made once, when it is measured. So clearing makes them again: every audio and video file of the workspace that has renditions is queued (`media-reproxy`, in the transcode lane, one at a time for each file), which packages new renditions from the original exactly as the first preparation did, with the ladder as the instance now sets it, and puts them in the old ones' place in one transaction; the old segments, and a filmstrip the new one replaces, are removed after. The file plays its old renditions until then, and if making them fails for good, it keeps them and is not marked failed. The original is only read. It is asked first, at most twice an hour for a workspace, operators only, and an instance event (`storage.renditions_rebuilt`). A player that held the old renditions' token needs the page opened again.

## 256. A project's default board arrangement: set by whoever may manage the project, and a person's own choice still wins

The prototype's board Customise panel has "Set as the project's default" beside Reset. How someone arranges a board (its grouping, what the cards carry, the fields, the columns put away) was each person's own and nothing else (#141).

- **Stored on the project, whole.** `projects.board_display` (jsonb, null for none) holds one arrangement in the same shape as a person's (`boardDisplaySchema`). `PUT /api/projects/:id/board/display` sets it, for those with `edit_project` (whoever may manage the project: owners, admins, producers), refused on an archived project as every other edit is; the board's own read returns it as `display`. There is no way to clear it: setting the built-in arrangement does the same.
- **Whose arrangement is shown.** A person's own once they have chosen one; else the project's default; else the built-in arrangement (`boardDisplayFor` in `packages/shared`). A person's arrangement stays one for every project, as before, so someone who has arranged their board sees their own on every board, as Linear keeps a person's view options over a team's default.
- **Knowing that someone chose.** A new preference, `boardChosen`, says whether `board` is theirs; changing anything in the panel sets it, and Reset clears it, so Reset now means "back to the project's default" (the built-in arrangement where the project has none). Preferences kept before it existed hold no such flag: an arrangement other than the built-in one counts as chosen, and the built-in one as not, so nobody loses an arrangement they made and nobody who never touched the panel is kept from the project's default. A stored arrangement that equals the built-in one is not read as unchosen once the flag is there, so choosing the built-in arrangement on a project with another default holds.
- **Shown as the prototype has it.** "Set as the project's default" sits opposite Reset for those who may manage the project; while what they see is already the project's default it reads "The project's default" and does nothing.

**Expanding only:** one nullable column added to `projects`, and one preference added, which an older release ignores (its own reading of preferences keeps only what it knows; a change it saves drops the flag, and the rule for preferences without one applies) (decision 198).

## 257. A project's downloads and deletion: settings that only narrow, applied where the permissions are read

The prototype's Asset security has *Downloads and deletion* and a per-project *Forensic marking*; the product had only the visible watermark. Now a project chooses who may download, whether anyone may have an original, who may delete, whether a file in a review may be deleted, and whether its recordings' downloads carry the workspace's inaudible mark (decision 239). The audio and video stream switches wait for stream marking (#451 to #453).

- **Only narrowing.** Each choice takes away from what the roles (and a guest's review) give, and never adds to it: *Who may download* is the roles that allow it, or nobody; *Originals*, the roles that allow them, only the workspace's owners and admins, or never (a download then gives the proxy); *Who may delete*, the roles that allow it, or only the workspace's owners and admins. The prototype's "Any member" (a second way to give downloads, beside the role matrix) and its role-named choices ("Producers", "Roles with delete") were replaced with these; the prototype now shows them.
- **One place.** The project's policy is applied where a member's permissions are read (`projectAccess`, through the pure `narrowedByPolicy`) and where a guest's reviews are read (`guestReach`, through `cappedDownload`), so every check that already asks for `download_proxy`, `download_original` or `delete_files` (downloads, score downloads, caption exports, deleting files and folders) follows it, and the project's `permissions` say so to the web app. Nobody means nobody: owners and admins too, until someone who edits the project changes it.
- **A reviewed file.** *Files in a review cannot be deleted*, on by default, is decision 027 as it was: an item of any review, open or closed, stays. The prototype said "an open review"; the server has always kept closed reviews' files too, so that what was approved stays on the record, and the switch keeps that rather than quietly letting a closed review lose its items. Off, a file may be deleted wherever it was reviewed; its review items go with it, and the access log's `file.delete` names the reviews it left. A file in a track's version still cannot be deleted.
- **Marking stays the owners'.** Marking is off until a workspace's owners turn it on (decision 239), so only an owner may stop it for a project; anyone who edits the project may turn it back on, and a project started from one that stopped it is marked unless an owner starts it. The switch shows as off, and cannot change, while the workspace does not mark. Downloads are still made for their recipient, marked visibly where they are (decision 080) and logged, whatever is chosen.
- **Logged.** Who edits the project changes the policy; each change is in the access log as `project.asset_security`, with what changed. A template carries the policy.

**Expanding only:** a nullable column, `projects.asset_policy` (null reads as the defaults above), and one value added to the access log's action type (decision 198).

## 258. Exceptions to the player tools: one person, guest or group, in place of their role's

The prototype's *Add an exception* under Player tools. An exception gives one member, guest or group of a project its own set of player tools there, in place of what their role has. It is a row of `project_tool_exceptions` (exactly one of a member, a guest or one of the project's groups), made, changed and removed by whoever manages the project's roles, and read by whoever reads them; a token cannot reach them, as it cannot reach roles.

- **Who it is for.** A member must hold a role in the project; a guest must have been asked by one of its reviews or groups; a group must be the project's. Anyone else is not found.
- **Which wins.** A person's own exception; else every tool any of their groups' exceptions gives; else their role's. Owners and admins keep every tool, as the matrix says.
- **Guests only lose tools.** A guest's tools come from their review's grant, which is what lets them reach a track's score at all (decision 057), so an exception for a guest, or a group with guests in it, only takes away what the review gives: it is applied in `guestReach`, before the score is reached, so a guest without the score no longer reaches the score file either.
- **Enforced where tools are.** Members' exceptions are applied in `projectAccess`, guests' in `guestReach`, so every server check of a tool (QA, meters, the spectrogram, the score, compare) and the project's `tools` follow them.
- **Shown in the matrix.** Each exception is a column of the Player tools matrix after the roles, named with whom it is for and toggled as a role is, with a menu to remove it. The prototype showed exceptions as a line of text under the matrix; it now shows them as columns too. On a phone, one column is shown at a time, chosen above the matrix.

**Expanding only:** a new table, `project_tool_exceptions`, moved with its project between workspaces like the project's reviewer defaults (decision 198).

## 259. Who a project's workflow tells: participants in the app and by mail, and everyone on a status change

The prototype's three switches under Workflow, Notifications, now change who is told.

- **Participants.** The people a stage names: a task's owner, the roles a notice stage names, and those told of a track's new status. *In the app* and *By mail* are the channels the project allows; within them, each person's own choice for workflow notices still applies (`withinChannels`). A kind that always reaches people in the app does not override the project here: workflow notices are not one.
- **Everyone, on a status change.** Off by default, as before: a new status goes to the track's followers (#110). On, everyone who may see the project hears of it too, once each, never whoever moved it.
- **Not the review.** An approval or feedback stage asks for its review as any review is asked: its reviewers are told by the review, and a guest has to be sent their link, whatever the switches say. A stage that could not do its work (no folder to move to, nobody to ask) still tells the run's starter, since that is the workflow failing, not a notice.
- **Who changes it.** Those who edit the project's workflows, from its settings. A template carries it.

**Expanding only:** a nullable column, `projects.workflow_notices` (null reads as in the app, by mail, and followers only) (decision 198).

## 260. A guest link that no longer works gives one answer

The prototype showed three pages for a guest link that does not open: expired, already used, and the review closed to the guest. The server has always given one answer (410, `link_gone`) for a link that is unknown, used, revoked or expired, and the web app one page, "This link no longer works", because which of them it was tells whoever holds a forwarded, intercepted or guessed link something: that the token was real, that someone has already opened it, or roughly when it was sent. A guest who should be in asks whoever invited them for a new link whatever the reason, so the three answers helped nobody who is meant to be there. The prototype now shows the one page in all three of its states.

A review closed to a guest (outside their access window, cancelled, a view or play limit reached) is not a property of the link: the link still signs them in, and their reviews say what is closed and why. The inventory's Guest link row says so.

## 261. A track's workflow history: every move, by name, with who made it

The inventory's Track workflow row asks for the history of transitions; the server kept it (each run's steps, decision 253's inspection reads them) but the web app never showed it, and the prototype did not draw it. The track's versions page now lists it under the versions, as the prototype now shows too.

- **What a line says.** Each step a run ended, by the step's name and its outcome as the workflow builder words it ("Conductor · submitted"), the status it set, who ended it and when; the run's start and a stop (a new version starting it again elsewhere) are lines of their own. Runs are newest first, each with how it stands ("Under way, at Engineer edits"); within one, the moves are in order.
- **Who.** The person whose action moved it on: who uploaded the version that started it, who decided, who marked the task done, who ended a step by hand. A review stage settled with no member named (a guest decided, or the member has since gone) was "A reviewer". A move nobody made (a wait elapsing, a due date passing, a check running) is Hekate's, and says so rather than leaving the column empty.
- **Names from the run.** A step is named as the run's own copy of its workflow had it, so renaming or removing a step later does not rewrite what happened; a step since removed from that copy shows its id. People are named from their accounts, read only for the ids the runs hold.
- **Who sees it.** Whoever may see the project's workflow (`view_workflows`), as the runs route already required; others still see where the track stands. The route's answer grew (each step's `name`, `type` and `byName`, the run's `startedBy`), which older clients ignore.

## 262. The shell's foot: the connection to the server and its version, from the health check

The prototype's Shell row puts the connection and the version at the foot of the sidebar, with "API unreachable" as a state; the prototype did not draw it, and now does (its own "API unreachable" state in the switcher).

- **From `/api/health`.** The foot reads the same check the image's HEALTHCHECK does, every half minute and every ten seconds while it fails: "Connected" with the version, "Database unreachable" when the server answers without its database, and "API unreachable" when nothing answers or something else does (a proxy's error page). The version is the last one known, so it stays while the server is away.
- **The version, not the build.** The health check is public and already gives the release; the build (HEKATE_BUILD) stays in System information, for operators, rather than telling everyone which commit an instance runs.
- **Phones.** There is no sidebar, so a lost connection shows as a line above the page; a working one shows nothing.

## 263. Narrowing by field: advanced search's file fields, and the access log's filters

The inventory's Search row asks for advanced search by type, duration, size, frame rate, tags, uploader and comment text, and its Access log row for filters by project, file, review, person, action and time. Search had type, tags and uploader (and saved searches); the server's log took every filter but the web app offered only the action.

- **Search: fields are filters.** A file's duration and size are ranges (at least, at most; minutes and megabytes in the page, seconds and bytes in the address and the API), its frame rate one of the rates people say (23.976 to 60), matched within a hundredth so 23.976 finds 24000/1001; comment text finds the comments that say it and the files they are on, through the comments' own search index. Each is a filter of `GET /api/search` beside the others, validated by the shared schema, and kept with a saved search, as the others are. A field of a file leaves only files; comment text leaves files and comments.
- **Indexed as read.** Size, duration and frame rate each have an index on the workspace and the value (duration and frame rate as expressions over the media info, defined once in `packages/db` and used by the query), so a range reads an index rather than every file. Expanding only: four new indexes, no column (decision 198).
- **Search: one Fields control.** The page keeps its menus for kind, project, person, time and tag; the fields sit behind a Fields control beside them, and each one set shows as a removable chip, as the files page shows its filters, so nobody wonders why things are missing. The prototype now shows this too.
- **The log: one Filter control and chips.** An action, a project, a person and a time are chosen from the Filter control (the person a member or a guest, so the log takes a guest's id as well as a member's, with its own index); a file and a review are too many to list, so an entry's menu offers *Only this file*, *Only this review*, *Only this person* and the like, as Stripe's and Linear's logs do. The filters in force are chips, each removable, and the CSV export takes exactly what the page shows. A time is a reach back (the past day, week, month or year), worked out when the log is read.
- **Who.** The log stays the owners' and admins' (requireRole), each filter is one more condition on a query already scoped to the workspace, and paging is unchanged.

## 264. Correcting a recording's embedded metadata: Hekate's own record, written into the file only as it leaves

The prototype's *Edit* on a recording's Embedded metadata (QA). It opens an editor in place of the list, for whoever has turned on *Metadata editor in QA* (Appearance, decision 249) and may correct it; Save sends only what changed, Escape or Cancel leaves, and a corrected field says what the file itself says, with *Restore* to go back to it. The list marks a corrected row *Corrected* and says that downloads carry the corrections.

- **Who.** Members whose role organises the project's files (`organise`, as a file's name, place and tags are changed) and has the QA tool; guests never. The prototype offered *Edit* to whoever chooses the delivery profile (`edit_workflows`), which leaves out the engineers and editors who correct metadata in practice; the prototype now uses the same rule. Reading the corrections needs the QA tool.
- **The original is never rewritten.** Stored objects are ciphertext under their own keys (docs/security.md), and nothing in the browser or on the server changes an original in place. A correction is a field of Hekate's own record (`files.metadata_corrections`): each corrected field's value (empty where it was cleared) and what the file itself says. `mediaInfo.metadata` carries the corrected values, so everything that reads a file's metadata (the QA view and its report, a project's rules on ISRC, title, artist and originator, a recording's start from its time reference) reads them; a file probed again has them laid back over what it says. A value typed back to what the file says is no longer a correction.
- **Written in as it leaves.** A download of the original, marked or not, carries the corrections; the proxy carries none, as it carries no tags at all. A wave file (RIFF, RF64, BW64) is written by Hekate, chunk by chunk, from a decrypted copy in the private workspace: its bext chunk with the fields in their slots (version, UMID, loudness and coding history kept), its iXML with each element's first value replaced or added, and its RIFF INFO tags (title, artist, album, genre, date, copyright, ISRC) replaced, removed or added; a chunk it lacks is added before the audio, every other chunk and the audio are copied as they are, and the RIFF or ds64 size is written again. A marked WAV original is its own file with the marked samples in place, as before, and its chunks corrected. FLAC and MP3 have their tags written by ffmpeg, the sound and any artwork copied as they are; a marked recording that leaves as FLAC has them written as it is encoded. The download dialog says the original comes with its corrected metadata. A workspace's export carries the records, the corrections among them, and the originals as stored.
- **Where it is offered.** Broadcast Wave and plain WAV (bext, iXML and tags, the time reference typed as timecode where the sample rate is known), FLAC and MP3 (tags, with album artist and composer, which RIFF INFO cannot hold). Not AIFF, MP4 or Ogg, whose tags ffmpeg does not write faithfully without decoding, nor a video, whose downloads are encoded again and leave its metadata out (decision 081): the prototype's *Edit* on a video's metadata is gone. A file whose metadata was never read cannot be corrected until it is probed again.
- **Checked.** bext's fields are as long as the chunk gives them and written in Latin-1; dates and times as bext writes them; an ISRC as one; one line of text each. A field the file's format has no place for is refused.
- **Logged.** Every edit is in the access log (`file.metadata_edit`) with each field changed, what it said and now says; an edit that changes nothing is not.

Routes: `GET /api/files/:id/metadata` (the format, the fields that may be corrected, the corrections, and whether this viewer may make them) and `PUT /api/files/:id/metadata` (`{ changes: { field: value | null } }`, null going back to what the file says).

**Expanding only:** a nullable column, `files.metadata_corrections`, and one value added to the access log's action type (decision 198). An older release ignores the column: it shows the corrected values, which it finds in the file's media information, but downloads the original as stored, and a file probed again there reads as the file says until it is probed again here.

## 265. CI runs what a pull request touches; one status gathers it

Every pull request waited for every job, about half an hour, whatever it changed. Now `scripts/ci-changes.sh` reads the diff against the base and each job in `ci.yml` runs only when files it covers changed: lint and typecheck for any code, the server suites (with their directory, identity provider and S3 server) for the server and the packages, the web and prototype suites for the web, the visual suite for anything the browser could see, the image for what goes into it, the chart for the chart, the docs site for the docs. A change to the workflow itself, the lockfile or a root manifest runs everything, as do a push to `master`, a nightly run and a run by hand, so nothing the filters miss stays missed for long.

The one required status is `ci`, a last job that fails if any job that ran failed and passes over the ones skipped, since a skipped required check would block merging. The visual suite is split four ways across runners, each shard seeding its own instance and the first running the restore drill; their captures are merged into the one `screens` artefact. The old `check` job is split in three (`check`, `server`, `web`) so a web change never waits for LDAP and Keycloak to start.
## 266. One seed for the visual suite and the demo, driven through the API

`deploy/compose.laptop.yml` brought up an empty Hekate: trying anything meant making projects, tracks, reviews and settings by hand, while the visual suite already seeded a rich instance, as a Playwright test. The seed is now one module the server ships (`apps/server/src/demo/seed.ts`), and `deploy/compose.demo.yml`, in place of the laptop file, brings up an instance that seeds itself: one command, no `.env`.

- **Through the HTTP API.** The seed drives a small session abstraction (a signed-in person's `fetch`, and a way to open another person's) rather than Playwright: the suite gives it Playwright's request contexts, the demo a cookie jar over Node's fetch. Setup goes through `POST /api/setup` with the setup code, files through the chunked upload routes as the browser sends them, and the sample media is made with ffmpeg as before. The few rows no route writes (failures, usage, a year of invoices, the fixed clock) take a database connection when one is given. So the demo needs no browser, and whatever the suite seeds, the demo shows.
- **Hooks for what only a browser does, or only the suite wants.** The suite's captures of the setup form, the empty home and a suspended workspace's owner, keeping the guest's sign-in for the guest screens, and reading the guest's link out of the server's log are hooks. The demo has no log file to read, so it leaves the guest's visit out and carries on; what it seeds says so (`usedGuestPath` empty).
- **A one-shot `seed` service**, the same image on the app's own network, runs `cli.ts demo seed` once the app answers its health check. The command does nothing once the instance is set up, so a second `up` is harmless, and it prints the sign-ins when it is done.
- **The demo guard.** The demo's keys and passwords are published (`deploy/demo.env`), so `HEKATE_DEMO=1` makes the server refuse to start, and the command refuse to run, with any `PUBLIC_URL` but `localhost` or `127.0.0.1`: those settings can never serve a real instance, however they are copied. The demo also keeps the breach check off (its passwords are known) and billing, support and the operator's password session on, as the suite does, so every screen has something to show.

## 267. Everything that floats is drawn in one layer at the app's root

Menus, dropdowns and popovers were absolutely positioned children of whatever declared them, so the collapsed sidebar's menus were clipped by the sidebar, a dropdown at the foot of a scrolling list was cut off by the list, and a panel opened from a dialog stacked under it or over it by accident. They now render through one `Floating` component (`apps/web/src/components/ui/floating.tsx`) into a `FloatingLayer` at the app's root, above the pane and its dialogs.

- **Positioned from the anchor, kept in the window.** `Floating` takes the anchor's ref and a side and alignment (start, end or stretched to the anchor's width). It measures the anchor and the layer, flips above when there is no room below (and the other way round), clamps to the window's edges with a margin, and scrolls inside itself when neither side fits. It follows the anchor through scrolling and resizing (a `ResizeObserver` on the anchor, itself and the layer, plus capture-phase scroll).
- **Focus and outside clicks still belong to the anchor.** A WeakMap from each floating root to its anchor lets `withinOrFloatingFrom` treat a click inside a floated menu as a click on the control that opened it, so `usePopover`'s outside-click rule is unchanged. Tab from the anchor moves into the floated box; focus leaving it for anywhere but the anchor closes it.
- **One layer, one z-index.** The layer is the app's root container, so nothing a page declares can stack over a menu. The visual suite treats each floated root as a layer (`data-e2e-layer`), the same as a dialog: what it covers is out of sight.
- **Without a layer, in place.** A page outside the shell (sign-in, a guest's link) has no layer and the component falls back to an absolutely positioned child, as before.
- **The prototype carries the same component**, so its menus behave as the product's do and the design contract stays whole.

## 268. Dates in ISO 8601 by default, with a format chosen per instance and per workspace

A label's people are in many countries, and a date such as 10/11 reads two ways; 2026-10-29 reads one way to all of them (#481). The language a member or a workspace chooses (Account, and the General settings' "Language and dates" row, now "Language") decided a date's shape until now, so the same review's due date read differently to two members of one workspace. It no longer does.

- **One format per workspace.** Dates read in ISO 8601 (`2026-10-29`, `2026-10-29 14:05`) and the 24-hour clock unless chosen otherwise. An operator chooses for every workspace (Instance, Settings, *Dates and times*): ISO 8601, day-month-year (`29 Oct 2026`) or month-day-year (`Oct 29, 2026`), with a 24-hour or 12-hour clock (`14:05`, `2:05 pm`). A workspace may choose its own in its General settings; its first choice is the instance's default, named, so leaving it alone follows the instance as the instance changes. A member's own choice may follow later; the formatter takes it without change.
- **Where it applies.** Everywhere a date is written: on screen (lists, details, comments, reviews, the board, tasks, the schedule and sessions, the access log, notifications, billing, the instance pages), in a guest's pages, in mail (review and share invitations, reminders, dunning and invoices), and in the documents a workspace hands out (the comment sheet, the QA report, the schedule PDF). Relative words ("2 weeks ago", "yesterday") and a month or a weekday named alone stay in the member's language. CSV exports keep ISO 8601 timestamps whatever the workspace chose: they are read by machines.
- **One formatter.** `formatDate`, `formatTime` and `formatDateTime` in `packages/shared` are pure, take a time zone, and are the only place a date's shape is decided; the web app's `formatDates` helper routes every request for a day and a month, or an hour and a minute, through them, so no screen formats a date itself. The server reads a workspace's effective format (`workspaceDateFormats`) where it writes mail or a document.
- **Stored as settings.** Both choices live in the existing settings documents (the instance's `dates`, the workspace's `dateFormat` and `timeFormat`, null meaning the instance's), read with their defaults where absent, so no migration is needed and an older release reads past them (decision 198).

## 269. One personal mark: a favourite, which also sits at the top of its folder

The files page had two personal marks that nobody else sees: a pin, which kept a thing at the top of its folder's listing, and a favourite, which gathered it into the Favourites filter across the project. Both sat in every row's menu and in the selection bar, each put a small icon beside the name, and nothing said how they differed, so people had to try both to find out (#491).

- **One mark.** The two answered the same wish, to have a thing at hand, and every product people know here has one mark for it (Linear's and Notion's favourites, Dropbox's and Google Drive's stars). A favourite now does both: it sorts first in its folder (favourites first, otherwise in the usual order) and it is in the Favourites filter. "Pin to the top" is gone from the menu and the selection bar, along with the pin beside a name; the star stays.
- **Expand, then contract (decision 198).** The marks table keeps `pinned_at` and the API its `pinned` for now. The web app writes both marks together, so an older release still sorts a favourite first, and reads either as the mark, so a pin made before this release counts as a favourite until it is written again. The server is unchanged. A later release removes `pinned` from the API and drops the column.
- **Nothing to explain.** The alternative, keeping both with a line under each menu item saying what it does, was the smaller diff and the worse page: a menu item that needs a sentence under it is unclear, and the same fact shown twice is a fault the review guidelines name.

## 270. The file browser works as a file manager does: one list of actions, reached four ways

Rows had a "…" menu, and moving meant a form under the row. People expect what the Finder, Explorer and Linear give them, so the browser now takes a right click (or a long press, the Menu key, Shift+F10) on a row, a selection or the empty space, renames in the row, and moves by dragging, by a folder picker, or by cut and paste (#487).

- **One list of actions.** A row's "…" menu and its context menu are the same list, built once (`actionsFor`); a selection's bar and the context menu on a selected row share another (`selectionActions`). Two lists would drift. The context menu floats through the floating layer like every other menu (decision 267): `Floating` takes a point as well as an element to hang from, and `MenuList` and the new `ContextMenu` pass it on. Opened from the keyboard it hangs from the row and focuses its first item, and focus goes back to the row when it shuts; the arrows move through every menu now.
- **Renaming in the row, no dialog.** F2, Rename in a menu, or a click on the name of a row that is already selected (after the double-click interval, as the Finder has it) turns the name into a field with the name selected, a file's without its extension. Enter keeps it, Escape does not, and leaving the field keeps a change: a half-typed rename is almost always meant. The same field renames in the file's details sheet. The old rename and move forms are gone.
- **A drop on a track attaches.** A track is a folder of a piece's versions, so loose files dropped on a track row go in as its next version, one variant each (the first primary), named as "Attach to a track" would name them; a folder or another track dropped on a track is refused. A drop on a folder row or a breadcrumb moves, as before.
- **Cut and paste is in-memory.** Cmd or Ctrl and X marks rows (they fade); Cmd or Ctrl and V, or Paste in the context menu, moves them into the open folder, or into the folder the menu was opened on. It is page state, not the system clipboard: moving files between windows is not what it is for, and the system clipboard cannot carry a row without a scheme of its own.
- **Tags are kept as they change.** Tags are edited in the row's tag cell (or, where the list shows no Tags column, over the name), in the details sheet and in the selection bar through one `TagInput`: pills a click takes off, a field offering the workspace's tags as one types (most used first) or making a new one. Each change is kept at once, so there is no Save and no form to abandon. Several files share one editor in the bar; a tag on some of them is a dashed pill that a click puts on all. The details route now returns a file's `tagIds` for the sheet.

## 271. Sharing one item is a share sheet: who, how, a link, Send

The share dialog (#138) asked what to share even when opened from the thing itself, and carried the whole review-request form. Sharing from a file, a track, a folder or a version (#489) now opens a small sheet for that item (`apps/web/src/components/share.tsx`), as Dropbox Replay's share sheet and Frame.io's share dialog do it: the item's name and kind are the title, and the body is three fields and a button.

- **Who** is one field for members, groups and guests, offered as you type and floated under it as a menu is (`PeoplePicker`; decision 267). An email address no one here answers to is offered as a new guest, named from its local part until the project's People page renames them.
- **How** is a segmented choice: *Can view*, *Can comment*, *Can download*. Viewing is a share, which has no comments by design (docs/domain.md, Reviews); commenting, and downloading with it, are a feedback review, which asks for comments and no sign-off. The sheet never adds a kind to the server: it sends `createReviewInput` as the request page does, with `comment`, `download` and `expiresAt` as the review's permissions.
- **The link** is the item's own address in the app, copied from the sheet; it opens for members in their session and nowhere else, as every address does (docs/security.md). Beside it, how long the share works, in words ("Link works for 7 days"; a week unless chosen otherwise, or until withdrawn), which is the review's expiry. Once sent, the row shows the share's own address instead, so what was just sent can be passed on. Guests still reach it only through the single-use link mailed to them; the sheet says so.
- **More options** carries the choices into the full request page through its search params (the items, a version, the people, what they may download and until when), for stages, an order or a deadline. What the sheet chose for everyone becomes each person's override there, since that page sets permissions one person at a time.
- **One sheet, three doors.** The file browser's row menu (folders included: a folder shares everything in it, at every depth), the player's header and a track's versions page (the track, or one version from its menu) open the same component. The reviews page no longer opens it: sharing starts from the thing shared, and its empty state says so. The prototype's sheet is the same.

## 272. Audio uploaded into a music project is asked what it is

Dropping a recording into a music project's files stored it as a plain file; making it a track, or a new version of one, meant finding "Attach to a track" in the file's menu afterwards, which nobody did (#485). The question is now asked where the file arrives: the upload dialog (the one dialog for every file chosen in a dialog, #477) carries three choices above the files chosen.

- **Three choices, in the dialog.** *New track*: the file becomes version 1 of a track named from the file (extension off, separators tidied). *New version of…*: the files become the variants of the track's next version, each with its variant name and one primary, as the track's own new-version dialog has it. *Just a file*: uploaded as it is. Several files under New track are a track each, or one track with a version per file in name order; the line before the button says which ("3 files become 3 tracks", "3 files become versions 1 to 3 of one track"), and the button is named for the job ("Make 3 tracks", "Upload version 4", "Upload 3 files").
- **Where it is asked.** Only in a music project, only for audio, and only where there is something to make: New track for those who may organise, New version where the project has a track. A drop on a track's row, or on a track's own page, opens on a new version of that track. Non-audio files, and projects whose profile is not music, go up as they did, with no question; non-audio dropped alongside audio goes up as it is.
- **Remembered per project.** The last choice made is kept in the browser, by project, so a producer who always makes tracks is not asked twice.
- **No new route.** The sequence is the routes that exist: upload the files, make the track, add the version; refusals by the project's checks (an edit decision list wanted, a true peak over the ceiling) are shown as the track's dialog shows them, and can be let through by those who may edit workflows. Which files become which tracks and versions is a pure function (`apps/web/src/lib/upload-plan.ts`), tested.
- **"Attach to a track" stays** in the menu for files already uploaded. The prototype's project files page carries the same dialog with the same three choices.

## 273. A variant is added from the player's variant switch, which then follows it

Adding a variant meant leaving the player for the track's versions page and finding the entry in a version's row menu (#486). The variant switch above the player is where people look for the version's variants, so it is where another is added.

- **The switch carries the action.** The segmented control (`Segmented` in the web app, `Seg` in the prototype) takes an optional `action`, a small button at its end after a hairline, named for a screen reader and on hover. Above the player the action is "Add a variant…" for whoever may upload to the project, the same permission the versions page checks and the server enforces. To those people the switch shows even where the version has one variant, so the action has a home; to everyone else it shows, as before, only where there is something to switch between.
- **One dialog, from both places.** `VariantDialog` moved from the versions page into `apps/web/src/components/variant-dialog.tsx` and is opened from the switch and from the version's row menu alike; the player does not pull the versions page's code in. It is the one upload dialog (#477) with the version fixed, and a new variant's name is read from its file or taken from one of the names the project's variants already go by (Dry, Wet, Instrumental): `variantNames` in `apps/web/src/lib/versions.ts` derives them from the project's tracks, each name once, the most recently used first, without those the version already has.
- **The player follows the new variant once it plays.** The switch shows it at once, marked analysing, from the track the server answers with; the file's own query, which already asks again every few seconds while a file is being prepared, says when it is ready, and the player then opens it at the same moment as the one playing. A file that fails stays where it is, marked failed in the switch, and the recording playing is not interrupted either way.

## 274. Takes are added from their track and live under it, not among the project's files

Adding takes for a track meant opening the player, its versions, then its Takes tab, and choosing files there with a bare file input; the files then appeared in the file browser beside the project's files, as if they were loose files, and were counted with the folder they landed in (#490).

- **Added from the track.** The track's Takes view has *Add takes* in its header (and its empty state), and the track's row in the files has *Add takes…* in its menu. Both open the take import (#45) through the one upload dialog (#477) with the track settled: several files, take numbers read from their iXML or names, a take's microphones grouped with its mix, and two choices made once for the batch, the session (as the files' day says, none, or one of the project's) and the bars the takes cover. A file that gives no take number and no timecode is a take of its own here, named after its file, rather than left out as a session import leaves it; nothing is chosen by row but a take's mix. The import route takes the bars (`barFrom`, `barTo`, optional, refused when they run backwards); a take logged live keeps the bars the log gave it. Dropping files on the takes opens the same dialog with them.
- **The listing rule, in one place.** A file is a take's when `files.take_id` is set, which everything that makes a take writes (the track's takes route, the import, the take log's matching) and removing a take clears. `apps/server/src/take-files.ts` says it once (`isListedFile`, tested as a pure function, and `listedFile`, the same condition for the queries): the project's files listing, the files a search finds and the project's file count leave a take's file out, so the file browser, its folder counts, the item pickers that read the listing (reviews, a version's variants, a file's next version) and the command palette never show one. A take's file is reached through its take: the track's Takes tab, Other takes in the player, its session's take log and its own page. Comments on it are still searched, a failed one still appears among the files to repair, and its bytes still count in the project's and the workspace's storage, which measure what is stored. Removing a take puts its file back in the folder it was uploaded to (the track's).
- **No migration.** Every take's file already carries `take_id`, so the rule reads existing rows as they are: the takes already listed as files leave the listing on upgrade with no data rewritten, and `pnpm db:generate` finds nothing to change. The release before reads the same rows and lists them as it did (decision 198).
- **The prototype** shows the Takes view for whoever has the tool, with *Add takes* in the header and the row's menu, the dialog in its track mode, and no take named among the files.

## 275. A file differing in treatment within a version is a variant

The dry mix and the wet mix of Version 4 were *variations* of it, in copy, in code and in the API. The word is replaced by *variant* everywhere (#484).

- **Why variant.** It is the word people use: a producer sends "the instrumental variant", never "the instrumental variation". *Variation* reads as a musical form (a theme and its variations), which in a tool whose first users are musicians is a second meaning too close to the first; a variant is plainly another form of the same thing. Video projects keep *rendition*, their own word for it (decision 138).
- **Everywhere at once.** `docs/domain.md` changed first; copy, component, file and function names, route and module names, the shared types and schemas, the API paths and field names, the prototype, the mail templates, the documentation site and the visual suite follow. Nothing in the interface or the reference says "variation" any more.
- **Two releases, expand then contract (decision 198).** The database and the public API cannot change in one step while an older replica or an older script still runs. This release adds the new shape beside the old and switches everything to read and say the new: a `variants` table with the shape of `variations`, backfilled from it (migration 0149_variants), which the server reads while writing both tables through one helper (`apps/server/src/variants.ts`); `/variants` routes with the `/variations` paths answering the same, marked deprecated in the reference; a new version's files taken as `variants` or, for now, `variations`, and answered under both names; `variant.replace` in the audit log, with `variation.replace` read from older rows. The shared package keeps the old names as deprecated aliases so both typecheck meanwhile. The next release removes the old shape: the `variations` table (a contracting migration), the alias routes, the old field in answers and the aliases.

## 276. The player is two groups: playback beneath the picture, the view at its edge

The transport sat far below the waveform with empty space between, playback and view controls shared one bar, volume was on or muted, and the format read as a button (#493, #498).

- **The media is the flexible part.** The waveform or the picture fills the height the frame gives it, with the transport directly beneath; on a tall window the waveform grows, on a short one it gives way, down to its usual height, before anything scrolls. The header and the comments keep their place. The cap that held the waveform at one height is gone.
- **Playback in the transport.** To the start, back ten and five seconds, play, the time, then the speed, the comparison and the volume. There is no forward button; L and the right arrow still go forward. The speaker mutes on a click and opens a slider on hover or focus, 0 to 100 in steps of 5, remembered per browser (`apps/web/src/lib/volume.ts`, one value for every player on the page); the arrows on the speaker, and up and down anywhere on the page, step it; M mutes. The page's gain (normalised playback, the comparison's level matching) multiplies the volume rather than replacing it.
- **The view at the waveform's edge.** A toolbar above the waveform holds what changes how the recording is looked at: the loop (on and off; the loop bar beneath keeps the edges and the rolls), the spectrogram (on and off; its place and colours stay in View), the meters (levels at the playhead beside the waveform, from the stored analysis) and the zoom. The waveform canvas, the scrub bar and the spectrogram themselves are unchanged by this.
- **The time is a field.** A double-click on the time (or Enter on it) turns it into a field: seconds, m:ss, a timecode on a video's own clock, or a bar and beat where the project maps bars to time, read by `parseTimeEntry` in `packages/shared`; Enter seeks, Escape leaves it. No project maps bars to time yet; the context carries the hook for it.
- **The format is information.** The rendition heard ("AAC · stereo") is a quiet line in the strip beside the version and variant, never a button; where there are several renditions it is a quiet menu named Quality. The variant switch above the player is the only place a variant is chosen.
- **The track's status from the player.** A track's file carries the board's status chip in the strip (`TrackStatusChip`): its standing, and for whoever may edit the project's workflows, the moves its running workflow allows from here (each step it waits on that someone ends, by each outcome), confirmed in a dialog and ended through the existing step route, which the track's history records. A note with the move would need a column of its own on run steps and waits for a release of its own.
- **A quieter footer.** The line of loudness and issues under the player is gone; the QA switch in the header carries a count badge when the checks found something, and the footer shows transport information only.
